# Daily Summary: 2025-06-05

### Adversarial Attacks on Robotic Vision Language Action Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03350v1)
- **Authors**: Eliot Krzysztof Jones, Alexander Robey, Andy Zou, Zachary Ravichandran, George J. Pappas, Hamed Hassani, Matt Fredrikson, J. Zico Kolter
- **Abstract**: The emergence of vision-language-action models (VLAs) for end-to-end control is reshaping the field of robotics by enabling the fusion of multimodal sensory inputs at the billion-parameter scale. The capabilities of VLAs stem primarily from their architectures, which are often based on frontier large language models (LLMs). However, LLMs are known to be susceptible to adversarial misuse, and given the significant physical risks inherent to robotics, questions remain regarding the extent to which VLAs inherit these vulnerabilities. Motivated by these concerns, in this work we initiate the study of adversarial attacks on VLA-controlled robots. Our main algorithmic contribution is the adaptation and application of LLM jailbreaking attacks to obtain complete control authority over VLAs. We find that textual attacks, which are applied once at the beginning of a rollout, facilitate full reachability of the action space of commonly used VLAs and often persist over longer horizons. This differs significantly from LLM jailbreaking literature, as attacks in the real world do not have to be semantically linked to notions of harm. We make all code available at https://github.com/eliotjones1/robogcg .
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper initiates the study of adversarial attacks on Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models controlling robots. The core idea is to adapt LLM jailbreaking techniques to gain control over a VLA-controlled robot by manipulating the textual input. The authors demonstrate that carefully crafted textual prompts, added as suffixes to nominal instructions, can reliably elicit specific actions from the robot, effectively achieving control authority over the VLA. They find that these "jailbreaks" are often persistent across multiple rollout steps and can transfer across different environments. They adapted the Greedy Coordinate Gradient (GCG) attack to work in the context of VLA-controlled robots. Their experiments show high success rates in eliciting targeted actions with various fine-tuned OpenVLA models. The paper also explores some defenses, such as perplexity filtering and smoothing, but find them largely ineffective.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper represents a novel contribution by exploring the vulnerability of VLA-controlled robots to adversarial attacks. While LLM jailbreaking is a well-studied area, applying it to robotic control, particularly at the low-level action space, is a fresh perspective. The adaptation of GCG to VLAs is a key algorithmic contribution.
*   **Significance:** The potential implications of adversarial control over robots are significant. The work raises serious concerns about the safety and security of VLA-integrated systems. By demonstrating that textual prompts can subvert intended behavior, the paper highlights the need for robust defenses. It encourages the AI safety community to look beyond traditional safety notions. The paper correctly points out that unlike chatbots, safety in robots is a matter of control authority rather than semantic harm, which makes the attacks different and more challenging to defend.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined threat models specific to VLA-controlled robots.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with high success rates for attacks.
    *   Demonstration of attack persistence and transferability.
    *   Identification of key differences between attacking chatbots and VLAs.
    * Code is available.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on a white-box attack (GCG) limits the immediate practical applicability. GCG requires access to the model's gradients.
    *   Limited exploration of defenses. The tested defenses were known to have limitations, and the exploration of other defenses is not thoroughly developed in the paper.
    *   The experiments are primarily conducted in simulation. While the paper does include real-world experiments, they are limited in scope. More extensive real-world validation is desirable.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of potential mitigation strategies, beyond the initial discussion of alignment.
    * Ensemble transfer experiments aren't promising and the analysis is a bit vague.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a positive impact by drawing attention to a critical vulnerability in robotics. It will stimulate further research into adversarial attacks and defenses for VLA-controlled systems. It will likely change the way researchers view robot safety.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a novel contribution to the field of AI safety by studying adversarial attacks on VLAs. The experimental results are compelling, and the analysis is insightful. However, the limitations of the white-box attack and limited defense exploration prevent it from achieving a higher score. The contribution is solid and of considerable interest for the robotics and AI safety community.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Robustness in Both Domains: CLIP Needs a Robust Text Encoder
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03355v1)
- **Authors**: Elias Abad Rocamora, Christian Schlarmann, Naman Deep Singh, Yongtao Wu, Matthias Hein, Volkan Cevher
- **Abstract**: Adversarial input attacks can cause a significant shift of CLIP embeddings. This can affect the downstream robustness of models incorporating CLIP in the pipeline, such as text-to-image generative models or large vision language models. While some efforts have been done towards making the CLIP image encoders robust, the robustness of text encoders remains unexplored. In this work, we cover this gap in the literature. We propose LEAF: an efficient adversarial finetuning method for the text domain, with the ability to scale to large CLIP models. Our models significantly improve the zero-shot adversarial accuracy in the text domain, while maintaining the vision performance provided by robust image encoders. When combined with text-to-image diffusion models, we can improve the generation quality under adversarial noise. When employing our robust CLIP encoders in multimodal retrieval tasks, we improve the recall under adversarial noise over standard CLIP models. Finally, we show that robust text encoders facilitate better reconstruction of input text from its embedding via direct optimization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the overlooked vulnerability of text encoders in CLIP models to adversarial attacks. While efforts have focused on improving the robustness of image encoders within CLIP, the authors highlight that the text encoder remains a weak point. They propose LEAF (Levenshtein Efficient Adversarial Finetuning), a method for efficiently and effectively adversarially finetuning the text encoder of CLIP models. Their experiments demonstrate that LEAF enhances robustness in text-based tasks, improves the quality of text-to-image generation under adversarial conditions, and boosts retrieval accuracy in multimodal retrieval tasks. Furthermore, the authors show that robust text encoders, trained with LEAF, can better facilitate the reconstruction of input text from its embedding via direct optimization, leading to more interpretable representations. The paper introduces a training-time attack to accelerate adversarial finetuning in the text domain, optimizing for the same objective as existing methods used for the image encoder.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in identifying and addressing the vulnerability of CLIP text encoders to adversarial attacks, a previously under-explored area. The LEAF method itself offers a novel and efficient approach to adversarially finetuning text encoders. The attack acceleration using batch-parallelization is also a practical contribution. The adaptation of the FARE objective function from image encoders to text encoders, while not entirely revolutionary, is well-motivated and contributes to the overall effectiveness of the approach.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they highlight a critical security concern in CLIP models and provide a practical solution. By improving the robustness of text encoders, the authors enhance the overall reliability and trustworthiness of CLIP-based systems used in a range of applications, including content filtering, generative models, and large multimodal models. The fact that the improvements can be achieved without significant performance degradation on clean data strengthens the argument for adopting their proposed methods. The results on embedding inversion are also interesting as they suggest better interpretability of embeddings learned through LEAF.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-designed experiments that evaluate the effectiveness of LEAF in various tasks.
    *   Efficient adversarial finetuning method suitable for large models.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in robustness across multiple modalities (text, image, retrieval, text-to-image).
    *   Showcasing the ability of LEAF to learn more interpretable representations through better embedding reconstruction.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The adaptation of the FARE objective to the text domain is incremental. While the modifications for efficiency (LEAF attack) are significant, the core objective remains largely the same.
    *   The paper mainly focuses on character-level perturbations. While semantics-preserving attacks are difficult in the text domain, investigating robustness against token-level attacks could provide a more comprehensive evaluation.
    *   The ablation experiments could be expanded to provide more insights into the individual components of the LEAF method, such as separate ablations of batch-parallel attack.
    *   The discussion of the text encoder model selection seems to be missing. Are some model architectures more amenable to robustification than others?

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in the area of adversarial robustness for CLIP models and other multimodal systems. It demonstrates the importance of considering the robustness of all components within a system and provides a concrete approach for improving the resilience of text encoders. The practical nature of LEAF makes it readily adoptable in real-world applications.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by addressing an important, and previously overlooked, aspect of CLIP model robustness. The LEAF method and attack are practically relevant and yield demonstrable improvements in performance across several modalities. However, the core objective is an adaptation, and while the proposed accelerations are welcome, this somewhat limits the overall novelty. The scope of the adversarial attacks considered could be broadened. Nevertheless, the identification of the problem, the design of an effective solution, and the extensive empirical evaluation justify a strong score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ask a Local: Detecting Hallucinations With Specialized Model Divergence
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03357v1)
- **Authors**: Aldan Creo, Héctor Cerezo-Costas, Pedro Alonso-Doval, Maximiliano Hormazábal-Lagos
- **Abstract**: Hallucinations in large language models (LLMs) - instances where models generate plausible but factually incorrect information - present a significant challenge for AI. We introduce "Ask a Local", a novel hallucination detection method exploiting the intuition that specialized models exhibit greater surprise when encountering domain-specific inaccuracies. Our approach computes divergence between perplexity distributions of language-specialized models to identify potentially hallucinated spans. Our method is particularly well-suited for a multilingual context, as it naturally scales to multiple languages without the need for adaptation, relying on external data sources, or performing training. Moreover, we select computationally efficient models, providing a scalable solution that can be applied to a wide range of languages and domains. Our results on a human-annotated question-answer dataset spanning 14 languages demonstrate consistent performance across languages, with Intersection-over-Union (IoU) scores around 0.3 and comparable Spearman correlation values. Our model shows particularly strong performance on Italian and Catalan, with IoU scores of 0.42 and 0.38, respectively, while maintaining cross-lingual effectiveness without language-specific adaptations. We release our code and architecture to facilitate further research in multilingual hallucination detection.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Ask a Local: Detecting Hallucinations With Specialized Model Divergence":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method called "Ask a Local" for detecting hallucinations in large language models (LLMs). The core idea is to leverage the divergence in perplexity scores from specialized language models. The intuition is that a statement is more likely to be a hallucination if it significantly surprises a model specializing in the relevant language or domain. The method compares the perplexity distributions of language-specific models to identify potentially hallucinated spans, offering a multilingual approach that requires no adaptation or external data sources. The authors evaluate their method on a human-annotated dataset spanning 14 languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using divergence between perplexities of specialized models is interesting and represents a somewhat novel approach to hallucination detection.  The focus on specialized knowledge and the intuition behind "surprise" from a relevant model is a decent departure from solely relying on single model metrics or external data verification. The implementation also scales naturally to multiple languages, avoiding the need for language-specific training.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work hinges on its practical effectiveness and potential for widespread use. While the paper demonstrates consistent performance across languages, the reported IoU scores (around 0.3) are only marginally better than a naive baseline and significantly below existing approaches cited in the paper (around 0.6). The performance needs significant improvement to be truly impactful.  However, the method's independence from external data and training requirements could be a valuable asset in scenarios where such resources are limited. Furthermore, the focus on computational efficiency makes the method scalable to a wider range of languages and domains. The claim of independence from external datasets is also not entirely true, as the method uses an instruction-following model for span tagging, implying that the results are indirectly dependent on that model's training data.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multilingual Capability:** The method's ability to scale to multiple languages without adaptation is a strong advantage.
    *   **No External Data/Training:** The independence from external datasets and training is valuable where such resources are limited.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** The use of computationally efficient models makes the approach scalable.
    *   **Clear Intuition:** The idea of leveraging specialized knowledge and model divergence is well-motivated.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Performance:** The IoU scores are relatively low and don't convincingly demonstrate a significant improvement over existing approaches.
    *   **Annotation Inconsistency:** Acknowledged annotation inconsistencies in the dataset introduce noise into the evaluation.
    *   **Indirect Dependence:**  Reliance on an instruction-following model for span tagging introduces an indirect dependency on external training data.
    *   **Simplified Heuristic:**  The method of span tagging using another model as reference seems heuristic rather than systematic or theoretically driven.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The work could influence future research in several ways: 1) Exploring alternative ways to measure model divergence, maybe using attention mechanisms. 2) Investigating more sophisticated span-tagging heuristics or techniques. 3)  Adapting the method for use in low-resource language settings where external data and training resources are scarce. 4) Improving the design of annotation schemes for hallucination detection tasks.

**Overall:**

The paper presents an interesting idea with a clear intuition, but the practical results are not yet compelling. The method shows some promise, especially in its ability to work across multiple languages without adaptation, but significant improvements in performance are needed to make it a truly impactful contribution. The novelty is moderate, but the significance is currently limited by the modest performance gains. It is also important to note the potential performance improvement by simply using a larger instruction following model for the final span selection step, as well as the indirect dependency on training data, as it uses an instruction following model.
The authors' openness about the limitations and areas for future work is commendable.

Score: 5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### A Multimodal, Multilingual, and Multidimensional Pipeline for Fine-grained Crowdsourcing Earthquake Damage Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03360v1)
- **Authors**: Zihui Ma, Lingyao Li, Juan Li, Wenyue Hua, Jingxiao Liu, Qingyuan Feng, Yuki Miura
- **Abstract**: Rapid, fine-grained disaster damage assessment is essential for effective emergency response, yet remains challenging due to limited ground sensors and delays in official reporting. Social media provides a rich, real-time source of human-centric observations, but its multimodal and unstructured nature presents challenges for traditional analytical methods. In this study, we propose a structured Multimodal, Multilingual, and Multidimensional (3M) pipeline that leverages multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to assess disaster impacts. We evaluate three foundation models across two major earthquake events using both macro- and micro-level analyses. Results show that MLLMs effectively integrate image-text signals and demonstrate a strong correlation with ground-truth seismic data. However, performance varies with language, epicentral distance, and input modality. This work highlights the potential of MLLMs for disaster assessment and provides a foundation for future research in applying MLLMs to real-time crisis contexts. The code and data are released at: https://github.com/missa7481/EMNLP25_earthquake
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a "Multimodal, Multilingual, and Multidimensional" (3M) pipeline designed to assess earthquake damage using social media data and multimodal large language models (MLLMs). The pipeline integrates data collection, damage classification, and model evaluation. The authors evaluate the pipeline using two earthquake events (Ridgecrest, CA, and Fukushima, Japan) and assess the performance of several MLLMs (including Gemini, LLaVA, and Qwen) across different modalities (text, images, and combined) and languages. The study finds that MLLMs can effectively extract event and location information, integrate image-text signals, and estimate damage levels correlating with seismic data. However, performance varies with language, epicentral distance, and input modality, indicating areas for future improvement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable system-level integration of existing technologies (MLLMs and social media data analysis) for a practical problem (disaster damage assessment). While individual components may not be groundbreaking, the specific combination of multimodal analysis, multilingual capabilities, and a structured pipeline *does* represent a novel and useful contribution. The application to a real-world problem with a focus on explainability (through CoT prompting and analysis of reasoning) enhances the practicality of the research.
*   **Significance:** Disaster response is a crucial area, and leveraging social media offers potential for rapid and comprehensive damage assessment. The work addresses limitations of traditional methods by incorporating human-centric observations and handling unstructured data. The multilingual aspect is particularly important for global applicability. The study's finding that MLLMs can correlate with ground-truth seismic data and provide interpretable reasoning patterns is significant and suggests potential for aiding emergency response efforts.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-structured Pipeline:** The 3M pipeline provides a clear and reproducible framework for researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Multimodal and Multilingual Focus:** Addressing both modalities and languages is essential for real-world disaster scenarios, making the work more applicable and impactful.
    *   **Real-world Case Studies:** Evaluating the pipeline on two distinct earthquake events strengthens the validity and generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Interpretability Analysis:** Investigating the models' reasoning through textual justifications is crucial for building trust and understanding limitations.
    *   **Analysis of Prompt Sensitivity**: Recognizes and attempts to mitigate the impact of prompt engineering on results, a key challenge in LLM use.
    * **Comprehensive experiments**: The paper presents a thorough set of experiments and insightful analysis, exploring macro- and micro-level perspectives of the task and the MLLM reasoning process.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Social Media Biases:** The inherent biases in social media data (demographic, access, etc.) remain a limitation, acknowledged by the authors. Further work is needed to mitigate these biases.
    *   **Limited Fine-Tuning:** Not fine-tuning the MLLMs on task-specific data restricts their full potential and could have improved performance.
    *   **Ground Truth Limitations:** DYFI (Did You Feel It?) data, while widely used, can be subjective and affected by reporting biases. Using additional and more objective ground truth data would strengthen the evaluation.
    * **Evaluation Metric**: While the paper does report correlation with ground truth data, it lacks a clear set of metrics for evaluation. Additionally, inter-annotator reliability assessment can be more rigorous.
    * **Scalability**: The cost-effectiveness of the closed source solutions makes scalability a major concern.

*   **Potential Influence:** The research can influence future work in disaster management, encouraging the development of more sophisticated MLLM-based tools for rapid damage assessment. The pipeline and code release will facilitate replication and further exploration. The study also highlights areas for improvement in MLLMs, such as language adaptation and handling of diverse data sources. The paper contributes to the growing body of work on responsible AI in high-stakes environments.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a novel system-level contribution in the application of MLLMs to a significant real-world problem (disaster damage assessment). It builds a structured pipeline to address this important task, evaluates the solution rigorously, and provides insightful analysis of MLLMs in a multilingual context. There are limitations concerning data biases and the generalizability of the system and further work is needed to increase the robustness of the system but the limitations are clearly recognized in the paper. Also, the paper's strength also lies in it's analysis and insights that future research can build on to address these limitations. For these reasons, a score of 7 seems appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Comparison of different Unique hard attention transformer models by the formal languages they can recognize
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03370v1)
- **Authors**: Leonid Ryvkin
- **Abstract**: This note is a survey of various results on the capabilities of unique hard attention transformers encoders (UHATs) to recognize formal languages. We distinguish between masked vs. non-masked, finite vs. infinite image and general vs. bilinear attention score functions. We recall some relations between these models, as well as a lower bound in terms of first-order logic and an upper bound in terms of circuit complexity.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Comparison of different Unique hard attention transformer models by the formal languages they can recognize," as presented in the OCR output:

**Summary:**

The paper provides a survey of the capabilities of unique hard attention transformer encoders (UHATs) in recognizing formal languages.  It distinguishes between masked vs. non-masked architectures, finite vs. infinite image representations, and general vs. bilinear attention score functions. It summarizes known relationships between these different model variants, including lower bounds in terms of first-order logic and upper bounds in terms of circuit complexity (AC0). The paper also presents some new observations, including a theorem stating that the expressiveness of GUHATs can be increased by adding layers, the notion of separable attention, and a formal proof showing that masked finite image UHATs can simulate their non-masked counterparts.  The paper culminates in a diagram summarizing the inclusion relations between various transformer classes and relevant language classes, and poses some open problems. The paper also makes a distinction between UHATs (which have bilinear attention functions) and GUHATs (which are more generalized)

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a mix of known results and potentially new observations. The compilation of existing results into a single, accessible survey is useful. However, the core novelty lies in the "potentially new observations." The result concerning the increase in expressiveness through adding layers to GUHATs seems genuinely new, though the author admits it has some similarities with depth hierarchy results.  The introduction of "separable attention" as a unifying concept is also a helpful contribution, facilitating a clearer comparison between different architectures. The proof relating masked and non-masked versions within finite image UHATs appears to be a novel, formal argument supporting a previously observed trend.

*   **Significance:**  The significance rests on how these results contribute to our understanding of the fundamental limitations and expressive power of transformers. Mapping the landscape of hard attention transformers onto formal language classes provides a mathematically rigorous method for comparing architectures.  The paper's focus on formal languages offers a way to bypass the empirical nature of much contemporary NLP research. The introduction of separable attention might influence future analyses of attention mechanisms, encouraging researchers to examine attention functions in terms of simpler, factorable components.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Survey:**  The paper provides a well-organized overview of the capabilities of various UHAT models.
    *   **Clear Definitions:** The concepts of UHATs, GUHATs, masking, finite/infinite image, and now separable attention are clearly defined.
    *   **Connection to Formal Languages:**  The paper bridges the gap between transformer research and formal language theory.
    *   **Identified Open Problems:** The paper identifies several areas for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Theoretical Focus:** The paper remains largely theoretical. While it is valuable to understand theoretical capabilities, it provides little guidance on how these insights translate to real-world NLP tasks.
    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The claims of increased expressiveness are primarily theoretical. Empirical demonstrations, while not strictly required, would strengthen the paper's impact.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the claimed new observations are useful, their contribution to the field can be argued as rather incremental. They refine and extend existing knowledge rather than revolutionizing the understanding of transformers.
    *   **OCR Quality:** The OCR quality of the provided document is problematic which makes judging and following the arguments more difficult.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper may primarily appeal to researchers working on the theoretical foundations of deep learning and formal language theory. The clear exposition and defined open problems may inspire further work in this area. The new techniques could be useful in proving properties of related architectures.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

A score of 6 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the theoretical understanding of hard attention transformers, balanced against its lack of empirical grounding and its incremental novelty. The systematic survey, clear definitions, and the identification of potentially new observations merit acknowledgement. However, the paper lacks a strong connection to practical NLP problems and presents a set of new arguments that, while important, are not transformative to the field. The paper is a solid technical contribution that builds upon prior work, adds to the understanding of the capabilities of hard attention transformers, and is well-written, but is not groundbreaking in its scope.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Universal Reusability in Recommender Systems: The Case for Dataset- and Task-Independent Frameworks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03391v1)
- **Authors**: Tri Kurniawan Wijaya, Xinyang Shao, Gonzalo Fiz Pontiveros, Edoardo D'Amico
- **Abstract**: Recommender systems are pivotal in delivering personalized experiences across industries, yet their adoption and scalability remain hindered by the need for extensive dataset- and task-specific configurations. Existing systems often require significant manual intervention, domain expertise, and engineering effort to adapt to new datasets or tasks, creating barriers to entry and limiting reusability. In contrast, recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated the transformative potential of reusable systems, where a single model can handle diverse tasks without significant reconfiguration. Inspired by this paradigm, we propose the Dataset- and Task-Independent Recommender System (DTIRS), a framework aimed at maximizing the reusability of recommender systems while minimizing barriers to entry. Unlike LLMs, which achieve task generalization directly, DTIRS focuses on eliminating the need to rebuild or reconfigure recommendation pipelines for every new dataset or task, even though models may still need retraining on new data. By leveraging the novel Dataset Description Language (DsDL), DTIRS enables standardized dataset descriptions and explicit task definitions, allowing autonomous feature engineering, model selection, and optimization. This paper introduces the concept of DTIRS and establishes a roadmap for transitioning from Level-1 automation (dataset-agnostic but task-specific systems) to Level-2 automation (fully dataset- and task-independent systems). Achieving this paradigm would maximize code reusability and lower barriers to adoption. We discuss key challenges, including the trade-offs between generalization and specialization, computational overhead, and scalability, while presenting DsDL as a foundational tool for this vision.
- **Summary**: Okay, I have reviewed the paper and will provide a summary, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, and assign a score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of Dataset- and Task-Independent Recommender Systems (DTIRS), a framework designed to maximize the reusability of recommender systems while minimizing the need for dataset- and task-specific configurations. Unlike Large Language Models (LLMs) that achieve task generalization directly, DTIRS aims to eliminate the need to rebuild or reconfigure recommendation pipelines for every new dataset or task. The framework leverages a novel Dataset Description Language (DsDL) to enable standardized dataset descriptions and explicit task definitions, facilitating autonomous feature engineering, model selection, and optimization. The paper outlines a roadmap for transitioning from dataset-agnostic (but task-specific) systems to fully dataset- and task-independent systems and discusses key challenges and tradeoffs. It proposes various levels of automation with respect to existing recommender systems and proposes a formal grammar for DsDL. The main goal is to increase code reusability and reduce barriers to adoption in the development of recommender systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of a dataset- and task-independent recommender system is a valuable goal, but the novelty of this paper is somewhat limited. The paper doesn't provide a working, fully realized system; instead, it proposes a conceptual framework and outlines a research roadmap. While DsDL offers a potential solution for standardizing dataset descriptions, it's essentially a schema definition language that needs further practical validation. Automated feature engineering and model selection are already well-explored areas in machine learning and recommender systems. The core contribution is more of an architectural vision than a concrete implementation. Many existing recommender system frameworks and AutoML solutions already automate parts of the pipeline that the authors aim to automate, albeit not in a fully task-independent manner. The formal grammar for DsDL also seems preliminary.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of DTIRS, if successfully implemented, could be significant. Recommender systems are often complex and require considerable expertise to deploy and maintain. A system that reduces the need for manual configuration and allows for greater reusability could lower the barrier to entry for smaller organizations and facilitate research reproducibility. The clear articulation of the problem of reusability and the proposed roadmap for achieving dataset- and task-independence is a valuable contribution. It can help to focus research efforts and encourage the development of more generalizable recommender systems.
    However, the paper currently lacks empirical evidence to support its claims of improved reusability and reduced configuration effort. Without experimental results, it is difficult to assess the true potential of DTIRS. The challenges outlined in Section 8 (Generalization vs. Specialization, Flat Table Dataset Representation, Computational Overhead, Scalability, Integration with Domain-Specific Knowledge) highlight some of the significant hurdles that must be overcome. Also, the comparison with LLMs seems a bit of a stretch, given the differences in the tasks and data involved. The paper relies heavily on existing ideas for which prior work is not clearly acknowledged.

*   **Strengths:** The paper clearly articulates a problem of reusability in recommender systems. The vision of dataset- and task-independence is well-defined and potentially beneficial. DsDL, although preliminary, is a promising approach to standardizing dataset descriptions.
*   **Weaknesses:** It lacks a concrete implementation and experimental results to support its claims. The novelty is somewhat limited, with many components already explored in previous research. Overstates the contribution by drawing parallels with LLMs, which do not completely overlap in this scenario. The challenges highlighted in Section 8 are significant. The paper does not give clear justification for the levels of automation they are proposing. The description of DSDL is rather vague.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the limited novelty and the lack of experimental validation, but acknowledging the potential significance of the vision and roadmap, I would give this paper a **Score: 5**. The paper identifies a relevant problem and proposes a framework, but the lack of implementation and evaluation significantly weakens its impact. Moreover, the contributions are more of an architectural vision rather than being a concrete system with tangible improvements. Although the idea is relevant and could influence research efforts, it does not provide sufficient novelty or impact in its current state to warrant a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 5/10

### Fault Localisation and Repair for DL Systems: An Empirical Study with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03396v1)
- **Authors**: Jinhan Kim, Nargiz Humbatova, Gunel Jahangirova, Shin Yoo, Paolo Tonella
- **Abstract**: Numerous Fault Localisation (FL) and repair techniques have been proposed to address faults in Deep Learning (DL) models. However, their effectiveness in practical applications remains uncertain due to the reliance on pre-defined rules. This paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of state-of-the-art FL and repair techniques, examining their advantages and limitations. Moreover, we introduce a novel approach that harnesses the power of Large Language Models (LLMs) in localising and repairing DL faults. Our evaluation, conducted on a carefully designed benchmark, reveals the strengths and weaknesses of current FL and repair techniques. We emphasise the importance of enhanced accuracy and the need for more rigorous assessment methods that employ multiple ground truth patches. Notably, LLMs exhibit remarkable performance in both FL and repair tasks. For instance, the GPT-4 model achieves 44% and 82% improvements in FL and repair tasks respectively, compared to the second-best tool, demonstrating the potential of LLMs in this domain. Our study sheds light on the current state of FL and repair techniques and suggests that LLMs could be a promising avenue for future advancements.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an empirical study on fault localization (FL) and repair techniques for deep learning (DL) systems, specifically focusing on the use of Large Language Models (LLMs). It evaluates existing FL and repair techniques, identifies their limitations, and proposes a novel approach leveraging LLMs to address DL faults. The authors conduct experiments on a carefully designed benchmark, revealing the strengths and weaknesses of current methods and highlighting the potential of LLMs in both FL and repair tasks. The study also introduces the concept of "neutrality analysis" to account for multiple alternative "ground truth" repairs, leading to a more comprehensive evaluation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the systematic evaluation of LLMs for both FL and repair in DL, particularly compared to established techniques and in integrating the idea of "neutrality analysis". Previous work has explored either LLMs in SE tasks (e.g., general code repair) or individual DL techniques, but this paper brings both together in a rigorous experimental setting and advances the state-of-the-art.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing a critical need in DL systems: improving reliability and accuracy. The findings demonstrate that LLMs offer substantial improvements over existing FL and repair approaches, offering a potentially transformative avenue for future research and practice. Furthermore, the neutrality analysis approach enhances the robustness and accuracy of DL fault evaluation, addressing a recognized limitation in current experimental practices.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a well-designed and executed empirical study, comparing various FL and repair techniques across a diverse benchmark, including real and artificial faults.
    *   **Neutrality Analysis:** The idea of neutrality analysis enhances the study by recognizing and accounting for multiple possible repairs, thus giving a better picture of technique effectiveness.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a clear methodology, including prompts used for LLMs, making the results reproducible.
    *   **Significant Results:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the potential of LLMs in DL FL and repair.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of DL Faults:** The benchmark focuses on specific categories of DL faults, mainly related to model architecture and hyperparameters. The paper acknowledges that it does not deal with data issues, which could limit the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Computational Resources**: Some of the repair tasks are very computationally intensive, with one repair even exceeding 48 hours. This makes the findings more accessible for researchers or engineers with access to more resources.
    *   **LLM Prompt Engineering:**  While the paper states best practices were followed for prompt engineering, there is more that could be done in this area. The results can be more generalized with automated prompt engineering.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field by:

    *   Encouraging further research on using LLMs for DL system debugging and repair.
    *   Promoting the adoption of neutrality analysis for more rigorous evaluation of DL fault localization and repair techniques.
    *   Informing the development of automated tools that leverage LLMs for DL system maintenance.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a significant contribution to the field. The use of LLMs with neutrality analysis is novel in a DL context, the study is thorough, and the results are compelling. The empirical study is comprehensive, the methodology is clear, and the findings have the potential to impact future research and practice. However, the limited scope of DL faults in the benchmark, particularly excluding data-related issues, prevents this from being considered a landmark paper, warranting a score below 9.

- **Classification**: cs.SE

### Sampling Preferences Yields Simple Trustworthiness Scores
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03399v1)
- **Authors**: Sean Steinle
- **Abstract**: With the onset of large language models (LLMs), the performance of artificial intelligence (AI) models is becoming increasingly multi-dimensional. Accordingly, there have been several large, multi-dimensional evaluation frameworks put forward to evaluate LLMs. Though these frameworks are much more realistic than previous attempts which only used a single score like accuracy, multi-dimensional evaluations can complicate decision-making since there is no obvious way to select an optimal model. This work introduces preference sampling, a method to extract a scalar trustworthiness score from multi-dimensional evaluation results by considering the many characteristics of model performance which users value. We show that preference sampling improves upon alternate aggregation methods by using multi-dimensional trustworthiness evaluations of LLMs from TrustLLM and DecodingTrust. We find that preference sampling is consistently reductive, fully reducing the set of candidate models 100% of the time whereas Pareto optimality never reduces the set by more than 50%. Likewise, preference sampling is consistently sensitive to user priors-allowing users to specify the relative weighting and confidence of their preferences-whereas averaging scores is intransigent to the users' prior knowledge.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

This paper introduces "preference sampling," a method for extracting a single trustworthiness score from multi-dimensional evaluations of AI models, particularly large language models (LLMs).  The method addresses the challenge of selecting the "best" model when performance is evaluated across many different characteristics (e.g., safety, truthfulness, fairness). Preference sampling works by sampling preference vectors from a Dirichlet distribution to represent user priorities regarding the characteristics of model performance.  These preference vectors are then used to weight the individual characteristic scores of each model, and the model with the highest weighted score is selected.  The authors demonstrate that preference sampling is more effective than Pareto optimality and averaging in reducing dimensionality while remaining sensitive to user preferences and incorporating user confidence levels in their prior knowledge.  The authors test their method using the multi-dimensional evaluation frameworks TrustLLM and DecodingTrust.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using preference sampling to create a single trustworthiness score *is* novel in the context of LLM evaluation. Previous methods either analyzed characteristics individually or used simple averaging, which is less sensitive to context and user preferences. The adaptation of techniques from multi-criteria decision-making (MCDM) to LLM evaluation is a worthwhile contribution. However, the paper doesn't substantially alter the MCDM techniques it employs. It mainly adapts them for the specific LLM context.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in the practical deployment of LLMs. As LLMs become more capable, their evaluation is inevitably more multi-faceted. Decision-makers need a way to aggregate diverse evaluation metrics into a coherent understanding of overall model trustworthiness.  The work provides a useful tool that enables AI practitioners to make model selection decisions in more nuanced ways, accounting for their specific values and goals. It offers a principled way to handle the inherent trade-offs between characteristics.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly defines the problem and motivates the need for a better dimensionality reduction technique.
    *   The preference sampling method is well-explained and intuitive.
    *   The paper provides empirical evidence showing that preference sampling outperforms Pareto optimality and averaging on two realistic evaluation frameworks (TrustLLM and DecodingTrust).
    *   The paper considers a range of user scenarios, including cases with strong prior knowledge and cases with low confidence in preferences.
    *   The method appears relatively easy to implement and integrate with existing evaluation pipelines.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental results, while demonstrating improvement, are limited to two LLM evaluation frameworks. Testing on a broader range of tasks and evaluation methodologies would strengthen the claims.
    *   The paper uses simulated preferences from a Dirichlet distribution. While this is a reasonable starting point, exploring how to elicit *real* user preferences would be valuable. The impact on model selection may be limited if the algorithm isn't well adopted for real-world use. The reliance on simulated data may not fully represent the complexities of actual user choices.
    *   The choice of the Dirichlet distribution, while convenient, is somewhat arbitrary. There could be other distributions that better model the diversity and relationships among user preferences.
    *   The paper touches on score interpretability but could delve deeper into how the resulting trustworthiness score should be communicated to and understood by non-expert stakeholders.
    *   The assumption that trustworthiness is fully compositional could be debated. Emergent properties of complex systems might not be adequately captured by a simple weighted sum of characteristics.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence how LLMs are evaluated and deployed in practice. As evaluation frameworks become more comprehensive, methods like preference sampling will become increasingly important for making informed decisions. Furthermore, its concepts can be generalized to other areas of AI, such as VLMs.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

While the paper's idea is novel in the LLM context and addresses a genuine practical problem, the core technical contribution is relatively incremental.  It adapts existing techniques from MCDM rather than developing fundamentally new algorithms or theoretical insights. The evaluation, though solid, is somewhat limited in scope. The reliance on simulated user preferences is a notable limitation, making it challenging to assess the real-world impact. The paper does not consider the interaction between preferences; it's possible that some combinations are inherently incompatible. While this may be beyond the scope of the current work, consideration would improve validity.

Considering the paper's novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of:

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Trajectory Prediction Meets Large Language Models: A Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03408v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Xu, Ruining Yang, Yitian Zhang, Yizhou Wang, Jianglin Lu, Mingyuan Zhang, Lili Su, Yun Fu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have sparked growing interest in integrating language-driven techniques into trajectory prediction. By leveraging their semantic and reasoning capabilities, LLMs are reshaping how autonomous systems perceive, model, and predict trajectories. This survey provides a comprehensive overview of this emerging field, categorizing recent work into five directions: (1) Trajectory prediction via language modeling paradigms, (2) Direct trajectory prediction with pretrained language models, (3) Language-guided scene understanding for trajectory prediction, (4) Language-driven data generation for trajectory prediction, (5) Language-based reasoning and interpretability for trajectory prediction. For each, we analyze representative methods, highlight core design choices, and identify open challenges. This survey bridges natural language processing and trajectory prediction, offering a unified perspective on how language can enrich trajectory prediction.
- **Summary**: This survey paper provides a comprehensive overview of the rapidly emerging field of trajectory prediction using large language models (LLMs). It categorizes existing works into five distinct areas: (1) Trajectory prediction via language modeling paradigms, (2) Direct trajectory prediction with pretrained language models, (3) Language-guided scene understanding for trajectory prediction, (4) Language-driven data generation for trajectory prediction, and (5) Language-based reasoning and interpretability for trajectory prediction.  The paper analyzes representative methods within each category, highlighting their design choices and identifying open challenges. The survey aims to bridge the gap between natural language processing and trajectory prediction research communities by providing a unified perspective on how language can enrich trajectory prediction.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey provides a broad overview of the intersection between LLMs and trajectory prediction, effectively categorizing and summarizing a diverse set of recent works. The categorization itself is insightful and helps to structure the field.
    *   **Timeliness:** The paper addresses a very timely and rapidly developing research area. The inclusion of papers published as recently as 2025 (assuming this isn't a typo, and the authors genuinely foresee publications) indicates an awareness of the latest developments.
    *   **Clear Organization and Presentation:** The paper is well-structured, with a clear taxonomy and concise summaries of individual methods. The figures and tables are helpful in visualizing the landscape of the field.
    *   **Identifies Open Challenges:** The survey doesn't just summarize existing work; it also identifies key challenges and potential future directions, which is valuable for guiding future research.
    *   **Bridging Communities:** It aims explicitly to connect two distinct research communities (NLP and trajectory prediction), which is a significant contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While broad in scope, the depth of analysis of individual papers is somewhat limited. The paper primarily summarizes the methods without in-depth critical evaluations of their performance, limitations, or assumptions.
    *   **Potential for Rapid Obsolescence:** Given the rapid pace of advancements in both LLMs and trajectory prediction, some parts of the survey may become outdated relatively quickly. Although the categorization will remain relevant, the details on specific methods might need frequent updates.
    *   **Uneven Emphasis:**  The survey's categorization, while logical, leads to an uneven distribution of emphasis across the categories. For example, "Trajectory Prediction via Language Modeling Paradigms" receives more attention than other categories.
    *   **Limited discussion of ethical implications**: The paper briefly touches on the amplification of existing biases but needs a more developed ethical discussion about using LLMs in safety-critical applications like autonomous driving.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The primary novelty lies in being the first dedicated survey of the intersection of LLMs and trajectory prediction. While surveys exist for LLMs in autonomous driving more broadly, a focused survey on trajectory prediction fills a significant gap.
    *   The significance of the survey stems from its ability to provide a coherent framework for understanding the diverse approaches being taken in this emerging field. It helps researchers navigate the literature, identify promising directions, and avoid redundant efforts.

**Justification for Score:**

The survey is a valuable and timely contribution to the field. Its comprehensiveness, clear organization, and identification of open challenges make it a useful resource for researchers and practitioners. However, the limited depth of analysis and the potential for rapid obsolescence, as well as the limited discussion on ethical considerations, prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hybrid Ensemble of Segmentation-Assisted Classification and GBDT for Skin Cancer Detection with Engineered Metadata and Synthetic Lesions from ISIC 2024 Non-Dermoscopic 3D-TBP Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03420v1)
- **Authors**: Muhammad Zubair Hasan, Fahmida Yasmin Rifat
- **Abstract**: Skin cancer is among the most prevalent and life-threatening diseases worldwide, with early detection being critical to patient outcomes. This work presents a hybrid machine and deep learning-based approach for classifying malignant and benign skin lesions using the SLICE-3D dataset from ISIC 2024, which comprises 401,059 cropped lesion images extracted from 3D Total Body Photography (TBP), emulating non-dermoscopic, smartphone-like conditions. Our method combines vision transformers (EVA02) and our designed convolutional ViT hybrid (EdgeNeXtSAC) to extract robust features, employing a segmentation-assisted classification pipeline to enhance lesion localization. Predictions from these models are fused with a gradient-boosted decision tree (GBDT) ensemble enriched by engineered features and patient-specific relational metrics. To address class imbalance and improve generalization, we augment malignant cases with Stable Diffusion-generated synthetic lesions and apply a diagnosis-informed relabeling strategy to harmonize external datasets into a 3-class format. Using partial AUC (pAUC) above 80 percent true positive rate (TPR) as the evaluation metric, our approach achieves a pAUC of 0.1755 -- the highest among all configurations. These results underscore the potential of hybrid, interpretable AI systems for skin cancer triage in telemedicine and resource-constrained settings.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, focusing on its novelty, significance, and overall contribution to the field of skin cancer detection.

**Summary**

The paper presents a hybrid machine and deep learning approach for classifying skin lesions as malignant or benign, using the ISIC 2024 SLICE-3D dataset (non-dermoscopic, 3D Total Body Photography images). The method combines vision transformers (EVA02) and convolutional ViT hybrids (EdgeNeXtSAC) for feature extraction, employing a segmentation-assisted classification pipeline. Predictions are then fused with a gradient-boosted decision tree (GBDT) ensemble that is enriched with engineered features and patient-specific relational metrics. The study also tackles class imbalance by augmenting malignant cases using Stable Diffusion-generated synthetic lesions and applies a diagnosis-informed relabeling strategy to integrate external datasets. The method is evaluated using partial AUC (pAUC) above 80% TPR, achieving a pAUC of 0.1755, which is the highest reported for their tested configurations.  The authors highlight the potential of their system for skin cancer triage in telemedicine and resource-constrained settings.

**Critical Evaluation**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Hybrid Approach:** The key strength is the combination of multiple techniques. Fusing deep learning (CNN, vision transformers) with traditional machine learning (GBDT) allows for leveraging the strengths of both paradigms. The deep learning models extract visual features, while GBDT leverages engineered metadata.
    * **Segmentation-Assisted Classification:** Integrating segmentation into the classification pipeline is a valuable addition. It helps to focus the model's attention on the relevant areas of the image (the lesion itself), potentially improving accuracy and explainability. The use of GradCAM++ visualizations supports this claim.
    * **Addressing Class Imbalance:** Tackling class imbalance is crucial for skin cancer detection. The use of Stable Diffusion to generate synthetic malignant lesions is a reasonable approach to increase the number of positive examples. The relabeling strategy for external datasets improves the utilization of pre-trained models for knowledge transfer, enhancing representation learning.
    * **Patient-Specific Features:**  Incorporating patient-specific relational features is another novel aspect. Contextual information like relative lesion characteristics could help in differentiating between benign and malignant lesions.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper uses pAUC as an evaluation metric, which prioritizes high sensitivity, a crucial aspect for skin cancer detection where missing a malignant case has high cost.
    * **3D-TBP focus:** The paper uses a dataset that mirrors the real-world of smartphone images and teledermatology.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Incremental Novelty:** While the paper integrates several existing techniques, the individual components are not particularly novel. The vision transformers, CNN architectures, GBDT ensembles, and even synthetic data generation are all established methods. The paper's novelty lies primarily in the specific combination and application of these techniques to the SLICE-3D dataset.
    * **Synthetic Data Validation:** A deeper analysis of the synthetic data quality is warranted. While Stable Diffusion can generate realistic images, it's important to ensure that the generated lesions are representative of real malignant lesions and do not introduce biases or artifacts.  The paper could have demonstrated the fidelity of synthetic data more rigourosly.
    * **Limited Explainability:** Although the segmentation and GradCAM++ visualizations provide some level of explainability, the GBDT models, while inherently more interpretable than deep neural networks, can still be complex and difficult to fully understand. Further investigation into feature importance is needed to validate the engineered features are indeed clinically relevant.
    * **Dataset Dependency:** The results are specific to the SLICE-3D dataset. Generalizability to other datasets or imaging modalities might be limited. The claim of resource-constrained settings needs to be further substantiated with results from deployment on such systems.
    * **Limited Error Analysis:** The error analysis highlights some challenges with texture and contrast, but could be more in-depth. Quantifying the contribution of each component to the overall performance would be very valuable.

* **Significance:**

    * The study demonstrates the potential of hybrid AI systems for skin cancer triage, potentially improving early detection and patient outcomes. The focus on non-dermoscopic images makes it more relevant to real-world teledermatology applications.
    * The combination of deep learning and machine learning approaches provides a more robust and potentially more interpretable system than using deep learning alone.
    * The methods to handle class imbalance and integrate external datasets can be valuable for other medical imaging tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a useful combination of existing techniques to address an important problem – early skin cancer detection. The use of a hybrid approach, the integration of segmentation, and the handling of class imbalance are all commendable. However, the lack of groundbreaking novelty in the individual components, the need for more in-depth analysis of the synthetic data, and the limited demonstration of generalizability restrict the overall impact.  The improvements, while present, feel incremental. Therefore, while the contribution is solid and practical, it doesn't represent a major breakthrough in the field.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### DistRAG: Towards Distance-Based Spatial Reasoning in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03424v1)
- **Authors**: Nicole R Schneider, Nandini Ramachandran, Kent O'Sullivan, Hanan Samet
- **Abstract**: Many real world tasks where Large Language Models (LLMs) can be used require spatial reasoning, like Point of Interest (POI) recommendation and itinerary planning. However, on their own LLMs lack reliable spatial reasoning capabilities, especially about distances. To address this problem, we develop a novel approach, DistRAG, that enables an LLM to retrieve relevant spatial information not explicitly learned during training. Our method encodes the geodesic distances between cities and towns in a graph and retrieves a context subgraph relevant to the question. Using this technique, our method enables an LLM to answer distance-based reasoning questions that it otherwise cannot answer. Given the vast array of possible places an LLM could be asked about, DistRAG offers a flexible first step towards providing a rudimentary `world model' to complement the linguistic knowledge held in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "DistRAG: Towards Distance-Based Spatial Reasoning in LLMs" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DistRAG, a novel approach designed to improve the spatial reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically focusing on distance-based reasoning. LLMs often struggle with geospatial understanding, especially when dealing with places not well-represented in their training data. DistRAG addresses this by:

1.  **Creating a Spatial Knowledge Store:** Encoding geodesic distances between cities and locations within an attributed graph.
2.  **Developing a Retriever:** Selecting relevant context subgraphs using either vector similarity or SPARQL query generation based on the user's question.
3.  **Augmenting LLM Input:** Providing the retrieved subgraph as context to the LLM to answer distance-based questions.

The authors evaluate DistRAG against a baseline LLM (GPT-4) on a dataset of varying difficulty levels, demonstrating improved performance on easy and medium spatial reasoning questions.  They also perform ablation studies to analyze the effect of knowledge store completeness on performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of augmenting LLMs with external knowledge for spatial reasoning isn't entirely new (RAG itself is well-established).  However, the *specific* implementation using a distance-attributed graph, coupled with both vector similarity and SPARQL query generation as retrieval mechanisms, does offer a distinct approach.  The application specifically focusing on *distance* reasoning is a valuable, focused contribution to spatial reasoning.  The ablation study on knowledge store sparsity adds further value.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a real limitation of LLMs: their lack of inherent geospatial understanding. While LLMs are powerful in language processing, they often struggle with tasks needing real-world spatial knowledge. DistRAG presents a practical method for integrating external geospatial information, which could significantly enhance the use of LLMs in applications like travel planning, location-based recommendations, and geographic information systems.  The explicit targeting of less-represented places, enhancing fairness and geographical accuracy, is a strong point.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of spatial reasoning in LLMs.
    *   **Well-defined method:**  DistRAG is thoroughly explained, with details on the spatial graph construction and retrieval mechanisms.
    *   **Empirical evaluation:** The experiments are well-designed, with a diverse dataset and comparisons against a strong baseline.  The ablation study provides valuable insights into the robustness of the approach.
    *   **Practical approach:** DistRAG provides a practical, adaptable method for grounding LLMs in the real world, avoiding the need for complete model retraining.
    *   **The zero MSE achieved for difficult questions using SPARQL is impressive, especially after providing a query structure template, which shows that model reasoning is more limited by the ability to form the correct query structure, not by its capabilities once the query structure is known.**

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the focus on distance-based reasoning is valuable, it's a relatively narrow aspect of spatial understanding. More complex spatial relationships (e.g., relative directions, topological relations) are not addressed.
    *   **Scalability:** While the authors address the computational burden of GraphRAG with subgraphs, no concrete evaluation on large-scale datasets is performed.
    *   **Complexity of SPARQL query generation:** The SPARQL query generation process, while effective when it works, can be brittle. The need to provide a query structure template is a limitation in fully automating the approach for difficult questions.
    *   **Error results for DistRAG using the vector method for Difficult datasets are worse than the baseline. More clarification on the possible causes of this regression may benefit the paper.**

*   **Potential Influence:**  DistRAG provides a concrete and implementable solution to a real problem. It can encourage further research in the following directions:

    *   Developing more robust retrieval techniques for spatial knowledge graphs.
    *   Extending DistRAG to handle more complex spatial relationships.
    *   Integrating DistRAG into real-world applications.
    *   Evaluating how this method might support few-shot or zero-shot knowledge transfer.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel, well-defined approach to address a specific challenge in LLM spatial reasoning. The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating the effectiveness of DistRAG for certain types of distance-based questions. The inclusion of an ablation study adds depth to the analysis. While the scope is limited, and the SPARQL query generation process requires manual assistance for complex queries, the paper provides a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to inspire future research and practical applications. The limitations of SPARQL query generation, and the limited scope relative to the broader field of Spatial Reasoning, temper the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Data-Driven Diffusion-based Approach for Audio Deepfake Explanations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03425v1)
- **Authors**: Petr Grinberg, Ankur Kumar, Surya Koppisetti, Gaurav Bharaj
- **Abstract**: Evaluating explainability techniques, such as SHAP and LRP, in the context of audio deepfake detection is challenging due to lack of clear ground truth annotations. In the cases when we are able to obtain the ground truth, we find that these methods struggle to provide accurate explanations. In this work, we propose a novel data-driven approach to identify artifact regions in deepfake audio. We consider paired real and vocoded audio, and use the difference in time-frequency representation as the ground-truth explanation. The difference signal then serves as a supervision to train a diffusion model to expose the deepfake artifacts in a given vocoded audio. Experimental results on the VocV4 and LibriSeVoc datasets demonstrate that our method outperforms traditional explainability techniques, both qualitatively and quantitatively.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of evaluating explainability techniques (XAI) for audio deepfake detection (ADD), a task hindered by the lack of readily available ground truth. The authors propose a novel data-driven approach leveraging paired real and vocoded audio samples, where the difference in time-frequency representation serves as a proxy for ground truth deepfake artifacts. This difference signal is then used to train a diffusion model, aiming to predict artifact regions in a given vocoded audio. The paper compares the proposed method with traditional XAI techniques (SHAP, LRP) on VocV4 and LibriSeVoc datasets, demonstrating qualitative and quantitative improvements in explaining deepfake detection. The diffusion model is conditioned in two ways, firstly on the spectrogram, and secondly, on intermediate features of a pre-trained ADD model.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel approach to addressing a significant challenge in the ADD explainability domain.  The idea of using vocoder-induced differences as ground truth is clever and provides a practical way to generate annotations for a task where they are usually absent. The application of a diffusion model in the context of XAI for audio, specifically focusing on artifact localization, is also a fresh perspective.  The idea of conditioning on both spectrogram and ADD-model intermediate features provides versatility. However, while the individual components (diffusion models, using paired vocoded data) are not entirely new, the combination and application to the problem of ADD explainability is a unique contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to improve our understanding of what ADD models are actually "seeing" or focusing on when making decisions.  By providing more accurate and interpretable explanations, the approach could help to identify vulnerabilities in ADD systems, leading to more robust detection mechanisms. Furthermore, a supervised technique like this could potentially lead to the development of more robust deepfake detection methods themselves by highlighting the key artifacts to focus on. The comparison against traditional XAI methods highlights the limitations of these approaches and strengthens the case for the data-driven diffusion model.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clever Approach:** The use of vocoder differences to generate ground truth is a key strength, addressing a major bottleneck in evaluating XAI for ADD.
    *   **Data-Driven:** By training a diffusion model, the approach learns specific patterns related to deepfake artifacts, potentially outperforming generic XAI techniques.
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:**  The paper provides a comprehensive set of quantitative metrics (GDice, F1, IoU, SSIM, Faithfulness) that support the qualitative findings.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The figures illustrate differences in attribution maps well.
    *   **Method versatility** The conditional diffusion model supports conditioning on either the spectrogram or intermediate features from an ADD model.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Vocoder Dependency:** The method's reliance on vocoded data could limit its generalizability to deepfakes generated using entirely different techniques (e.g., generative models trained end-to-end without vocoders). While vocoders are prevalent, they are not the only deepfake generation method. This is a key limitation.
    *   **Ground Truth Approximation:** While the difference signal is a reasonable proxy for ground truth, it's still an approximation. The artifacts introduced by a vocoder might not perfectly capture all the nuances of real-world deepfakes. Moreover, the method of binarizing this difference signal could be improved.
    *   **Limited Scope of XAI Techniques:** While SHAP and LRP are commonly used, the paper only explores a limited subset of XAI methods. Expanding the comparison could provide a more comprehensive picture. Transformer relevancy is not addressed because of the difficulty applying it to the architecture used.
    *   **Small Datasets:** Results are only demonstrated on VocV4 and LibriSeVoc, which, despite being relevant, may not fully represent the diverse landscape of audio deepfakes.
    *   **ADDSegDiff improvement gap to SpecSegDiff:** There are still existing gaps in accuracy when conditioning the diffusion model on ADD intermediate features.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in ADD explainability, providing a template for generating annotations and evaluating XAI methods in this domain. The diffusion-based approach could inspire the development of more accurate and robust deepfake detection systems by directing attention towards the most salient artifacts. However, future researchers must address the limitations concerning generalizability to non-vocoder based approaches.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and practically valuable approach to a challenging problem. The method of using vocoder-induced differences as ground truth is a significant strength, addressing a key hurdle in ADD explainability research. The application of diffusion models for artifact localization is also innovative. However, the vocoder dependency is a crucial limitation that impacts generalizability. While the experimental results are promising, the dependence on these datasets is a point that slightly limits the paper's impact and the scope of the XAI comparison, and the fact that ADDSegDiff still lags behind SpecSegDiff reduces the score.  Overall, the paper provides a solid contribution with the potential to influence future research, but the limitations need to be addressed in future work.

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 7/10

### Adaptive Task Vectors for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03426v1)
- **Authors**: Joonseong Kang, Soojeong Lee, Subeen Park, Sumin Park, Taero Kim, Jihee Kim, Ryunyi Lee, Kyungwoo Song
- **Abstract**: In-Context Learning (ICL) enables Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform tasks without parameter updates by conditioning on a few demonstrations provided in the prompt. Despite its success, ICL suffers from several limitations, including sensitivity to demonstration order, context length constraints, and computational inefficiency. To address these challenges, task vector-based approaches compress task information into a single vector. However, these methods typically construct task vectors from fixed sets of demonstrations and reuse them across input queries, without conditioning on the specific input. This limitation can lead models to struggle with effective adaptation when the input query is not well aligned with the underlying demonstrations, consequently degrading their generalization performance on unseen tasks. To overcome this limitation, we propose Adaptive Task Vectors (ATV), a simple and effective framework that dynamically generates task vectors conditioned on each input query. ATV employs a small language model to generate task vectors, which are then transformed to match the target LLM's architecture and applied to guide its output generation. In contrast to ICL and previous vector-based approaches, which rely on fixed demonstration sets and their corresponding vectors, ATV dynamically generates task vectors tailored to each specific input query and task. Consequently, ATV demonstrates strong performance and generalization capabilities, even for unseen tasks. Furthermore, we provide a theoretical analysis indicating that ATV is expressively equivalent to LoRA under equal rank budgets and more expressive than Prefix-Tuning, thereby offering formal support for its representational advantage.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Adaptive Task Vectors for Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces Adaptive Task Vectors (ATV), a novel framework designed to improve the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) in in-context learning (ICL) scenarios. ATV addresses limitations of traditional ICL and existing task vector approaches. Instead of relying on fixed demonstrations or static task vectors, ATV dynamically generates task vectors conditioned on each specific input query. This is achieved using a small language model to create intermediate task representations, which are then transformed to match the architecture of the target LLM and guide its output generation. The paper presents theoretical analysis, demonstrating that ATV is expressively equivalent to LoRA under equal rank budgets and more expressive than Prefix-Tuning. Empirical evaluations showcase ATV's strong performance, generalization capabilities, and interesting insights into model capacity and injection behavior across various tasks and model families.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of dynamically generating task vectors conditioned on each input query is a significant step forward. While task vector methods have been explored before, the input-awareness aspect is a key differentiator.
