    *   **Limited VLM Understanding of Complex Motion:** While VLM labeling works well for identifying artifacts, the paper admits its limitations in understanding complex actions. Further exploration into improving VLM reasoning about temporal dynamics could lead to further gains.
    *   **Training Instability:** The authors note training instability issues common in DPO, which needs to be more carefully investigated.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in preference learning for video generation. The ideas of guided sampling and segment-level preferences could be adopted and extended by other researchers. The successful application of VLMs for annotation could inspire more exploration of automated feedback signals.

**Justification:**

The paper's clear articulation of the problem, technically sound solutions, thorough empirical validation, and practical implications justify a high score. While the limitations related to reliance on ground truth videos and VLM understanding of complex motion are valid concerns, they do not overshadow the significant contributions of the work. The paper offers a substantial advancement in the field of text-to-video generation, with clear potential for further research and development.

### VChatter: Exploring Generative Conversational Agents for Simulating Exposure Therapy to Reduce Social Anxiety
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03520v1)
- **Authors**: Han Zhang, KaWing Tsang, Zhenhui Peng
- **Abstract**: Many people struggle with social anxiety, feeling fear, or even physically uncomfortable in social situations like talking to strangers. Exposure therapy, a clinical method that gradually and repeatedly exposes individuals to the source of their fear and helps them build coping mechanisms, can reduce social anxiety but traditionally requires human therapists' guidance and constructions of situations. In this paper, we developed a multi-agent system VChatter to explore large language models(LLMs)-based conversational agents for simulating exposure therapy with users. Based on a survey study (N=36) and an expert interview, VChatter includes an Agent-P, which acts as a psychotherapist to design the exposure therapy plans for users, and two Agent-Hs, which can take on different interactive roles in low, medium, and high exposure scenarios. A six-day qualitative study (N=10) showcases VChatter's usefulness in reducing users' social anxiety, feelings of isolation, and avoidance of social interactions. We demonstrated the feasibility of using LLMs-based conversational agents to simulate exposure therapy for addressing social anxiety and discussed future concerns for designing agents tailored to social anxiety.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VChatter, a multi-agent system leveraging large language models (LLMs) to simulate exposure therapy for individuals experiencing social anxiety. VChatter consists of Agent-P (a psychotherapist agent) and Agent-H (social agents embodying various interactive roles in different exposure scenarios). The system uses ChatGPT to personalize exposure therapy plans based on a user survey and expert interview. A six-day qualitative study (N=10) suggested VChatter's usefulness in reducing social anxiety, loneliness, and avoidance behaviors.  The paper demonstrates the feasibility of using LLM-based agents for simulated exposure therapy and discusses future design considerations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses an Important Problem:**  Social anxiety is a significant mental health challenge, and the paper directly tackles the problem of providing accessible and potentially less intimidating therapeutic interventions.
    *   **Novel Application of LLMs:** The paper showcases the potential of LLMs in creating dynamic and personalized exposure therapy simulations, a relatively unexplored area.
    *   **Well-Designed System:** The multi-agent architecture of VChatter (Agent-P and Agent-H) is conceptually sound, mirroring the roles of therapist and social actors in real-life exposure therapy. The integration of voice output and virtual avatars further enhances immersion, a key design concern.
    *   **Promising Preliminary Results:** The qualitative study shows promising results regarding anxiety reduction, reduced loneliness, and increased willingness to socialize, suggesting that VChatter can be effective.
    *   **Incorporates User Feedback:** The design of the system is informed by a survey study, showing an awareness of the target user group's specific needs and preferences. The use of an expert is also important.
    *   **Addresses Access Issues:** The use of a chatbot based on text and voice rather than VR headsets addresses potential accessibility issues linked to cost and technology literacy.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Small Sample Size:** The qualitative study involves only 10 participants, limiting the generalizability of the findings. A larger, more diverse sample would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Lack of Control Group:** The absence of a control group makes it difficult to attribute the observed improvements directly to VChatter. Factors like placebo effects or natural fluctuations in anxiety levels could be contributing to the results. A control group undergoing traditional therapy, or a waitlist control, would be extremely helpful.
    *   **Short Duration of the Study:** The six-day duration of the exposure therapy is relatively short compared to standard clinical practice. It is unclear whether the observed benefits are sustained over a longer period.
    *   **Limited Clinical Validation:** The study did not include participants with a clinical diagnosis of SAD. The conclusions may not apply to individuals with more severe social anxiety or comorbid mental health conditions. Furthermore, a clinical psychologist or psychiatrist was not directly involved in the analysis of the qualitative data, or in the system's iterative development.
    *   **Simplistic Friendliness Gradients:** The feedback indicates that a simple Low/Medium/High scale for agent friendliness is insufficient, and the agents may need to better reflect the specific exposure scenario to be effective.
    *   **Privacy Concerns:** While mentioned, the discussion about data anonymization could be strengthened. A more robust discussion of safety and privacy protocols, especially concerning sensitive user information, is needed.
    *   **Overclaiming without direct comparative evidence:**  The paper overclaims that *Chatbot with VHA offers users a more realistic interactive experience*. It would have been interesting to see a comparison between solely a chatbot, a VHA and a VHA with the chatbot.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The application of LLMs to simulate exposure therapy using a multi-agent system offers a novel approach. The potential for increased accessibility and reduced stigma makes it a valuable direction for research. However, the small scale of the evaluation and the lack of clinical validation somewhat limit the significance of the findings. Future research should focus on addressing the outlined weaknesses.
    Given the significance of reducing barriers to accessing therapy, and the novelty of approach using LLMs, the paper makes a substantial contribution to the field.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a promising and innovative approach to addressing social anxiety. While the preliminary results are encouraging, the small sample size, absence of a control group, and the study's short duration limit the strength of the conclusions. Addressing these limitations in future work would significantly increase the impact and significance of this research. The safety concerns could also be addressed further and more robustly.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### TokAlign: Efficient Vocabulary Adaptation via Token Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03523v1)
- **Authors**: Chong Li, Jiajun Zhang, Chengqing Zong
- **Abstract**: Tokenization serves as a foundational step for Large Language Models (LLMs) to process text. In new domains or languages, the inefficiency of the tokenizer will slow down the training and generation of LLM. The mismatch in vocabulary also hinders deep knowledge transfer between LLMs like token-level distillation. To mitigate this gap, we propose an efficient method named TokAlign to replace the vocabulary of LLM from the token co-occurrences view, and further transfer the token-level knowledge between models. It first aligns the source vocabulary to the target one by learning a one-to-one mapping matrix for token IDs. Model parameters, including embeddings, are rearranged and progressively fine-tuned for the new vocabulary. Our method significantly improves multilingual text compression rates and vocabulary initialization for LLMs, decreasing the perplexity from 3.4$\text{e}^2$ of strong baseline methods to 1.2$\text{e}^2$ after initialization. Experimental results on models across multiple parameter scales demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of TokAlign, which costs as few as 5k steps to restore the performance of the vanilla model. After unifying vocabularies between LLMs, token-level distillation can remarkably boost (+4.4% than sentence-level distillation) the base model, costing only 235M tokens.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TokAlign: Efficient Vocabulary Adaptation via Token Alignment":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TokAlign, a novel method for efficient vocabulary adaptation in large language models (LLMs). The approach leverages token co-occurrence statistics to align the vocabulary of a source LLM with a target vocabulary, aiming to improve performance in new domains or languages and facilitate token-level knowledge transfer. TokAlign learns a token-token alignment matrix based on GloVe embeddings trained on a large corpus. It then initializes the embedding layer and language modeling head of the target model using the alignment matrix and fine-tunes the model in a two-stage process. The authors demonstrate that TokAlign improves compression rates on multilingual text, achieves strong vocabulary initialization, and enhances cross-lingual knowledge transfer and token-level distillation. They show that the proposed method achieves comparable results or even outperforms models with a higher parameter count.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The idea of aligning token IDs based on co-occurrence is not entirely new. GloVe embeddings and co-occurrence matrices are well-established techniques. The novelty lies in the *application* of these techniques to vocabulary adaptation for LLMs and in the specific pipeline proposed (token-token alignment matrix, two-stage fine-tuning). The two-stage fine-tuning process may not be revolutionary. But it seems like a valuable piece that contributes to performance improvement. While other works exist on vocabulary expansion and adaptation, TokAlign distinguishes itself by its unsupervised nature (not requiring bilingual dictionaries), its focus on whole vocabulary replacement and transfer, and its efficient initialization.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses an important problem in the LLM space. Retraining LLMs from scratch for new vocabularies is extremely costly. Efficient vocabulary adaptation techniques like TokAlign are crucial for improving the practical applicability and accessibility of LLMs. The demonstrated improvements in compression rate, cross-lingual transfer, and token-level distillation suggest that TokAlign offers a valuable solution.  The ability to transfer knowledge between models with different vocabularies is particularly significant for enabling knowledge sharing and reducing the need for large-scale retraining. Demonstrating a performance boost over sentence-level distillation (token-level) is an advantage.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing tokenizers in new domains and languages and the challenges of vocabulary mismatch in knowledge transfer.
*   **Well-Defined Method:** TokAlign is well-defined and described in a step-by-step manner.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors provide a thorough evaluation of TokAlign across multiple parameter scales, languages, and tasks.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness and generalization of TokAlign, showing improvements in compression rates, perplexity, cross-lingual transfer, and token-level distillation.
*   **Efficiency:** The paper demonstrates that TokAlign is faster and less computationally expensive compared to strong baselines.
* The two-stage process leads to a more stable convergence.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Innovation:** While the application to LLMs is valuable, the core ideas behind TokAlign (GloVe embeddings, co-occurrence) are well-established, which can make the novelty limited.
*   **Dependence on Corpus Quality:** The performance of TokAlign relies heavily on the quality and representativeness of the pre-training corpus used to learn the GloVe embeddings. This dependence is not explicitly discussed.
*   **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:**  The paper does not provide an in-depth analysis of the cases where TokAlign fails or performs poorly. More analysis could potentially highlight areas for improvement. The token-alignment process seems to be a bit simplistic, and this needs to be clearly mentioned in the limitations.
*   **Baseline Methods:** While compared to baseline techniques, random initializations and permutations are fairly low-hanging. Comparing to better baseline methods like sentence-level distillation is more competitive.
* The paper needs to mention that it does not discuss open-source language models with unknown pre-training data distributions.
* Some parts are a bit vague, for example what is `relative representations`. This needs to be made clearer.

**Potential Influence:**

TokAlign has the potential to become a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners working with LLMs in resource-constrained settings or in multilingual scenarios. The efficient vocabulary adaptation and knowledge transfer capabilities of TokAlign can facilitate the development of more accessible and adaptable LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper. The paper clearly addresses a relevant problem, provides a well-defined solution, and presents solid empirical results. TokAlign offers a practical approach to efficient vocabulary adaptation that has the potential to impact the LLM field. But some analysis are vague and the paper does not have some in-depth discussions. I also thought the core method is a little bit simplistic.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Seed-Coder: Let the Code Model Curate Data for Itself
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03524v1)
- **Authors**: Yuyu Zhang, Jing Su, Yifan Sun, Chenguang Xi, Xia Xiao, Shen Zheng, Anxiang Zhang, Kaibo Liu, Daoguang Zan, Tao Sun, Jinhua Zhu, Shulin Xin, Dong Huang, Yetao Bai, Lixin Dong, Chao Li, Jianchong Chen, Hanzhi Zhou, Yifan Huang, Guanghan Ning, Xierui Song, Jiaze Chen, Siyao Liu, Kai Shen, Liang Xiang, Yonghui Wu
- **Abstract**: Code data in large language model (LLM) pretraining is recognized crucial not only for code-related tasks but also for enhancing general intelligence of LLMs. Current open-source LLMs often heavily rely on human effort to produce their code pretraining data, such as employing hand-crafted filtering rules tailored to individual programming languages, or using human-annotated data to train quality filters. However, these approaches are inherently limited in scalability, prone to subjective biases, and costly to extend and maintain across diverse programming languages. To address these challenges, we introduce Seed-Coder, a series of open-source LLMs comprising base, instruct and reasoning models of 8B size, minimizing human involvement in data construction. Our code pretraining data is produced by a model-centric data pipeline, which predominantly leverages LLMs for scoring and filtering code data. The instruct model is further trained via supervised fine-tuning and preference optimization, and the reasoning model leverages Long-Chain-of-Thought (LongCoT) reinforcement learning to improve multi-step code reasoning. Seed-Coder achieves state-of-the-art results among open-source models of similar size and even surpasses some much larger models, demonstrating superior performance in code generation, code completion, code editing, code reasoning, and software engineering tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Seed-Coder paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Seed-Coder, a family of open-source 8B Large Language Models (LLMs) for code, including base, instruct, and reasoning models.  A core focus is minimizing human involvement in data construction. Their approach uses LLMs to score and filter code data, instead of relying on hand-crafted rules. The instruct model undergoes Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and direct preference optimization (DPO), while the reasoning model uses Long-Chain-of-Thought (LongCoT) reinforcement learning for multi-step code reasoning.  The models achieve state-of-the-art performance among open-source models of similar size across code generation, completion, editing, reasoning, and software engineering tasks. The paper details the data pipeline, training strategies, and extensive evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The primary novelty of the paper lies in its model-centric data curation pipeline.  While other open-source code LLMs use human-engineered rules or human annotations, Seed-Coder leverages LLMs to score and filter training data. It’s an interesting attempt to embrace scaling by relying on an LLM to produce more LLMs, which addresses the "Bitter Lesson". The paper doesn't *invent* new LLM architectures or training algorithms, but its data-centric approach is a valuable contribution. The use of LongCoT reinforcement learning for an 8B model is also notable. Also, the paper offers detailed architectural description of the base architecture.
*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from a few key aspects:

    *   **Open Source Contribution:** Releasing open-source code LLMs contributes directly to the research community, enabling further exploration and advancements. Seed-Coder offers a competitive alternative to closed-source models.

    *   **Demonstrating Data-Centric Scaling:** The results provide evidence that a model-centric data pipeline can produce high-performing code LLMs with minimal human intervention, opening up possibilities for scaling data production.

    *   **Performance:** The models achieve strong results compared to other open-source 8B models and sometimes even outperform larger ones. The reported outperformance of Qwen2.5-Coder-14B with a smaller 8B model is an important insight.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Detailed Data Pipeline Description:** The paper provides a good level of detail about the data pipeline, including data sources, filtering methods, and FIM application.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluations are extensive and cover a wide range of coding tasks. The use of both static and live benchmarks (e.g., LiveCodeBench) is appreciated.
    *   **Focus on Reasoning:** The work tackles code reasoning which is a critical area, and their demonstration of improved reasoning with LongCoT is a strong point.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalization:** The heavy focus on coding tasks during training may come at the expense of general natural language understanding. This is acknowledged in the conclusion, but it remains a significant limitation.
    *   **Limited Novelty in RL application:** The RL setup using an existing code codebase seems rather standard. More innovation could be added to that end.
    *   **Reliance on existing LLMs:** The LLM-powered data curation is itself reliant on existing powerful (and potentially closed-source) LLMs like DeepSeek-V2-Chat. This poses a scalability bottleneck and introduces bias, even though it's a clever starting point. If those models had strong biases, then the new one will learn to amplify them.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence research in a few ways:

*  It will encourage further research into model-centric data pipelines for LLMs, moving away from exclusively relying on hand-engineered rules.
*   It provides the community with an accessible and competitive open-source code LLM.
*   It demonstrates the potential for RL to enhance the reasoning capabilities of smaller-scale models.

**Overall Assessment:**

The Seed-Coder paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of code LLMs. Its model-centric data curation pipeline is a novel and promising approach that can contribute to scaling data production for these models. While it relies heavily on other LLMs to build the data corpus, the empirical demonstration is convincing.

### Video-Skill-CoT: Skill-based Chain-of-Thoughts for Domain-Adaptive Video Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03525v1)
- **Authors**: Daeun Lee, Jaehong Yoon, Jaemin Cho, Mohit Bansal
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning have improved complex video understanding, but existing methods often struggle to adapt to domain-specific skills (e.g., event detection, spatial relation understanding, emotion understanding) over various video content. To address this, we propose Video-Skill-CoT (a.k.a. Video-SKoT), a framework that automatically constructs and leverages skill-aware CoT supervisions for domain-adaptive video reasoning. First, we construct skill-based CoT annotations: we extract domain-relevant reasoning skills from training questions, cluster them into a shared skill taxonomy, and create detailed multi-step CoT rationale tailored to each video-question pair for training. Second, we introduce a skill-specific expert learning framework. Each expert module specializes in a subset of reasoning skills and is trained with lightweight adapters using the collected CoT supervision. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach on three video understanding benchmarks, where Video-SKoT consistently outperforms strong baselines. We also provide in-depth analyses on comparing different CoT annotation pipelines and learned skills over multiple video domains.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "VIDEO-SKILL-COT: Skill-based Chain-of-Thoughts for Domain-Adaptive Video Reasoning," based on the provided OCR:

**Summary**

The paper introduces VIDEO-SKILL-COT (VIDEO-SKOT), a novel framework for improving video reasoning capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), especially when adapting to different video domains. The core idea is to create and leverage *skill-aware* Chain-of-Thought (CoT) supervision.  The approach involves two main steps:

1.  **Skill-Based CoT Annotation:** This is done automatically. The method first extracts high-level reasoning skill descriptions from training questions. These skills are then clustered into a taxonomy. Questions are then annotated with the most relevant skills, and multi-step CoT rationales are generated, conditioned on these skills.
2.  **Skill-Specific Expert Learning:**  The authors train specialized expert models (using LoRA adapters) for different subsets of reasoning skills.  During inference, each input is routed to the most appropriate expert based on the question's required skills.

The method is evaluated on three diverse video QA datasets: E.T.-Bench (temporal), VSI-Bench (spatial), and CinePile (movie narrative). VIDEO-SKOT consistently outperforms strong baseline MLLMs. The authors also provide ablation studies and visualizations to validate their approach.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable and relatively novel approach. While CoT has been applied to video understanding, and modular/expert-based learning is not new, the combination of:
    *   Automatically generating skill-aware CoT annotations specifically for video understanding,
    *   Creating a skill taxonomy
    *   Training skill-specialized experts with LoRA
    is a unique and well-motivated contribution. The focus on *domain adaptation* using skills is a significant aspect of the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in video understanding: the difficulty of MLLMs generalizing across different video domains and reasoning tasks. The proposed solution, if effective, can improve the robustness and adaptability of these models. The experimental results demonstrate tangible improvements on several challenging datasets.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing CoT approaches in adapting to domain-specific skills for video understanding.
    *   **Well-Designed Framework:** The VIDEO-SKOT framework is logically structured and clearly explained.
    *   **Automatic Annotation:** The method automates the CoT annotation process, making it scalable and avoiding the cost of manual annotation.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the superiority of VIDEO-SKOT over strong baseline models.  The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of the key components (skill-based CoT and skill-specific experts).
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:**  The qualitative examples and visualizations help to illustrate the benefits of skill-based CoT.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on underlying MLLM:** The framework's performance depends on the quality of the pre-trained MLLM. The authors acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **Complexity:** The framework is relatively complex, involving several components. While the paper explains the components clearly, reimplementation may be somewhat challenging.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:**  While the authors provide training details, a more in-depth discussion of hyperparameter sensitivity would be useful.
    *   **Limited generalization testing:** While the paper evaluates domain adaptation on unseen video QA datasets with distinct domains, it would be even stronger to demonstrate transfer to tasks requiring skills that were not explicitly seen during training.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of video understanding by:
    *   Motivating researchers to explore skill-aware reasoning in MLLMs.
    *   Providing a practical framework for domain adaptation in video QA.
    *   Inspiring new methods for automatic CoT annotation.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8**. The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated framework that addresses a significant problem in video understanding. The automatic generation of skill-aware CoT annotations and the use of skill-specific experts are valuable contributions. The experimental results are convincing, and the ablation studies provide insights into the effectiveness of the framework. The paper is also well-written and clearly explains the proposed approach.

The weaknesses of the paper (complexity, some dependency on the underlying MLLM, and potential halluciations) are present, but are somewhat mitigated by its strengths and the clarity of the presentation.

### Across Programming Language Silos: A Study on Cross-Lingual Retrieval-augmented Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03535v1)
- **Authors**: Qiming Zhu, Jialun Cao, Xuanang Chen, Yaojie Lu, Hongyu Lin, Xianpei Han, Le Sun, Shing-Chi Cheung
- **Abstract**: Current research on large language models (LLMs) with retrieval-augmented code generation (RACG) mainly focuses on single-language settings, leaving cross-lingual effectiveness and security unexplored. Multi-lingual RACG systems are valuable for migrating code-bases across programming languages (PLs), yet face risks from error (e.g. adversarial data corruption) propagation in cross-lingual transfer. We construct a dataset spanning 13 PLs with nearly 14k instances to explore utility and robustness of multi-lingual RACG systems. Our investigation reveals four key insights: (1) Effectiveness: multi-lingual RACG significantly enhances multi-lingual code LLMs generation; (2) Inequality: Java demonstrate superior cross-lingual utility over Python in RACG; (3) Robustness: Adversarial attacks degrade performance significantly in mono-lingual RACG but show mitigated impacts in cross-lingual scenarios; Counterintuitively, perturbed code may improve RACG in cross-lingual scenarios; (4) Specialization: Domain-specific code retrievers outperform significantly general text retrievers. These findings establish foundation for developing effective and secure multi-lingual code assistants.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents a comprehensive study on cross-lingual Retrieval-Augmented Code Generation (RACG).  The authors construct a dataset of nearly 14,000 code generation instances across 13 programming languages to investigate the effectiveness and robustness of multi-lingual RACG. The study reveals several key insights:  Multi-lingual RACG enhances code generation, Java demonstrates superior cross-lingual utility over Python in RACG, adversarial attacks are mitigated in cross-lingual scenarios, and domain-specific retrievers outperform general text retrievers.  The work analyzes both utility (effectiveness) and security (robustness against adversarial attacks) aspects of multi-lingual RACG.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper is among the first to systematically explore multi-lingual RACG across a diverse set of programming languages. Previous work largely focused on single-language settings or a limited number of languages (typically Python and Java). The exploration of adversarial attacks in a *cross-lingual* setting is also a novel contribution.  The finding that perturbed code can sometimes *improve* cross-lingual performance is particularly counterintuitive and interesting.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important and growing area of research: leveraging large language models for code generation and migration across different programming languages. The findings have practical implications for enterprise modernization efforts, polyglot software development, and the construction of robust and secure code assistants. The identified advantages of Java over Python as a retrieval corpus, and the mitigated impacts of adversarial attacks in cross-lingual scenarios are valuable insights for future research. The dataset construction (spanning 13 PLs and 14k instances) contributes a valuable resource to the community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The study provides a thorough evaluation of multi-lingual RACG across numerous settings and programming languages.
    *   **Well-Defined Research Questions:** The research questions (RQ1-RQ4) are clearly formulated and address important aspects of multi-lingual RACG.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The construction and release of a high-quality, multi-lingual code generation dataset is a significant contribution.
    *   **Interesting and Actionable Insights:** The paper uncovers several non-trivial findings (e.g., effectiveness of Java corpus, mitigation of adversarial attacks) that have practical implications.
    *   **Analysis of Different LLMs:** Analyzing both multi-lingual and mono-lingual LLMs provides a more nuanced understanding of cross-lingual transfer.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Model Scale:** The study primarily focuses on relatively small language models (around 7B parameters).  It is unclear how the findings would generalize to larger, more powerful models. The paper should acknowledge this limitation and encourage future research on larger models.
    *   **Retrieval Method Focus:** The paper could benefit from more extensive exploration of retrieval methods. While CodeRankEmbed is used, a more detailed comparison of different embedding techniques (including more recent advances in code-specific embeddings) would strengthen the analysis in RQ4.  The current discussion is brief.
    *   **Limited Attack Analysis:** The adversarial attack analysis, while novel, could be deepened. Examining specific attack strategies tailored for cross-lingual transfer (beyond simple perturbations) could reveal more targeted vulnerabilities.

*   **Justification for the Score:** The paper is a solid contribution to the field. While the model size limitations and opportunities for deeper analysis prevent it from being a groundbreaking work, the scope, systematic evaluation, and novel findings are significant. The dataset release further enhances its value.  The paper fills a gap in the current literature and provides a strong foundation for future research in this area.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Debate, Reflect, and Distill: Multi-Agent Feedback with Tree-Structured Preference Optimization for Efficient Language Model Enhancement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03541v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaofeng Zhou, Heyan Huang, Lizi Liao
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) continue to set new standards in knowledge-intensive and complex reasoning tasks, yet their high computational demands limit widespread adoption. While distilling large models into smaller ones offers a sustainable solution, current techniques--such as static knowledge distillation, resource-intensive reinforcement learning from human feedback, or limited self-reflection--struggle to yield substantial and lasting performance gains. In this paper, we present a novel Debate and Reflect (D&R) framework that orchestrates multi-turn debates between smaller models and stronger teacher models, eliciting actionable feedback (e.g., error analysis, corrective strategies) to guide student models. Further, we introduce Tree-structured Direct Preference Optimization (T-DPO) to efficiently leverage these debate logs, organizing interactions into a hierarchical format for effective training. Empirical evaluations across diverse NLP benchmarks demonstrate that our approach significantly improves smaller-model accuracy, robustness, and generalization, outperforming conventional baselines by a large margin.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Debate, Reflect, and Distill: Multi-Agent Feedback with Tree-Structured Preference Optimization for Efficient Language Model Enhancement":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Debate and Reflect (D&R), a novel framework for distilling knowledge from large language models (LLMs) into smaller, more efficient models. The core idea involves a multi-turn debate between a student model (the smaller model being trained) and one or more teacher models (stronger LLMs). During these debates, the student receives feedback from the teachers in the form of error analysis and corrective strategies. The student also engages in self-reflection.  A key component is the Tree-structured Direct Preference Optimization (T-DPO) which organizes the debate logs into hierarchical preference trees.  This structured data allows the student model to learn not only *what* the best answer is, but also *why* certain answers are preferred.  Empirical evaluations across several NLP benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements in accuracy, robustness, and generalization compared to conventional knowledge distillation, supervised fine-tuning, and multi-teacher knowledge distillation methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty stems from the combination of several existing techniques in a new and effective way. While knowledge distillation, multi-agent debate, and preference optimization are individually established concepts, their integration within the D&R framework, especially with the tree-structured preference learning, is a valuable contribution. The use of *both* teacher feedback *and* student self-reflection is a notable feature, addressing a weakness in many existing knowledge distillation approaches.  However, each component is not entirely novel. D&R builds upon existing methods like MAGDI, and uses similar underlying techniques such as LoRA.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of D&R is substantial.  Making LLMs more accessible and sustainable is a critical challenge. By demonstrating that smaller models can achieve significantly higher performance with a carefully designed distillation process, the paper contributes to this goal.  The empirical results are compelling, showing clear improvements over established baselines across a range of tasks. The analysis of the different components, showing the importance of self-reflection and teacher feedback, helps to better understand the process. The result that D&R can work effectively with limited data and scales efficiently is also significant.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** The D&R framework is well-defined and addresses several limitations of traditional knowledge distillation methods.
    *   **Effective Use of Data:** The approach leverages the limited data resources very effectively by constructing diverse debate logs and using preference tree construction to help improve results in data-scarce environments.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The results on diverse NLP benchmarks provide convincing evidence of the effectiveness of D&R.  The experiments are well-designed and controlled, allowing for a clear comparison with baselines.
    *   **Analysis of Components:** The paper includes insightful analysis, showing the contribution of different components like self-reflection and teacher feedback.
    *   **Generalizability:** D&R achieves improvements across student models of varying capabilities and is demonstrated on various knowledge-intensive tasks, showcasing the framework's generalizability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The framework is more complex to implement and tune than standard knowledge distillation. This potentially hinders adoption. The need to generate debate data also adds overhead compared to SFT.
    *   **Reliance on Teacher Models:** The performance of D&R is inherently tied to the quality of the teacher models. The paper addresses the importance of this and uses some strong teacher models, but how it performs with weaker teacher models is less clear.
    *   **Evaluation Metrics:** While the use of accuracy and exact match metrics is standard, a more in-depth analysis of the *reasoning process* of the student models would be valuable.
    *   **Computational Cost:** While it improves inference efficiency, generating the debate data and training with T-DPO still incurs a significant computational cost, though the paper attempts to alleviate this with LORA.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Reasoning Paths:** The primary evaluation metric is correctness of the final answer, which may not fully capture the validity of the reasoning paths, potentially leading to some false positive results.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   D&R could influence future research on knowledge distillation, particularly in the context of LLMs.
    *   The framework could be adopted in practical applications where efficient and accurate smaller models are needed.
    *   The approach could inspire new methods for integrating multi-agent interaction and preference learning in other AI domains.
    *   The analysis of the relative contributions of self-reflection and teacher feedback could inform the design of future AI learning systems.

**Justification for the Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, and its novel approach to knowledge distillation, I would give this paper a score of **Score: 7**. While the D&R framework is not radically different from existing techniques, the integration of multi-agent debate, self-reflection, targeted feedback, and T-DPO is a significant advancement. The empirical results are compelling and demonstrate the potential of D&R to improve the performance of smaller language models. The weaknesses related to complexity and potential computational cost (debate data generation) somewhat temper its impact. Future research building on this approach could potentially address these shortcomings and further increase its impact. The limitations of only measuring correctness of final answers, instead of reasoning paths also brings down the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### BPO: Revisiting Preference Modeling in Direct Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03557v1)
- **Authors**: Lin Sun, Chuang Liu, Peng Liu, Bingyang Li, Weijia Lu, Ning Wu
- **Abstract**: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) have emerged as a popular method for aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences. While DPO effectively preserves the relative ordering between chosen and rejected responses through pairwise ranking losses, it often neglects absolute reward magnitudes. This oversight can decrease the likelihood of chosen responses and increase the risk of generating out-of-distribution responses, leading to poor performance. We term this issue Degraded Chosen Responses (DCR).To address this issue, we propose Balanced Preference Optimization (BPO), a novel framework that dynamically balances the optimization of chosen and rejected responses through two key components: balanced reward margin and gap adaptor. Unlike previous methods, BPO can fundamentally resolve DPO's DCR issue, without introducing additional constraints to the loss function. Experimental results on multiple mathematical reasoning tasks show that BPO significantly outperforms DPO, improving accuracy by +10.1% with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (18.8% to 28.9%) and +11.7% with Qwen2.5-Math-7B (35.0% to 46.7%). It also surpasses DPO variants by +3.6% over IPO (43.1%), +5.0% over SLiC (41.7%), and +3.1% over Cal-DPO (43.6%) on the same model. Remarkably, our algorithm requires only a single line of code modification, making it simple to implement and fully compatible with existing DPO-based frameworks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper identifies a problem called "Degraded Chosen Responses" (DCR) in Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), a popular technique for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. DCR occurs when the likelihood of chosen responses decreases during DPO training, potentially leading to out-of-distribution responses and degraded performance. To address this, the authors propose Balanced Preference Optimization (BPO), a novel framework that balances the optimization of chosen and rejected responses through two components: a balanced reward margin and a gap adaptor. BPO requires only a single line of code modification to existing DPO implementations and shows significant performance improvements on mathematical reasoning tasks compared to DPO and its variants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the identification and characterization of the DCR problem within the context of DPO and the subsequent BPO framework to address it. This is more than a simple hyperparameter tuning; it's a rethinking of the reward signal to maintain the quality of chosen responses.  The balanced reward margin concept, while simple in implementation (a `min` function), is a crucial conceptual contribution. The gap adaptor provides a tunable mechanism to fine-tune the balance between maximizing chosen responses and suppressing rejected ones.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper comes from its practical implications. DPO has become a widely adopted method for LLM alignment, and any improvement in its stability and performance has a direct impact on the quality of LLMs.  The empirical results, demonstrating consistent improvements over DPO and its variants across various model architectures and scales, underscore the significance. Moreover, the simplicity of implementation makes BPO easy to adopt, increasing its potential impact. While the experiments primarily focus on mathematical reasoning, this application highlights the potential benefit in tasks requiring factual accuracy and reasoning capability, which is crucial for many LLM applications. The theoretical analysis, though presented concisely, also adds significance by providing insights into the mechanism driving performance improvements and proving a lower bound on chosen response likelihood.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition (DCR).
    *   Simple and elegant solution (BPO).
    *   Significant performance improvements across multiple models and benchmarks.
    *   Easy to implement (single line code change).
    *   Provides theoretical justification.
    *   Addresses a relevant and important problem in LLM alignment.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Experiments focus primarily on mathematical reasoning. Further experiments across diverse datasets like text generation, instruction following would strengthen the claim.
    *   Limited limitations discussion. While the paper mentions the restriction to offline methods, a more thorough discussion of potential failure cases or sensitivity to hyperparameter settings would enhance the paper.

**Rationale for the score:**

The paper presents a well-defined problem in DPO, a popular alignment technique, and offers a surprisingly simple and effective solution. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating consistent improvements over DPO and its variants. The ease of implementation further increases its potential impact. While the experimental validation could be expanded, and a more comprehensive discussion of limitations would enhance the paper, the novelty and significance of the contribution justify a strong score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### ConsistentChat: Building Skeleton-Guided Consistent Dialogues for Large Language Models from Scratch
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03558v1)
- **Authors**: Jiawei Chen, Xinyan Guan, Qianhao Yuan, Guozhao Mo, Weixiang Zhou, Yaojie Lu, Hongyu Lin, Ben He, Le Sun, Xianpei Han
- **Abstract**: Current instruction data synthesis methods primarily focus on single-turn instructions and often neglect cross-turn coherence, resulting in context drift and reduced task completion rates in extended conversations. To address this limitation, we propose Skeleton-Guided Multi-Turn Dialogue Generation, a framework that constrains multi-turn instruction synthesis by explicitly modeling human conversational intent. It operates in two stages: (1) Intent Modeling, which captures the global structure of human dialogues by assigning each conversation to one of nine well-defined intent trajectories, ensuring a coherent and goal-oriented information flow; and (2) Skeleton Generation, which constructs a structurally grounded sequence of user queries aligned with the modeled intent, thereby serving as a scaffold that constrains and guides the downstream instruction synthesis process. Based on this process, we construct ConsistentChat, a multi-turn instruction dataset with approximately 15,000 multi-turn conversations and 224,392 utterances. Experiments on the Light, Topdial, and MT-Eval benchmarks show that models fine-tuned on ConsistentChat achieve a 20-30% improvement in chat consistency and up to a 15% increase in task success rate, significantly outperforming models trained on existing single-turn and multi-turn instruction datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ConsistentChat: Building Skeleton-Guided Consistent Dialogues for Large Language Models from Scratch":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces *ConsistentChat*, a novel framework for synthesizing multi-turn instruction datasets for Large Language Models (LLMs). Addressing the limitations of existing methods that focus on single-turn interactions and often result in context drift, ConsistentChat models human conversational intent by (1) assigning dialogues to predefined intent trajectories (Intent Modeling) and (2) generating structurally grounded user queries aligned with these intents (Skeleton Generation). This approach aims to produce more coherent and goal-oriented conversations. The authors create a dataset with approximately 15,000 multi-turn dialogues and 224,392 utterances using their framework, and experimental results on various benchmarks demonstrate that models fine-tuned on ConsistentChat exhibit significant improvements in chat consistency and task success rates compared to models trained on existing datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of modeling conversational intent with predefined trajectories to guide multi-turn dialogue generation is a significant contribution. Prior works have largely focused on turn-level coherence or used less structured approaches. Explicitly modeling conversational intent and information flow is a good way to structure multi-turn conversations and avoid context drift. The introduction of the skeleton-guided approach is also novel.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation in current instruction data synthesis methods. By tackling the problem of context drift and improving consistency in multi-turn dialogues, this work is definitely relevant. The experimental results demonstrating improvements in both chat consistency and task success suggest that ConsistentChat can enable LLMs to engage in more effective and realistic conversations.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is well-written and presents a clear and concise methodology.
    *   The experiments are comprehensive, covering several dialogue benchmarks and comparing against various baselines.
    *   The reported improvements in chat consistency and task success are substantial and statistically significant.
    *   The analysis of how existing models degrade in multi-turn conversations is insightful and motivates the proposed approach well.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The specific intent trajectories used (nine categories) may be somewhat arbitrary, and the impact of different choices could be explored further. The description in the Appendix is helpful, but a more detailed justification for the selection of these particular categories would be useful.
    *   The framework heavily relies on LLMs for query and response generation. The generated content might still suffer from biases or limitations of the underlying LLMs. Human evaluation of the generated dialogues could strengthen the validation of the dataset's quality.
    *   While the paper shows improvements, there's limited discussion on the types of dialogues/tasks where ConsistentChat provides the most significant benefit.
*   **Potential Influence:** The ConsistentChat framework has the potential to influence the development of more sophisticated and realistic dialogue agents. The insights from the paper could guide future research on instruction data synthesis, multi-turn dialogue modeling, and evaluation metrics for conversational AI.
*   **Justification:** The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated approach to address a key challenge in multi-turn dialogue generation. While there are areas for further investigation and improvement, the ConsistentChat framework and the generated dataset offer a valuable resource for the research community. The significant performance gains demonstrated on multiple benchmarks solidify the paper's contribution.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The study effectively combines a theoretical understanding of the problem (context drift), a structured approach (intent trajectories and skeleton generation), and empirical validation (improvements on established benchmarks). The authors also provide detailed implementation details, which promote reproducibility. While acknowledging the LLM's role and potential biases is important, the novel approach to guiding the generation process is significant and the positive empirical results justify a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### MiMo-VL Technical Report
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03569v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaomi LLM-Core Team, :, Zihao Yue, Zhenru Lin, Yifan Song, Weikun Wang, Shuhuai Ren, Shuhao Gu, Shicheng Li, Peidian Li, Liang Zhao, Lei Li, Kainan Bao, Hao Tian, Hailin Zhang, Gang Wang, Dawei Zhu, Cici, Chenhong He, Bowen Ye, Bowen Shen, Zihan Zhang, Zihan Jiang, Zhixian Zheng, Zhichao Song, Zhenbo Luo, Yue Yu, Yudong Wang, Yuanyuan Tian, Yu Tu, Yihan Yan, Yi Huang, Xu Wang, Xinzhe Xu, Xingchen Song, Xing Zhang, Xing Yong, Xin Zhang, Xiangwei Deng, Wenyu Yang, Wenhan Ma, Weiwei Lv, Weiji Zhuang, Wei Liu, Sirui Deng, Shuo Liu, Shimao Chen, Shihua Yu, Shaohui Liu, Shande Wang, Rui Ma, Qiantong Wang, Peng Wang, Nuo Chen, Menghang Zhu, Kangyang Zhou, Kang Zhou, Kai Fang, Jun Shi, Jinhao Dong, Jiebao Xiao, Jiaming Xu, Huaqiu Liu, Hongshen Xu, Heng Qu, Haochen Zhao, Hanglong Lv, Guoan Wang, Duo Zhang, Dong Zhang, Di Zhang, Chong Ma, Chang Liu, Can Cai, Bingquan Xia
- **Abstract**: We open-source MiMo-VL-7B-SFT and MiMo-VL-7B-RL, two powerful vision-language models delivering state-of-the-art performance in both general visual understanding and multimodal reasoning. MiMo-VL-7B-RL outperforms Qwen2.5-VL-7B on 35 out of 40 evaluated tasks, and scores 59.4 on OlympiadBench, surpassing models with up to 78B parameters. For GUI grounding applications, it sets a new standard with 56.1 on OSWorld-G, even outperforming specialized models such as UI-TARS. Our training combines four-stage pre-training (2.4 trillion tokens) with Mixed On-policy Reinforcement Learning (MORL) integrating diverse reward signals. We identify the importance of incorporating high-quality reasoning data with long Chain-of-Thought into pre-training stages, and the benefits of mixed RL despite challenges in simultaneous multi-domain optimization. We also contribute a comprehensive evaluation suite covering 50+ tasks to promote reproducibility and advance the field. The model checkpoints and full evaluation suite are available at https://github.com/XiaomiMiMo/MiMo-VL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MiMo-VL Technical Report:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MiMo-VL-7B, a vision-language model (VLM) family comprising MiMo-VL-7B-SFT and MiMo-VL-7B-RL. The models are based on a native-resolution ViT encoder, an MLP projector, and a MiMo-7B language model.  The training involves a four-stage pre-training process (2.4T tokens) and a novel Mixed On-policy Reinforcement Learning (MORL) approach. The paper highlights the importance of high-quality reasoning data during pre-training and the benefits of MORL for improving model performance across various capabilities. Extensive experiments on 50+ tasks demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in general visual understanding, multimodal reasoning, and GUI grounding, even surpassing larger models and specialized systems. The authors release model checkpoints and a comprehensive evaluation suite.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strengths:** The MORL framework appears to be a novel contribution, integrating diverse reward signals (perception, grounding, reasoning, human preference) within an on-policy RL setting. The emphasis on high-quality, long Chain-of-Thought reasoning data during pre-training is also a valuable finding.
    *   **Weaknesses:** The core architecture (ViT encoder, MLP projector, LLM) is relatively standard. The novelty primarily resides in the training methodology and data curation strategies, rather than architectural innovations. While using native resolution ViT is notable, it doesn't represent a fundamental architectural breakthrough.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** Achieving state-of-the-art results with a 7B model is significant, particularly in challenging tasks like OlympiadBench and OSWorld-G.  The comprehensive evaluation suite (50+ tasks) is a valuable resource for the community, promoting reproducibility and standardized comparisons. The improved performance in GUI grounding has high practical importance for agentic applications. The Elo ratings demonstrate a high degree of user preference.
    *   **Weaknesses:** While impressive, the performance gains need to be contextualized. The paper compares against open-source models of similar scale, but the gap with larger proprietary models may still be substantial.  The MORL approach, while promising, appears to suffer from task interference, suggesting limitations in scaling to a truly general-purpose agent. The qualitative results and case studies, while interesting, are anecdotal and don't provide a rigorous quantitative assessment of the model's capabilities in real-world scenarios. The improvement stems mostly from training on large datasets with careful data curation, so the significance of the architectural design is not clear.

*   **Justification:**

    The paper's core strength is demonstrating that a relatively small VLM can achieve impressive performance through carefully designed training methods and data curation. The emphasis on reasoning data and MORL provides valuable insights into improving model capabilities. However, the paper relies on standard architectural components. Task interference in MORL and the gap to larger proprietary models suggest areas for future research.

Score: 7

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The MiMo-VL-7B model demonstrates significant engineering effort and achieves compelling results, particularly in specialized tasks. The key insights are the effect of incorporating strong reasoning data into pre-training and applying Mixed On-policy Reinforcement Learning. However, it is not a groundbreaking innovation in architectural design, and there is still space for improvement in creating more robust frameworks that mitigate the task interference in MORL and close the gap with larger models. The thorough and released evaluation suite has the potential to positively influence the research community. The model demonstrates notable achievements and insights that benefit the community, but with limitations in innovation. This places it above average but stops it from achieving truly exceptional status.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### FreePRM: Training Process Reward Models Without Ground Truth Process Labels
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03570v1)
- **Authors**: Lin Sun, Chuang Liu, Xiaofeng Ma, Tao Yang, Weijia Lu, Ning Wu
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated that Process Reward Models (PRMs) play a crucial role in enhancing model performance. However, training PRMs typically requires step-level labels, either manually annotated or automatically generated, which can be costly and difficult to obtain at scale. To address this challenge, we introduce FreePRM, a weakly supervised framework for training PRMs without access to ground-truth step-level labels. FreePRM first generates pseudo step-level labels based on the correctness of final outcome, and then employs Buffer Probability to eliminate impact of noise inherent in pseudo labeling. Experimental results show that FreePRM achieves an average F1 score of 53.0% on ProcessBench, outperforming fully supervised PRM trained on Math-Shepherd by +24.1%. Compared to other open-source PRMs, FreePRM outperforms upon RLHFlow-PRM-Mistral-8B (28.4%) by +24.6%, EurusPRM (31.3%) by +21.7%, and Skywork-PRM-7B (42.1%) by +10.9%. This work introduces a new paradigm in PRM training, significantly reducing reliance on costly step-level annotations while maintaining strong performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FreePRM: Training Process Reward Models Without Ground Truth Process Labels."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FreePRM, a novel weakly supervised framework for training Process Reward Models (PRMs) without requiring costly step-level labels.  Instead of relying on manually annotated or automatically generated step-level labels, FreePRM generates pseudo step-level labels based on the correctness of the final outcome. It then incorporates a "buffer probability" mechanism to mitigate the impact of noise inherent in these pseudo labels. The method aims to represent a neutral state for uncertain steps and absorb the inaccuracies in label assignments.  Experimental results on ProcessBench and mathematical reasoning tasks demonstrate that FreePRM achieves competitive or even superior performance compared to PRMs trained with full or automatically-generated labels, offering a more scalable and practical training approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the weak supervision approach that leverages the final outcome and buffer probability. While pseudo-labeling isn't entirely new, its application to PRM training, coupled with the buffer probability to handle noise in the pseudo labels, represents a novel combination of techniques. The idea of a "buffer" state to account for uncertainty in step-wise correctness is a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from addressing a key bottleneck in PRM training: the difficulty and cost of obtaining accurate step-level labels. By developing a method that significantly reduces the reliance on such labels, FreePRM could make PRMs more accessible and applicable to a broader range of tasks.  The improved performance compared to some supervised PRMs further underscores the potential of the approach. The empirical results on ProcessBench are compelling and provide solid evidence of the method's effectiveness. The fact that FreePRM shows performance comparable or even better than some existing PRMs trained with explicit step-level labels is a significant result and suggests that the proposed approach can effectively learn from weaker supervision.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and motivation.
    *   Well-defined methodology with a clear explanation of the buffer probability mechanism.
    *   Strong empirical results on a challenging benchmark.
    *   Comparison to relevant baselines.
    *   Ablation studies to demonstrate the importance of different components.
    *   Theoretical analysis that supports the robustness and stability of FreePRM.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper compares against several baselines, it could benefit from comparisons with other weak supervision techniques applied to similar tasks, if any exist.
    *   The performance gap with the fully supervised PRM800K model (though admitted as small) could be addressed in the future by combining FreePRM with automated data annotation techniques to further improve performance.
    *   The societal impact discussion is relatively brief. More in-depth consideration of potential biases introduced by using outcome correctness as a proxy for step correctness would be beneficial.
    *   Some of the examples in Appendixes are trivial and not necessary.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work has the potential to significantly influence the field of PRM training by providing a more scalable and cost-effective alternative to traditional methods. The weak supervision approach could inspire further research into developing similar techniques for other areas of machine learning where labeled data is scarce.

**Rigorous Rationale:**
The score reflects the paper's strong contributions to the field. While pseudo-labeling is not a completely new concept, the authors present a novel and effective way to apply it to PRM training, overcoming a significant bottleneck. The introduction of the buffer probability further enhances the method's robustness. The experimental results clearly demonstrate the method's superiority to several existing baselines, including some trained with full supervision. The limitations are clearly addressed, and the paper is well-written and easy to follow. Overall, this work provides a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to significantly impact future research in PRM training.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Exchange of Perspective Prompting Enhances Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03573v1)
- **Authors**: Lin Sun, Can Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have made significant advancements in addressing diverse natural language processing (NLP) tasks. However, their performance is often limited by inherent comprehension of problems. To address this limitation, we propose Exchange-of-Perspective (EoP), a novel framework designed to exchange perspectives across different definitions of problem, so that it can break the fixed mindset from any particular formulation of the question. We conducted extensive and comprehensive experiments on 8 benchmarks. The results show that EoP can significantly improve performance. For instance, compared to the non-commutative baseline PHP, with GPT-3.5-Turbo and EoP, we observe a 3.6% improvement on AQuA (60.6% to 64.2%), while GPT-4-powered EoP demonstrates a 7.7% overall accuracy enhancement on Math (53.9% to 61.6%) and a 3.5% improvement on OlympiadBench Maths (43.5% to 47.0%) when using Qwen-2.5-72b.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Exchange-of-Perspective (EoP), a novel prompting framework designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  Instead of focusing on refining the *reasoning process* (as with CoT or self-correction), EoP emphasizes enhancing the *understanding of the question* itself.  It achieves this by: 1) Redefining the original question to create an "augmented" version, generating two parallel branches (original and augmented); 2) Having the LLM generate initial answers for both branches; 3) Iteratively exchanging answers between the branches using a "Hint" mechanism, thus providing external perspectives; and 4) Repeating the answer exchange until a termination condition is met (consensus or stability). The paper presents extensive experiments on various arithmetic and mathematical reasoning datasets, showing that EoP outperforms several established prompting baselines.  Ablation studies explore the effectiveness of different redefinition strategies and demonstrate that the performance gains come from error correction and complementary information gained from the perspective exchange, not simply rephrasing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a clearly novel and well-articulated approach to improving LLM reasoning. The idea of focusing on enhancing question understanding *before* reasoning, and specifically through *perspective exchange*, is a worthwhile contribution. While self-correction and re-prompting are existing techniques, EoP distinguishes itself by its structured approach to generating alternative perspectives through question redefinition and iterative answer exchange. The redefinition methods (PEC and QR), while not entirely groundbreaking on their own, are effectively integrated into the EoP framework.

**Significance:**  The experimental results indicate a significant improvement over strong baselines, particularly on more complex tasks like AQUA and Math. The ablation studies offer insight into why EoP works, suggesting that the perspective exchange allows the model to correct errors and leverage complementary information.  These insights are valuable and could guide future research on improving LLM reasoning. The method is model-agnostic and relatively simple to implement, increasing its potential for adoption. However, the increase in the average number of interactions required to obtain a final answer from the LLM might affect the inference costs of EoP compared to simpler approaches.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Approach:**  EoP provides a fresh perspective on improving LLM reasoning by focusing on question understanding rather than just reasoning refinement.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The paper includes a robust set of experiments on diverse datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of EoP against strong baselines.
*   **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the underlying mechanisms of EoP and the importance of its components.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the EoP framework and the experimental setup.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Redefinition Strategies:** The redefinition strategies (PEC and QR), while effective, are relatively simple.  More sophisticated methods for generating alternative perspectives could further enhance EoP's performance.  It also limits the potential of the "external perspective" to the model's own internal generation capabilities, and potentially neglects true external knowledge bases or alternative problem solving approaches outside the model's scope.
*   **Computational Cost:** The iterative nature of EoP could increase the computational cost compared to simpler prompting techniques. The paper could benefit from a more explicit discussion of this trade-off, and potential methods for mitigating it. The evaluation of the computational performance is missing.
*   **Generalizability Limitations:** The datasets are limited to mathematical reasoning. The effectiveness of EoP on other reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, logical reasoning) should be explored to increase the applicability and generality of the approach.
*   **Error Analysis:** The error analysis that investigates in which specific scenario EoP fails, would improve the study and lead to further research directions.

**Potential Influence:**

EoP has the potential to influence the field by shifting the focus from solely refining reasoning processes to also enhancing the *understanding of the input question itself*. The EoP framework provides a concrete approach for achieving this, and the insights from the ablation studies could guide future research on developing more effective prompting strategies. This is likely to stimulate further research into perspective taking within language models, potentially leading to more robust and reliable reasoning systems.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-validated approach to improve LLM reasoning, offering a fresh perspective on the problem. The clear experimental results and insightful ablation studies demonstrate the potential of the method. Although the redefinition strategies are relatively simple and computational cost requires further consideration, the overall contribution is significant. The current implementation limits the external perspective taking to the model's own capabilities but offers directions for further work.

### KG-BiLM: Knowledge Graph Embedding via Bidirectional Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03576v1)
- **Authors**: Zirui Chen, Xin Wang, Zhao Li, Wenbin Guo, Dongxiao He
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in knowledge representation learning (KRL) highlight the urgent necessity to unify symbolic knowledge graphs (KGs) with language models (LMs) for richer semantic understanding. However, existing approaches typically prioritize either graph structure or textual semantics, leaving a gap: a unified framework that simultaneously captures global KG connectivity, nuanced linguistic context, and discriminative reasoning semantics. To bridge this gap, we introduce KG-BiLM, a bidirectional LM framework that fuses structural cues from KGs with the semantic expressiveness of generative transformers. KG-BiLM incorporates three key components: (i) Bidirectional Knowledge Attention, which removes the causal mask to enable full interaction among all tokens and entities; (ii) Knowledge-Masked Prediction, which encourages the model to leverage both local semantic contexts and global graph connectivity; and (iii) Contrastive Graph Semantic Aggregation, which preserves KG structure via contrastive alignment of sampled sub-graph representations. Extensive experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate that KG-BiLM outperforms strong baselines in link prediction, especially on large-scale graphs with complex multi-hop relations - validating its effectiveness in unifying structural information and textual semantics.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "KG-BiLM: Knowledge Graph Embedding via Bidirectional Language Models."

**Summary:**
The paper introduces KG-BiLM, a bidirectional language model framework designed to unify symbolic knowledge graphs (KGs) with language models (LMs) for enhanced semantic understanding in knowledge representation learning (KRL). The framework aims to capture global KG connectivity, nuanced linguistic context, and discriminative reasoning semantics simultaneously. KG-BiLM achieves this by integrating three key components: (1) Bidirectional Knowledge Attention, which enables full interaction between tokens and entities by removing the causal mask in decoders; (2) Knowledge-Masked Prediction, which encourages the model to leverage both local semantic contexts and global graph connectivity; and (3) Contrastive Graph Semantic Aggregation, which preserves KG structure via contrastive alignment of sampled sub-graph representations.  Experimental results on standard benchmarks demonstrate that KG-BiLM outperforms existing methods, especially on large-scale graphs with complex multi-hop relations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a novel architecture, KG-BiLM, that combines bidirectional language modeling with knowledge graph embeddings.  The core novelty lies in its attempt to overcome limitations of previous approaches that tend to prioritize either graph structure or textual semantics but struggle to effectively fuse both. The three key components – Bidirectional Knowledge Attention, Knowledge-Masked Prediction, and Contrastive Graph Semantic Aggregation – represent innovative contributions.  The Bidirectional Knowledge Attention is particularly compelling as it re-imagines the traditionally causal decoder approach to enable richer, context-aware interactions between tokens and entities. Knowledge Masking ensures the model is not overly reliant on one source of information and combines contextual inferences with structured graph understanding. Finally the contrastive view aggregation ensures clusters in the embedding space capture relational similarity by contrasting different perspectives.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a significant challenge in the KRL field: effectively integrating structural and textual information for comprehensive semantic understanding. The proposed KG-BiLM framework demonstrates promising results on standard benchmarks, outperforming strong baselines in link prediction tasks, particularly on large-scale graphs with complex relationships. The improved performance suggests that KG-BiLM successfully captures and utilizes both structural and textual semantics, making it a valuable contribution to the field.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition and motivation:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing KRL approaches and motivates the need for a unified framework that integrates structural and textual information.
*   **Novel architecture:** The KG-BiLM architecture presents a novel approach to knowledge graph embedding by combining bidirectional language modeling with knowledge graph structure.
*   **Effective integration of components:** The three key components of KG-BiLM (Bidirectional Knowledge Attention, Knowledge-Masked Prediction, and Contrastive Graph Semantic Aggregation) are well-designed and effectively integrated to capture both structural and textual semantics.
*   **Strong experimental results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results on standard benchmarks, demonstrating that KG-BiLM outperforms existing methods in link prediction tasks.
*   **Ablation studies:** Ablation studies are undertaken to evaluate the importance of the architecture design and evaluate the impact of the individual components.
*   **Zero-shot capability.** Shows the ability to do zero-shot inference on the proposed architecture.
*   **Qualitative Analysis.** Showing the attention mechanism works as claimed, and visualising the embedding space.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:** The KG-BiLM architecture is relatively complex, potentially making it challenging to implement and scale to even larger knowledge graphs.
*   **Computational cost:**  Bidirectional attention, while powerful, is computationally expensive. The paper doesn't deeply analyze the computational cost compared to more efficient methods.
*   **Reliance on textual descriptions:** KG-BiLM still requires textual descriptions for the entities. It is less impactful when such signals are completely absent as shown by the results on FB15k-237 (no text descriptions). While it improves the model it does not outperform the SOTA.
*   **Limited Evaluation on other KRL tasks:** The paper mainly focuses on link prediction. Evaluating KG-BiLM on other KRL tasks, such as entity typing or relation extraction, could further demonstrate its versatility.
*   **Lack of statistical significance:** The paper claims state-of-the-art results for their framework, but lacks statistical significance on their results.

**Justification for Score:**

KG-BiLM presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of knowledge representation learning. It offers an innovative way to integrate structural and textual information, addressing a major limitation in existing methods. The strong experimental results on challenging benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed framework. However, the complexity of the architecture, the potential computational cost, and the reliance on textual descriptions limit its applicability in certain scenarios.  Also the lack of statistical significance is a detriment to claiming SOTA performance. Overall, given the advancements in combining text and KG, while the paper is innovative and promising, the lack of significance testing limits the impact of the contribution.
