However, the specific application of this technique to *event-based vision*, especially with the controlled injection of noise tailored to event camera characteristics (approximating a Poisson process), is a valuable contribution. The paper systematically explores different noise levels and their impact on various network architectures in the context of event data, which hadn't been explored before. The comparison with traditional filtering methods is also a strong point, highlighting the limitations of purely denoising approaches that might sacrifice true event data. This specific application within the event-based domain represents a notable advance.

*   **Significance:** Event cameras are becoming increasingly important in applications with high dynamic range or fast motion, where traditional cameras struggle. Addressing the inherent noise sensitivity of these sensors is critical to deploying robust vision systems. By providing a training methodology that improves robustness, this paper directly contributes to making event-based vision more practical and reliable in real-world scenarios. The performance gains and the stability achieved across various noise conditions and network architectures are significant. The code release also contributes to the re-usability and impact of the work.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the issue of noise in event-based vision and the limitations of existing filtering methods.
    *   **Novel Methodology:** The noise-injection training method is well-motivated and presented.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental design is thorough, covering multiple datasets, network architectures, and noise levels. The inclusion of several noise filtering methods in the comparative analysis is a major strength.
    *   **Qualitative Analysis:** The Grad-CAM visualizations provide insights into how noise injection affects feature learning.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The release of code increases the impact and adoption potential.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Exploration of Noise Types:** The paper focuses primarily on synthetic shot noise modeled as a Poisson process. While this is a common and relevant noise source, other types of noise (e.g., related to sensor temperature or specific hardware imperfections) could be considered in future work.
    *   **Performance Trade-offs:**  While the method enhances noise robustness, a slight accuracy decrease is observed under ideal (noise-free) conditions. A deeper analysis of this trade-off and potential mitigation strategies would be beneficial.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** Although addressed in supplementary, The optimal level of injected noise could be dataset and network specific. Investigating methods to automatically adjust this parameter would be a valuable extension. The use of a limited range of neural network architectures might also be a consideration. Testing with different architecture parameter configurations might also be useful.
*   **Real Noise Validation (supplementary):** This paper introduces the validation of the models in real noise conditions in supplementary material which is not ideal, it could be better to include it in the main paper.

*   **Scope:** The evaluation focuses primarily on object classification. While important, exploring the effectiveness of noise-injection training in other event-based vision tasks (e.g., object detection, tracking, scene reconstruction) would broaden the impact of the work. The object detection results included in supplementary materials is a good starting point, but a more expanded discussion of its benefits in this area could be highlighted.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a solid, well-executed contribution to the field of event-based vision.  The application of noise injection to this domain, the systematic evaluation across different architectures and datasets, and the comparison with traditional filtering methods all contribute to its value. While the core idea of noise injection isn't entirely novel, the *specific* adaptation and demonstrated impact on event data processing justify a high rating. The weaknesses are areas for future research and don't detract significantly from the overall contribution. The inclusion of supplementary material about the validation in real-noise datasets should ideally be in the main body of the article. Because this is only included in supplementary material, it causes some decrease in score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### HSSBench: Benchmarking Humanities and Social Sciences Ability for Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03922v1)
- **Authors**: Zhaolu Kang, Junhao Gong, Jiaxu Yan, Wanke Xia, Yian Wang, Ziwen Wang, Huaxuan Ding, Zhuo Cheng, Wenhao Cao, Zhiyuan Feng, Siqi He, Shannan Yan, Junzhe Chen, Xiaomin He, Chaoya Jiang, Wei Ye, Kaidong Yu, Xuelong Li
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant potential to advance a broad range of domains. However, current benchmarks for evaluating MLLMs primarily emphasize general knowledge and vertical step-by-step reasoning typical of STEM disciplines, while overlooking the distinct needs and potential of the Humanities and Social Sciences (HSS). Tasks in the HSS domain require more horizontal, interdisciplinary thinking and a deep integration of knowledge across related fields, which presents unique challenges for MLLMs, particularly in linking abstract concepts with corresponding visual representations. Addressing this gap, we present HSSBench, a dedicated benchmark designed to assess the capabilities of MLLMs on HSS tasks in multiple languages, including the six official languages of the United Nations. We also introduce a novel data generation pipeline tailored for HSS scenarios, in which multiple domain experts and automated agents collaborate to generate and iteratively refine each sample. HSSBench contains over 13,000 meticulously designed samples, covering six key categories. We benchmark more than 20 mainstream MLLMs on HSSBench and demonstrate that it poses significant challenges even for state-of-the-art models. We hope that this benchmark will inspire further research into enhancing the cross-disciplinary reasoning abilities of MLLMs, especially their capacity to internalize and connect knowledge across fields.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper "HSSBench: Benchmarking Humanities and Social Sciences Ability for Multimodal Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HSSBench, a new benchmark specifically designed to evaluate the performance of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) on tasks related to the Humanities and Social Sciences (HSS). The benchmark comprises over 13,000 samples across six categories and 45 sub-types, collected in the six official languages of the United Nations. The authors also detail a multi-agent data generation pipeline involving both domain experts and automated agents to create high-quality, diverse datasets. They benchmarked over 20 mainstream MLLMs on HSSBench, finding that existing models struggle significantly with the tasks, highlighting the need for improved cross-disciplinary reasoning abilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper is novel in its focus on the HSS domains, which are largely overlooked by current MLLM benchmarks that are more oriented towards STEM fields. The paper identifies a crucial gap in current MLLM evaluation, as well as the need for cross-disciplinary reasoning and the integration of abstract concepts with visual representations within HSS tasks.

*   **Significance:** The creation of HSSBench fills a significant gap by offering a standardized, multi-lingual resource for assessing MLLMs in areas vital to a comprehensive understanding of intelligence. By highlighting MLLMs' struggles with HSS tasks, the authors emphasize the importance of developing models with more horizontal, interdisciplinary reasoning skills, as opposed to vertical, step-by-step STEM reasoning.
    The dataset's multi-lingual nature is a strength, but the paper should address potential data biases or the impact of translation on task difficulty.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Targeted Benchmark:** The HSSBench is specifically designed to capture the nuances and complexities of HSS tasks, which differ substantially from STEM tasks.
    *   **Multi-lingual Data:** The inclusion of six languages broadens the scope and accessibility of the benchmark.
    *   **Careful Data Generation:** The authors implemented a novel data generation pipeline that is tailored for HSS scenarios.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** Benchmarking over 20 MLLMs offers a comprehensive view of the current state of the art.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper persuasively articulates the limitations of current MLLMs regarding cross-disciplinary reasoning.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Potential Data Bias:** While the data generation pipeline involves domain experts, it is not clear how representative the selected experts are of their respective fields, or how cultural biases might be addressed in both the data and the questions. Furthermore, the translation process might also affect the quality and relevance of knowledge representation.
    *   **Task Design Complexity:** The evaluation focuses heavily on visual question answering (VQA). However, it has VQA-specific failure modes that may confound the results. The paper mentions a focus on horizontal reasoning, but it does not describe how the VQA data structure facilitates horizontal reasoning (compared to traditional VQA that asks questions about the primary objects).
    *   **Limited Analysis of Failure Modes:** Although the paper benchmarked several models, the analysis of failure cases is high-level. A more in-depth error analysis could provide better insights on the models' weaknesses and areas for improvement.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Existing HSS Benchmarks:** The paper does not mention any related benchmarks or efforts to evaluate large models on the humanities and social sciences.

*   **Potential Influence:** The HSSBench has the potential to encourage further research into improving the cross-disciplinary reasoning abilities of MLLMs, and serves as a solid basis for future studies in this field. The benchmark's comprehensive design and multi-lingual approach provide a strong foundation for future development. The work can potentially influence the development of MLLMs toward more comprehensive and humanistic intelligence.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a solid contribution by creating HSSBench and highlighting the need for more specialized evaluation of MLLMs in the Humanities and Social Sciences. The dataset is large, thoughtfully designed, and thoroughly evaluated. The novelty lies in the focus on HSS, and its significance rests on filling a vital gap in MLLM evaluation. However, the work could be improved by addressing potential biases in data and analysis, including a more in-depth exploration of MLLM error modes and a lack of related benchmarks and the evaluation metrics focus. In addition, the evaluation metrics emphasize a singular correct answer; horizontal reasoning requires a variety of appropriate answers.

Therefore, the assigned score of 7 reflects the positive aspects of its approach, but it also acknowledges these points.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### More or Less Wrong: A Benchmark for Directional Bias in LLM Comparative Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03923v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammadamin Shafiei, Hamidreza Saffari, Nafise Sadat Moosavi
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are known to be sensitive to input phrasing, but the mechanisms by which semantic cues shape reasoning remain poorly understood. We investigate this phenomenon in the context of comparative math problems with objective ground truth, revealing a consistent and directional framing bias: logically equivalent questions containing the words ``more'', ``less'', or ``equal'' systematically steer predictions in the direction of the framing term. To study this effect, we introduce MathComp, a controlled benchmark of 300 comparison scenarios, each evaluated under 14 prompt variants across three LLM families. We find that model errors frequently reflect linguistic steering, systematic shifts toward the comparative term present in the prompt. Chain-of-thought prompting reduces these biases, but its effectiveness varies: free-form reasoning is more robust, while structured formats may preserve or reintroduce directional drift. Finally, we show that including demographic identity terms (e.g., ``a woman'', ``a Black person'') in input scenarios amplifies directional drift, despite identical underlying quantities, highlighting the interplay between semantic framing and social referents. These findings expose critical blind spots in standard evaluation and motivate framing-aware benchmarks for diagnosing reasoning robustness and fairness in LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MathComp, a novel benchmark to investigate directional biases in large language models (LLMs) when performing comparative reasoning tasks involving math word problems. The authors show that LLMs exhibit a systematic bias towards the framing terms used in prompts (e.g., "more," "less," or "equal").  Even logically equivalent questions framed differently can elicit different answers, steering predictions in the direction of the framing. They also investigate how this framing bias interacts with demographic identity terms, finding that the inclusion of terms like "a woman" or "a Black person" can amplify the bias.  Finally, they explore mitigation strategies such as chain-of-thought prompting and structured outputs, finding that while these techniques offer some reduction in bias, they don't eliminate it entirely. The paper emphasizes the importance of framing-aware benchmarks to evaluate reasoning robustness and fairness in LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in isolating and quantifying the effect of semantic framing on LLM reasoning, specifically in a controlled, objective mathematical context. Prior work has explored prompt sensitivity and demographic bias in LLMs, but this paper uniquely focuses on the directional nature of errors introduced by comparative phrasing and its interaction with social cues. The creation of the MathComp benchmark itself is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for how we evaluate and trust LLMs. The paper demonstrates that standard accuracy metrics can obscure systematic reasoning biases that arise from subtle linguistic variations. This suggests that existing benchmarks may overestimate the true capabilities of LLMs and that we need more sophisticated evaluation methods that account for framing effects. The fact that demographic cues can amplify these biases raises concerns about fairness and the potential for LLMs to perpetuate harmful stereotypes.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Controlled Experimentation:** The design of the MathComp benchmark is well-suited for isolating framing effects, allowing for precise measurement of directional errors.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The authors evaluate a range of LLMs, prompt variations, and output modes, providing a thorough investigation of the phenomenon.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper identifies specific biases that can affect LLM reasoning and suggests directions for mitigation, such as framing-aware prompting.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Releasing the dataset and code allows for future research and validation of the findings.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of MathComp:** While controlled, the MathComp benchmark is limited to simple comparative math problems. The extent to which these findings generalize to more complex, real-world reasoning tasks remains to be seen.
    *   **Binary Gender Representation:** The use of only binary gender categories is a limitation, as acknowledged by the authors.
    *   **Mitigation Strategies:** Although the paper investigates mitigation strategies, they only partially reduce the framing-induced errors. Further research is needed to develop more effective methods for mitigating these biases.

* **Justification for Score:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting a previously underappreciated limitation in how LLMs reason. The methodical construction of the MathComp benchmark and comprehensive analysis across LLMs, prompt types and demographic attributes demonstrates that semantic phrasing systematically biases model outputs and reveals the potential for the magnification of societal stereotypes. Though the tasks examined are relatively simple, they still surface a substantial problem that exists to some degree across all language models and evaluation methods. 
In short, the paper demonstrates the need to rethink how language models are evaluated and to pay greater attention to potentially harmful biases arising from subtle framing effects.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### VisCoder: Fine-Tuning LLMs for Executable Python Visualization Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03930v1)
- **Authors**: Yuansheng Ni, Ping Nie, Kai Zou, Xiang Yue, Wenhu Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) often struggle with visualization tasks like plotting diagrams, charts, where success depends on both code correctness and visual semantics. Existing instruction-tuning datasets lack execution-grounded supervision and offer limited support for iterative code correction, resulting in fragile and unreliable plot generation. We present VisCode-200K, a large-scale instruction tuning dataset for Python-based visualization and self-correction. It contains over 200K examples from two sources: (1) validated plotting code from open-source repositories, paired with natural language instructions and rendered plots; and (2) 45K multi-turn correction dialogues from Code-Feedback, enabling models to revise faulty code using runtime feedback. We fine-tune Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct on VisCode-200K to create VisCoder, and evaluate it on PandasPlotBench. VisCoder significantly outperforms strong open-source baselines and approaches the performance of proprietary models like GPT-4o-mini. We further adopt a self-debug evaluation protocol to assess iterative repair, demonstrating the benefits of feedback-driven learning for executable, visually accurate code generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VisCode-200K, a large-scale instruction tuning dataset designed for executable Python visualization code generation.  The dataset addresses limitations in existing datasets by including both validated plotting code from open-source repositories paired with natural language instructions and rendered plots, and multi-turn correction dialogues derived from Code-Feedback for iterative code refinement. The authors fine-tune Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct on VisCode-200K, creating VisCoder, and evaluate its performance on PandasPlotBench, demonstrating improvements over open-source baselines and approaching the performance of proprietary models like GPT-4o-mini, especially with a self-debug evaluation protocol. The paper highlights the benefits of feedback-driven learning for generating visually accurate and executable code.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Dataset Contribution:** The VisCode-200K dataset appears to be a substantial and valuable resource.  Its scale (200K examples), the inclusion of both single-shot generation data and multi-turn correction dialogues, and the focus on executable visualization code are significant. Datasets in this domain are crucial for advancing research.
*   **Addressing a Gap:** The paper correctly identifies a significant weakness in existing LLM applications to visualization, namely the fragility and unreliability of plot generation due to lack of execution grounding and limited iterative correction.  The dataset directly addresses this gap.
*   **Empirical Validation:** The authors perform a thorough empirical evaluation, comparing VisCoder to strong baselines (including proprietary models). The self-debug evaluation mode provides a realistic and informative assessment of the models' ability to recover from errors. The error analysis also sheds light on failure modes.
*   **Reproducibility:** The open-source release of VisCoder makes the work reproducible and fosters further research in the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Innovation in Model Architecture/Training:** While the paper presents a valuable dataset and effective fine-tuning, it doesn't introduce significant novelties in model architecture or training techniques *beyond* the data itself.  The primary contribution is in the data, not the modeling approach. It essentially uses existing fine-tuning methods on the developed dataset.
*   **Dependency on Existing LLMs:** The VisCoder model is built upon the Qwen2.5-Coder-Instruct model. This limits the model's overall potential as it depends on the abilities of the base model.
*   **Plotly Challenges Still Exist:** The paper acknowledges that Plotly still poses a significant challenge, suggesting that even with VisCode-200K, certain complexities in visualization libraries remain difficult for current LLMs to handle. This indicates there are limitations in the current approach.
*   **Evaluation Metric Dependency:**  The evaluation using GPT-4o-judged scores, while common, introduces potential bias.  An independent evaluation, although complex, would increase confidence in the results. It relies on the judgment of another LLM.
*   **Limited Limitation Analysis:** While some limitations are stated, a deeper investigation into the cases where VisCoder still fails, even with self-debugging, could provide more insight and drive future research.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The primary novelty lies in the creation and curation of the VisCode-200K dataset. While fine-tuning is a well-established technique, the specific application to visualization code generation with a focus on executability and iterative correction is significant. The dataset itself has the potential to become a standard benchmark and training resource in the field. Its influence will hinge on its adoption by the broader research community. The performance gains observed on PandasPlotBench compared to open-source baselines supports the significance of this contribution.

The introduction of a 'self-debug' setting for evaluation provides a more thorough and realistic evaluation of the model's abilities to generate plotting code, compared to using existing validation metrics.

The work's significance is in enabling more reliable and robust visualization code generation. This can have a downstream impact on data analysis workflows, making it easier for researchers and practitioners to create accurate and informative plots directly from language descriptions.

**Justification for Score:**

The score assigned reflects the significant contribution of the VisCode-200K dataset, coupled with a solid empirical validation and an acknowledgment of existing limitations. While the modeling approach itself isn't radically innovative, the data and its impact on model performance are substantial. The score is tempered by the dependency on existing LLMs, limited innovation in model architecture/training, and some evaluation metric dependencies.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### DiffCAP: Diffusion-based Cumulative Adversarial Purification for Vision Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03933v1)
- **Authors**: Jia Fu, Yongtao Wu, Yihang Chen, Kunyu Peng, Xiao Zhang, Volkan Cevher, Sepideh Pashami, Anders Holst
- **Abstract**: Vision Language Models (VLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities in multimodal understanding, yet their susceptibility to perturbations poses a significant threat to their reliability in real-world applications. Despite often being imperceptible to humans, these perturbations can drastically alter model outputs, leading to erroneous interpretations and decisions. This paper introduces DiffCAP, a novel diffusion-based purification strategy that can effectively neutralize adversarial corruptions in VLMs. We observe that adding minimal noise to an adversarially corrupted image significantly alters its latent embedding with respect to VLMs. Building on this insight, DiffCAP cumulatively injects random Gaussian noise into adversarially perturbed input data. This process continues until the embeddings of two consecutive noisy images reach a predefined similarity threshold, indicating a potential approach to neutralize the adversarial effect. Subsequently, a pretrained diffusion model is employed to denoise the stabilized image, recovering a clean representation suitable for the VLMs to produce an output. Through extensive experiments across six datasets with three VLMs under varying attack strengths in three task scenarios, we show that DiffCAP consistently outperforms existing defense techniques by a substantial margin. Notably, DiffCAP significantly reduces both hyperparameter tuning complexity and the required diffusion time, thereby accelerating the denoising process. Equipped with strong theoretical and empirical support, DiffCAP provides a robust and practical solution for securely deploying VLMs in adversarial environments.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffCAP, a novel diffusion-based adversarial purification strategy for Vision Language Models (VLMs).  DiffCAP aims to neutralize adversarial corruptions by cumulatively adding Gaussian noise to adversarially perturbed input images until the embeddings of consecutive noisy images reach a similarity threshold.  A pre-trained diffusion model is then used to denoise the stabilized image, recovering a clean representation for VLM inference. Through extensive experiments across various datasets, VLMs, and attack strengths, DiffCAP outperforms existing defense techniques while significantly reducing hyperparameter tuning complexity and diffusion time. The paper also provides theoretical support for the proposed approach, demonstrating semantic stability in the embedding space during the forward diffusion process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in the specific method of using diffusion models for adversarial purification in VLMs. While diffusion models have been applied to adversarial defense before (e.g., DiffPure), the core contribution of DiffCAP is the cumulative injection of Gaussian noise with a dynamic stopping criterion based on embedding similarity.  This adaptive approach to diffusion time allocation, designed to minimize computational overhead, distinguishes it from prior works that use fixed diffusion times.  The paper also presents a theoretical analysis establishing a recovery region and quantifying embedding convergence during forward diffusion, adding to the understanding of the underlying process.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in VLM research: the vulnerability to adversarial perturbations.  The demonstrated improvements over existing defenses in robustness, efficiency, and reduced hyperparameter tuning offer a practical solution for deploying VLMs in adversarial environments. The comprehensive experiments across multiple datasets, models, and tasks enhance the credibility of the results. The theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation for understanding the method's effectiveness. The reported benefits of reduced diffusion time are particularly significant given the computational demands of diffusion models, potentially widening the applicability of such defenses. However, the paper primarily concentrates on image-based attacks, and the limitations in handling text-based or joint vision-text adversarial threats are acknowledged. Furthermore, while showing resilience against adaptive attacks like BPDA and EOT, the study could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the method's robustness to sophisticated adaptive attacks tailored specifically to exploit potential weaknesses in DiffCAP. The broader impact section acknowledges these aspects.

**Strengths:**

*   **Effective Defense:** Demonstrably outperforms existing defenses in a variety of settings.
*   **Improved Efficiency:** Reduces diffusion time and hyperparameter tuning complexity.
*   **Theoretical Analysis:** Provides a theoretical foundation for the method's effectiveness.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Extensive experiments across datasets, models, and tasks.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Attacks:** Primarily focuses on image-based adversarial perturbations.
*   **Potential Vulnerability to Sophisticated Adaptive Attacks:** While demonstrating improvements over existing defences, a deeper investigation of the method's robustness against adaptive attacks tailored explicitly to exploit potential weaknesses in DiffCAP could further strengthen the claims.
*   **Reliance on Image-Based Diffusion Models:** Limits applicability to non-image modalities.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a significant advancement in adversarial defense for VLMs by proposing DiffCAP, an efficient and robust method backed by theoretical analysis and comprehensive experimental results.  The dynamic diffusion time allocation, guided by embedding similarity, is a valuable contribution. However, the limited scope of attacks considered (image-only) and a potential vulnerability to sophisticated adaptive attacks prevents a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Graph Counselor: Adaptive Graph Exploration via Multi-Agent Synergy to Enhance LLM Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03939v1)
- **Authors**: Junqi Gao, Xiang Zou, YIng Ai, Dong Li, Yichen Niu, Biqing Qi, Jianxing Liu
- **Abstract**: Graph Retrieval Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) effectively enhances external knowledge integration capabilities by explicitly modeling knowledge relationships, thereby improving the factual accuracy and generation quality of Large Language Models (LLMs) in specialized domains. However, existing methods suffer from two inherent limitations: 1) Inefficient Information Aggregation: They rely on a single agent and fixed iterative patterns, making it difficult to adaptively capture multi-level textual, structural, and degree information within graph data. 2) Rigid Reasoning Mechanism: They employ preset reasoning schemes, which cannot dynamically adjust reasoning depth nor achieve precise semantic correction. To overcome these limitations, we propose Graph Counselor, an GraphRAG method based on multi-agent collaboration. This method uses the Adaptive Graph Information Extraction Module (AGIEM), where Planning, Thought, and Execution Agents work together to precisely model complex graph structures and dynamically adjust information extraction strategies, addressing the challenges of multi-level dependency modeling and adaptive reasoning depth. Additionally, the Self-Reflection with Multiple Perspectives (SR) module improves the accuracy and semantic consistency of reasoning results through self-reflection and backward reasoning mechanisms. Experiments demonstrate that Graph Counselor outperforms existing methods in multiple graph reasoning tasks, exhibiting higher reasoning accuracy and generalization ability. Our code is available at https://github.com/gjq100/Graph-Counselor.git.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Graph Counselor: Adaptive Graph Exploration via Multi-Agent Synergy to Enhance LLM Reasoning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Graph Counselor," a novel Graph Retrieval Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) framework designed to improve the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) when working with graph-structured knowledge. It addresses two key limitations of existing GraphRAG methods: inefficient information aggregation and rigid reasoning mechanisms. Graph Counselor employs a multi-agent collaborative approach, featuring the Adaptive Graph Information Extraction Module (AGIEM) with Planning, Thought, and Execution agents, and a Self-Reflection with Multiple Perspectives (SR) module. AGIEM aims to adaptively capture multi-level textual, structural, and degree information within graphs, while SR enhances the accuracy and semantic consistency of reasoning through self-reflection and backward reasoning. The paper presents experimental results on multiple graph reasoning tasks, demonstrating that Graph Counselor outperforms existing methods in reasoning accuracy and generalization ability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the multi-agent collaborative approach for graph exploration and the integration of a self-reflection mechanism designed to mitigate semantic misalignment between graph structures and LLM understanding. The AGIEM module, with its specialized agents and dynamically adjustable reasoning strategies, is a significant contribution. The SR module, although building on existing self-reflection techniques, tailors it to graph reasoning and enhances it with multiple perspectives.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a crucial problem in the field of LLMs – hallucination and factual inconsistency, particularly when dealing with specialized domains and structured knowledge. GraphRAG is a promising area, and this paper advances the state-of-the-art by offering a more adaptive and robust framework.  The improved accuracy and generalization abilities reported in the experiments are significant and suggest the potential for broader applications.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies limitations of existing GraphRAG methods.
    *   The proposed Graph Counselor framework is well-designed and modular, making it easier to understand and potentially extend.
    *   The multi-agent approach in AGIEM is intuitive and addresses the complexities of graph data extraction and reasoning depth.
    *   The self-reflection mechanism is a valuable addition, as it attempts to reconcile the inherent differences between graph structure and linear text understanding of LLMs.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive, covering multiple datasets and LLM backbones.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The complexity of the architecture might make it computationally expensive compared to simpler GraphRAG methods, though the results imply it is worth it. More detailed analysis is warranted.
    *   Although ablation studies are performed, more analysis into why certain submodules perform better in specific datasets or conditions would greatly improve the analysis of the method.
    *   The paper's novelty also comes from the composition of existing methods, while the modularity allows further adaptation, it also means the novelty lies less in a new individual component but the overall system design.

*   **Impact and Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant impact on the field of GraphRAG.  The multi-agent collaborative approach could inspire new designs for knowledge extraction and reasoning. The self-reflection mechanism can provide guidance in developing better training paradigms for LLMs in graph-based reasoning tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7.5**.

*   **High points:**  The paper tackles a critical problem in LLM reasoning with structured data, proposes a well-structured and adaptable framework, and provides compelling experimental results demonstrating improved accuracy and generalization. The multi-agent and self-reflection components significantly contribute to its advancements.
*   **Lower points:**  The complexity of the approach and potential computational cost need further evaluation. The novelty, while significant in its integration of existing methods, could have been higher with the development of novel, specific modules. Greater insight is necessary in the role of submodules and analysis of datasets.

### From Real to Synthetic: Synthesizing Millions of Diversified and Complicated User Instructions with Attributed Grounding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03968v1)
- **Authors**: Chiwei Zhu, Benfeng Xu, Xiaorui Wang, Zhendong Mao
- **Abstract**: The pursuit of diverse, complex, and large-scale instruction data is crucial for automatically aligning large language models (LLMs). While there are methods capable of generating synthetic instructions at scale, they either suffer from limited grounding sources, leading to a narrow distribution, or rely on trivial extensions that fail to produce meaningful trajectories in terms of complexity. In contrast, instructions that benefit efficient alignment are typically crafted with cognitive insights and grounded in real-world use cases. In this paper, we synthesize such instructions using attributed grounding, which involves 1) a top-down attribution process that grounds a selective set of real instructions to situated users, and 2) a bottom-up synthesis process that leverages web documents to first generate a situation, then a meaningful instruction. This framework allows us to harvest diverse and complex instructions at scale, utilizing the vast range of web documents. Specifically, we construct a dataset of 1 million instructions, called SynthQuestions, and demonstrate that models trained on it achieve leading performance on several common benchmarks, with improvements that continually scale with more web corpora. Data, models and codes will be available at https://github.com/Ignoramus0817/SynthQuestions.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary of the paper, along with a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper "From REAL to SYNTHETIC: Synthesizing Millions of Diversified and Complicated User Instructions with Attributed Grounding" presents a novel framework for synthesizing instruction data for training large language models (LLMs). The core idea is attributed grounding, which involves: (1) a top-down attribution process that grounds real instructions to users, documents, and motivations, and (2) a bottom-up synthesis process that leverages web documents to generate situations and then meaningful instructions. This approach aims to overcome limitations of existing synthetic instruction generation methods, which often suffer from narrow distributions or trivial extensions. The authors construct a dataset of 1 million instructions called SYNTHQUESTIONS and demonstrate that models trained on it achieve leading performance on several benchmarks. The paper claims that their method scales effectively with more web corpora and their data will be publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:** The attributed grounding framework is a sound idea. It addresses the limitations of existing synthetic instruction generation by explicitly grounding instructions in real-world contexts. This approach is more sophisticated than simply augmenting seed tasks or generating instructions based on abstract concepts. The combination of top-down attribution and bottom-up synthesis is also a significant contribution. The paper attempts to inject realism and complexity into generated instructions, addressing a key bottleneck for LLM alignment.
    *   **Significance:** Instruction data is crucial for aligning LLMs, and this paper attempts to alleviate the scarcity of high-quality instruction data. The authors demonstrate through experiments the effectiveness of their synthetic data. The claim that models trained on SYNTHQUESTIONS achieve leading performance on several benchmarks is compelling, and the reported improvements scaling with more web corpora is highly valuable. If validated by independent replication, this could have a significant impact on how LLMs are pre-trained and fine-tuned.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a variety of analyses to support its claims, including basic statistics, diversity assessment, complexity analysis, and safety evaluation. This level of detail contributes to the credibility of the results.
    *   **Open Availability:** The authors claim that data, models, and code will be made available, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The method relies on LLMs (specifically LLaMA-3-70B-Instruct and LLaMA-3-8B-Instruct) for both attribution and synthesis. This introduces potential biases and limitations inherent in the LLMs themselves. The generated instructions will likely reflect the knowledge and biases of the LLMs used in the process. While the paper uses REALQUESTIONS to seed the process, the potential influence of the LLMs themselves is a major concern. The instructions generated would be good given the LLMs used to generate them, but may not be a complete representation of all the instructions that are possible.
    *   **Subjectivity in Scoring:** The scoring metrics used to filter instructions are subjective and based on criteria from Arena Hard. While the authors attempt to quantify instruction quality, these measures are not definitive and could influence the results.
    *   **Limited Details on Data Filtering:** Details on cleaning and deduplication of the seed datasets (REALQUESTIONS) should be expanded. The effectiveness of this step is crucial to preventing the injection of low-quality data into the synthetic dataset.
    *   **Lack of Robustness Analysis:** While the authors analyze diversity and complexity, they do not deeply address the robustness of instructions generated.
    *   **Validation of "Realism":** Although the core idea is attributed grounding to simulate real-world scenarios, the extent to which SYNTHQUESTIONS captures the full diversity and nuance of *real* user instructions remains an open question. Further analysis and user studies would be needed to validate this.
    *   **Missing Hallucination Assessment:** The risk of hallucination and unfaithful or wrong information in the synthesized data is not assessed explicitly. This is crucial for trustworthy LLMs. This reduces the "trustworthiness" of SYNTHQUESTIONS.
    *   **Missing "human-in-the-loop" Evaluation**: The evaluation metrics heavily relies on LLM judges. A key part of instruction following LLM is the alignment of the LLM output with human preference.

*   **Overall Impact:**

    The paper presents a promising approach to generating synthetic instruction data, but the reliance on LLMs and the limitations of the evaluation metrics make it difficult to fully assess its impact. If the authors demonstrate the real-world utility of SYNTHQUESTIONS in a controlled setting, it could become a valuable resource for the LLM community. However, further research is needed to address the biases inherent in the LLM-driven synthesis process.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper has significant novelty in its attributed grounding framework and the construction of a large synthetic dataset. The experimental results show promise and its open availability enhances reproducibility. However, the reliance on LLMs for synthesis introduces biases and the lack of thorough human evaluation reduces the potential impact on the field. A score of 6.5 reflects the paper's contribution while acknowledging its limitations. More rigorous analysis of the generated instructions, and human evaluation of the models trained using the synthesized data, would significantly increase the paper's significance.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Solving Inverse Problems via Diffusion-Based Priors: An Approximation-Free Ensemble Sampling Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03979v1)
- **Authors**: Haoxuan Chen, Yinuo Ren, Martin Renqiang Min, Lexing Ying, Zachary Izzo
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models (DMs) have proven to be effective in modeling high-dimensional distributions, leading to their widespread adoption for representing complex priors in Bayesian inverse problems (BIPs). However, current DM-based posterior sampling methods proposed for solving common BIPs rely on heuristic approximations to the generative process. To exploit the generative capability of DMs and avoid the usage of such approximations, we propose an ensemble-based algorithm that performs posterior sampling without the use of heuristic approximations. Our algorithm is motivated by existing works that combine DM-based methods with the sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) method. By examining how the prior evolves through the diffusion process encoded by the pre-trained score function, we derive a modified partial differential equation (PDE) governing the evolution of the corresponding posterior distribution. This PDE includes a modified diffusion term and a reweighting term, which can be simulated via stochastic weighted particle methods. Theoretically, we prove that the error between the true posterior distribution can be bounded in terms of the training error of the pre-trained score function and the number of particles in the ensemble. Empirically, we validate our algorithm on several inverse problems in imaging to show that our method gives more accurate reconstructions compared to existing DM-based methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the paper "Solving Inverse Problems via Diffusion-Based Priors: An Approximation-Free Ensemble Sampling Approach" and will provide a summary and critical evaluation.

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of solving Bayesian inverse problems (BIPs) using diffusion models (DMs) as priors.  Current DM-based posterior sampling methods often rely on heuristic approximations to the generative process.  The authors propose a novel ensemble-based algorithm called Approximation-Free Diffusion Posterior Sampler (AFDPS) that avoids these approximations. The key idea is to leverage the sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) method, combined with a principled utilization of pre-trained DMs for prior evolution.  The authors derive a modified partial differential equation (PDE) governing the evolution of the posterior distribution, which includes a modified diffusion term and a reweighting term.  This PDE is then simulated using stochastic weighted particle methods. The paper offers theoretical guarantees, proving error bounds relating the posterior sampling accuracy to the training error of the pre-trained score function.  Empirical validation on imaging inverse problems demonstrates that AFDPS provides more accurate reconstructions than existing DM-based methods. The AFDPS allows flexible frameworks based on SDE and ODE+Corrector, respectively.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a significant advance in DM-based BIPs by moving away from heuristic approximations. Deriving the exact PDE governing the posterior evolution given a pre-trained diffusion model is a genuinely novel contribution. The idea of incorporating this PDE within an SMC framework to achieve approximation-free posterior sampling is also a worthwhile innovation. The error analysis is a strong feature which provides a concrete justification of the method convergence.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the accuracy and reliability of solutions to BIPs. Bypassing heuristic approximations can lead to more trustworthy reconstructions, especially in scenarios where the approximations may be inaccurate or invalid. The paper's approach offers a theoretically sound alternative to the often ad-hoc nature of current DM-based BIP methods. The presented results in diverse imaging problems validates the method's practical value.

**Strengths:**

*   **Principled approach:** Avoids heuristic approximations by deriving and utilizing the exact posterior evolution PDE.
*   **Theoretical guarantees:**  Provides rigorous error bounds and convergence analysis.
*   **Empirical validation:** Demonstrates improved reconstruction accuracy compared to existing methods on multiple imaging inverse problems.
*   **Flexibility:** Provides two variants based on SDE and ODE, providing flexibility and compatibility with different diffusion architectures.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational cost:**  Ensemble-based methods can be computationally expensive.  While the paper addresses this to some extent by reducing the number of particles for the ODE version, the computational cost remains a factor to consider. The empirical details about convergence rate in practical time are still relatively unclear.
*   **Dependence on pre-trained score function:**  The method's performance relies on the quality of the pre-trained score function. Thus the error is inherently bounded by the approximation error of the score function which may have practical limitations.
*   **Limited scope:** The theoretical analysis does not consider the effects of discretization, as used in the Algorithm, meaning that a theoretical gap to reality remains.

**Influence on the Field:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field by setting a new standard for DM-based BIP methods.  The emphasis on approximation-free sampling and theoretical rigor could encourage future research to focus on more principled approaches. The work could be especially impactful in applications where accurate and reliable reconstructions are crucial.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of 8 because the paper makes a novel and significant contribution to the field. The mathematical rigor, clear presentation of the method, and strong empirical validation are convincing. While the computational cost and dependence on the pre-trained score function are limitations, the benefits of approximation-free sampling and theoretical guarantees outweigh these drawbacks. Its reliance on pre-trained models and specific datasets may reduce its broader impact to other areas, although the results are very promising.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Around the World in 24 Hours: Probing LLM Knowledge of Time and Place
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.03984v1)
- **Authors**: Carolin Holtermann, Paul Röttger, Anne Lauscher
- **Abstract**: Reasoning over time and space is essential for understanding our world. However, the abilities of language models in this area are largely unexplored as previous work has tested their abilities for logical reasoning in terms of time and space in isolation or only in simple or artificial environments. In this paper, we present the first evaluation of the ability of language models to jointly reason over time and space. To enable our analysis, we create GeoTemp, a dataset of 320k prompts covering 289 cities in 217 countries and 37 time zones. Using GeoTemp, we evaluate eight open chat models of three different model families for different combinations of temporal and geographic knowledge. We find that most models perform well on reasoning tasks involving only temporal knowledge and that overall performance improves with scale. However, performance remains constrained in tasks that require connecting temporal and geographical information. We do not find clear correlations of performance with specific geographic regions. Instead, we find a significant performance increase for location names with low model perplexity, suggesting their repeated occurrence during model training. We further demonstrate that their performance is heavily influenced by prompt formulation - a direct injection of geographical knowledge leads to performance gains, whereas, surprisingly, techniques like chain-of-thought prompting decrease performance on simpler tasks.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces GEOTEMP, a novel dataset for evaluating the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to jointly reason over time and space. The dataset consists of 320k prompts covering 289 cities in 217 countries and 37 time zones, designed to test various levels of temporal and geographical knowledge. The authors evaluate eight open chat models, finding that while most models perform well on tasks involving only temporal knowledge, their performance significantly drops when both temporal and geographical reasoning are required. They also observe that performance correlates with location name perplexity and can be improved by injecting geotemporal knowledge. The study further analyzes error patterns, revealing difficulties in handling DST and UTC conversions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation and application of GEOTEMP, a dataset explicitly designed to probe joint temporal and spatial reasoning. Prior work has focused on these areas in isolation or in simplified settings. While existing benchmarks address temporal or geographical reasoning, GEOTEMP's focus on their intersection in real-world scenarios is a genuine advancement. The analysis of model performance on this combined reasoning task is also a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the increasing use of LLMs in tasks that require understanding and integration of time and location information. By highlighting the deficiencies of current LLMs in this area, the paper underscores a critical gap in their capabilities. The findings can inform future research directions aimed at improving LLM reasoning abilities for real-world applications. Furthermore, the dataset itself serves as a valuable resource for the community, enabling standardized evaluation and development of more capable models. The experiments are reasonably thorough, testing a range of models and instruction types. The error analysis, while limited in scale, provides valuable insights into the sources of model failure.

*   **Limitations and Weaknesses:** The paper acknowledges limitations, including the use of a regex-based evaluation method (though validated), prompt robustness being assessed on a subset, and the lack of tasks focused solely on location. Also, the analysis of location biases and perplexity influence could be expanded with more models and data points. The lack of tasks involving *planning* or more complex inference based on spacetime is a missed opportunity. The study largely focuses on *factual recall and arithmetic*, without more rich or pragmatic scenarios.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper and dataset have the potential to influence future research on LLMs by drawing attention to a specific, yet important, reasoning challenge. The error analysis and proposed knowledge injection strategies suggest potential avenues for improving model performance. The findings are particularly relevant to applications in logistics, travel planning, scheduling, and other domains that rely on accurate temporal and spatial reasoning.

*   **Score Justification:** While the paper presents a valuable dataset and analysis, its impact is somewhat limited by the relatively narrow scope of the tasks and the preliminary nature of the error analysis. The knowledge injection experiments, while promising, require further investigation. The findings confirm some anticipated behaviors, which somewhat reduces the novelty. However, the new dataset and systematic evaluation of a previously unexplored task justifies a solid score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### GORACS: Group-level Optimal Transport-guided Coreset Selection for LLM-based Recommender Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04015v1)
- **Authors**: Tiehua Mei, Hengrui Chen, Peng Yu, Jiaqing Liang, Deqing Yang
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) have shown great potential in recommender systems, the prohibitive computational costs for fine-tuning LLMs on entire datasets hinder their successful deployment in real-world scenarios. To develop affordable and effective LLM-based recommender systems, we focus on the task of coreset selection which identifies a small subset of fine-tuning data to optimize the test loss, thereby facilitating efficient LLMs' fine-tuning. Although there exist some intuitive solutions of subset selection, including distribution-based and importance-based approaches, they often lead to suboptimal performance due to the misalignment with downstream fine-tuning objectives or weak generalization ability caused by individual-level sample selection. To overcome these challenges, we propose GORACS, which is a novel Group-level Optimal tRAnsport-guided Coreset Selection framework for LLM-based recommender systems. GORACS is designed based on two key principles for coreset selection: 1) selecting the subsets that minimize the test loss to align with fine-tuning objectives, and 2) enhancing model generalization through group-level data selection. Corresponding to these two principles, GORACS has two key components: 1) a Proxy Optimization Objective (POO) leveraging optimal transport and gradient information to bound the intractable test loss, thus reducing computational costs by avoiding repeated LLM retraining, and 2) a two-stage Initialization-Then-Refinement Algorithm (ITRA) for efficient group-level selection. Our extensive experiments across diverse recommendation datasets and tasks validate that GORACS significantly reduces fine-tuning costs of LLMs while achieving superior performance over the state-of-the-art baselines and full data training. The source code of GORACS are available at https://github.com/Mithas-114/GORACS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "GORACS: Group-level Optimal Transport-guided Coreset Selection for LLM-based Recommender Systems."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the computational cost challenge of fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) for recommender systems. It introduces GORACS, a novel Group-level Optimal tRAnsport-guided Coreset Selection framework. GORACS aims to select a small, representative subset of the training data for fine-tuning, minimizing test loss and enhancing model generalization. The approach is based on two key principles: minimizing test loss and adopting group-level data selection to capture inter-sample correlations.  GORACS employs a Proxy Optimization Objective (POO) that uses optimal transport and gradient information to estimate test loss efficiently, avoiding repeated LLM retraining. It also introduces a two-stage Initialization-Then-Refinement Algorithm (ITRA) for efficient group-level selection. Experiments on diverse recommendation datasets and tasks validate GORACS's effectiveness in reducing fine-tuning costs and achieving superior performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to coreset selection tailored for LLM-based recommender systems. The use of optimal transport (OT) to bound the test loss and guide coreset selection is innovative. The group-level selection strategy, although conceptually plausible, is where most of the novelty is. The POO and ITRA algorithms further enhance this approach. Compared to existing coreset selection methods that often rely on individual sample importance or distribution matching without direct test loss minimization, GORACS offers a theoretically grounded and practically efficient alternative. The label-enhanced approach for discriminative recommendation also enhances the practical relevance of the framework.

*   **Significance:** The high computational cost of fine-tuning LLMs is a significant bottleneck for their deployment in real-world recommender systems. GORACS provides a solution that can significantly reduce this cost while maintaining or even improving performance. The work has practical implications for making LLM-based recommendation more accessible and scalable. The reported experimental results demonstrate substantial cost savings and performance gains compared to other coreset selection methods and full-data training. Further, analyzing the properties such as memorization, and biases is beneficial in coreset design.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Grounding:** The use of optimal transport and gradient analysis provides a solid theoretical foundation for the approach.
    *   **Practical Efficiency:** The POO and ITRA algorithms are designed to be computationally efficient, addressing the key challenge of coreset selection.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** Extensive experiments on diverse datasets and tasks validate the effectiveness of GORACS.
    *   **Problem Solving**: Focus on solving an existing problem by minimizing the test loss, leading to efficient fine-tuning.
    *   **Comparison**: Outperforms full datasets and other baselines, including some distribution based ones.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity:** The approach involves several components (OT, gradient analysis, ITRA), making it somewhat complex to understand and implement.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of GORACS may be sensitive to the choice of the hyperparameter λ, which balances the OT distance and gradient norm terms. The selection is also a potential bottleneck. The work could benefit from an analysis of how this parameter affects the performance and guidance on its setting.

    *   **Encoder dependence**: Relying on pre-trained encoders for data representation could potentially limit the effectiveness of GORACS. The encoder needs to be up to date.

    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis on Optimality**: The analysis focuses more on computational efficiency and practical effectiveness. It does not analyze the upper bound on the optimality gap.
* **Future research**: There are limited discussions on how to generalize GORACS into other areas.

*   **Potential Influence:** GORACS has the potential to influence the field of LLM-based recommender systems by providing a more efficient and effective way to fine-tune these models. The work could inspire further research on coreset selection for LLMs and the application of optimal transport in recommendation.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the novelty of the approach, the significance of the problem addressed, and the strengths and weaknesses of the paper, a score of 7 is appropriate. It presents a novel approach that can be significantly impactful with potential for influence on the field. The experimental validation provides sufficient evidence for the effectiveness of the approach. GORACS addresses a very important issue and will be influential but not transformative.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Rex-Thinker: Grounded Object Referring via Chain-of-Thought Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04034v1)
- **Authors**: Qing Jiang, Xingyu Chen, Zhaoyang Zeng, Junzhi Yu, Lei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Object referring aims to detect all objects in an image that match a given natural language description. We argue that a robust object referring model should be grounded, meaning its predictions should be both explainable and faithful to the visual content. Specifically, it should satisfy two key properties: 1) Verifiable, by producing interpretable reasoning that justifies its predictions and clearly links them to visual evidence; and 2) Trustworthy, by learning to abstain when no object in the image satisfies the given expression. However, most methods treat referring as a direct bounding box prediction task, offering limited interpretability and struggling to reject expressions with no matching object. In this work, we propose Rex-Thinker, a model that formulates object referring as an explicit CoT reasoning task. Given a referring expression, we first identify all candidate object instances corresponding to the referred object category. Rex-Thinker then performs step-by-step reasoning over each candidate to assess whether it matches the given expression, before making a final prediction. To support this paradigm, we construct a large-scale CoT-style referring dataset named HumanRef-CoT by prompting GPT-4o on the HumanRef dataset. Each reasoning trace follows a structured planning, action, and summarization format, enabling the model to learn decomposed, interpretable reasoning over object candidates. We then train Rex-Thinker in two stages: a cold-start supervised fine-tuning phase to teach the model how to perform structured reasoning, followed by GRPO-based RL learning to improve accuracy and generalization. Experiments show that our approach outperforms standard baselines in both precision and interpretability on in-domain evaluation, while also demonstrating improved ability to reject hallucinated outputs and strong generalization in out-of-domain settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the Rex-Thinker paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Rex-Thinker, a novel framework for grounded object referring that leverages Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. Instead of directly predicting bounding box coordinates, Rex-Thinker first identifies candidate objects and then performs step-by-step reasoning to assess whether each candidate matches the given referring expression. This process ensures that the model's predictions are more explainable, verifiable, and trustworthy. To support this approach, the authors construct a large-scale CoT-style referring dataset, HumanRef-CoT, by prompting GPT-40. Rex-Thinker is trained in two stages: supervised fine-tuning for CoT reasoning followed by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) based reinforcement learning to improve accuracy and generalization. Experiments demonstrate that Rex-Thinker outperforms standard baselines, especially in rejecting hallucinated objects and generalizing to out-of-domain scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of applying CoT reasoning to object referring is a significant and novel contribution. While CoT has been explored in other areas of multimodal learning, its application to grounded object referring with a focus on verifiability and trustworthiness is a fresh perspective. The HumanRef-CoT dataset is also a valuable contribution, enabling the training of more interpretable models. The GRPO-based reinforcement learning approach is a plus.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses two key limitations of existing object referring models: lack of interpretability and inability to reject hallucinated outputs. By providing explicit reasoning steps, Rex-Thinker makes its predictions more transparent and verifiable, which is crucial for building trustworthy systems. The improved generalization performance is also significant, as it allows the model to be applied to a wider range of scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The formulation of object referring as a CoT reasoning task is well-motivated and leads to more explainable predictions.
    *   The HumanRef-CoT dataset is a valuable resource for training and evaluating CoT-based referring models.
    *   The two-stage training approach (SFT + GRPO) is effective in improving both accuracy and generalization.
    *   Experiments demonstrate that Rex-Thinker outperforms standard baselines and achieves state-of-the-art performance on the HumanRef benchmark.
    *   The model shows improved ability to reject hallucinated outputs, a key requirement for real-world applications.
    *   Strong zero-shot generalization to out-of-domain datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-40 for generating the HumanRef-CoT dataset raises concerns about potential biases and inconsistencies. While the authors mention filtering low-quality annotations, further analysis of the dataset's quality and diversity would be helpful.
    *   The increase in inference time with the CoT-based approach is a limitation that could hinder real-time applications. The authors acknowledge this trade-off, but future work could explore methods to improve the model's efficiency.
    * The observed inconsistencies between the reasoning and final output, while rare, suggest room for improvement in enforcing tighter alignment between the reasoning process and prediction, and require further investigation.
    * The model's relatively weaker performance in the interaction subset, which the authors acknowledge, suggests that it has difficulty modeling more complex relationships, which raises a question about the scalability of the current formulation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of object referring by promoting the development of more interpretable, verifiable, and trustworthy models. The HumanRef-CoT dataset could become a valuable resource for future research. The proposed framework could also be extended to other multimodal reasoning tasks.

*   **Score:** 7

*   **Rationale:**  Rex-Thinker presents a novel and well-motivated approach to object referring, supported by a substantial dataset and thorough experiments. The results are strong, demonstrating clear improvements over existing methods. While the reliance on GPT-40 for data generation and the increase in inference time are limitations, the paper's contributions to interpretability and trustworthiness justify a high score. Overall, it is a valuable contribution, pushing the field towards grounded and explainable AI for object referring.
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Privacy and Security Threat for OpenAI GPTs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04036v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Wenying, Zhao Kaifa, Xue Lei, Fan Ming
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate powerful information handling capabilities and are widely integrated into chatbot applications. OpenAI provides a platform for developers to construct custom GPTs, extending ChatGPT's functions and integrating external services. Since its release in November 2023, over 3 million custom GPTs have been created. However, such a vast ecosystem also conceals security and privacy threats. For developers, instruction leaking attacks threaten the intellectual property of instructions in custom GPTs through carefully crafted adversarial prompts. For users, unwanted data access behavior by custom GPTs or integrated third-party services raises significant privacy concerns. To systematically evaluate the scope of threats in real-world LLM applications, we develop three phases instruction leaking attacks target GPTs with different defense level. Our widespread experiments on 10,000 real-world custom GPTs reveal that over 98.8% of GPTs are vulnerable to instruction leaking attacks via one or more adversarial prompts, and half of the remaining GPTs can also be attacked through multiround conversations. We also developed a framework to assess the effectiveness of defensive strategies and identify unwanted behaviors in custom GPTs. Our findings show that 77.5% of custom GPTs with defense strategies are vulnerable to basic instruction leaking attacks. Additionally, we reveal that 738 custom GPTs collect user conversational information, and identified 8 GPTs exhibiting data access behaviors that are unnecessary for their intended functionalities. Our findings raise awareness among GPT developers about the importance of integrating specific defensive strategies in their instructions and highlight users' concerns about data privacy when using LLM-based applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates privacy and security threats in OpenAI's custom GPT ecosystem, focusing on instruction leaking attacks (ILAs) and unwanted data collection by third-party services.  The authors develop a three-phase ILA framework targeting GPTs with varying defense levels and conduct experiments on 10,000 real-world custom GPTs. They find that a high percentage of GPTs are vulnerable to ILAs, even those with defense strategies. The paper also analyzes data collection practices, identifying instances of sensitive and unnecessary data collection by integrated third-party services. The authors propose a prompting framework for LLM-based analysis of unwanted behaviors and copyright infringements, concluding by discussing the implications for developers and users, emphasizing the need for stronger defenses and greater user awareness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper's primary strength lies in its large-scale empirical study of instruction leaking attacks on a real-world LLM application ecosystem. The three-phase attack framework is a well-structured approach, considering varying defense levels. The analysis of unwanted data collection by third-party services is also a valuable contribution, as is the framework to detect copyright issues.
*   **Weaknesses:** While instruction leaking attacks have been studied before, the novelty lies in the scale of real-world application. Some components, such as the detailed adversarial prompts, build upon existing work in prompt engineering and jailbreaking, limiting the groundbreaking aspect of the research. The copyright infringement detection, while relevant, uses standard similarity metrics which might be seen as incremental.

**Significance:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper has significant practical implications.  The high vulnerability rates revealed by the study highlight a critical security gap in the OpenAI GPT ecosystem.  The identified instances of unwanted data collection raise important privacy concerns for users. By providing a prompting framework the paper can assist others to analyse these issues. The findings can inform developers about specific weaknesses in their defense strategies and encourage the implementation of more robust protections.
*   **Weaknesses:** The analysis could be strengthened by explicitly discussing the impact of successful attacks (e.g. what a malicious user can actually do if they leak instructions). Furthermore, while the paper mentions OpenAI's content filters, a more detailed evaluation of their effectiveness in preventing ILAs would be beneficial. While it identifies problems, there is not significant analysis of solutions outside of simple best practices.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a valuable empirical study of security and privacy threats in a widely used LLM application ecosystem. While it builds upon previous research in prompt engineering and attack strategies, the scale of the experiments, the detailed analysis of real-world GPTs, and the identification of privacy issues justify a solid score. The lack of significant proposed solutions and a clear assessment of damage scenarios impact the overall novelty. The work provides a useful practical survey and framework for future investigations of custom LLMs.
