### Generating Automotive Code: Large Language Models for Software Development and Verification in Safety-Critical Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04038v1)
- **Authors**: Sven Kirchner, Alois C. Knoll
- **Abstract**: Developing safety-critical automotive software presents significant challenges due to increasing system complexity and strict regulatory demands. This paper proposes a novel framework integrating Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) into the Software Development Lifecycle (SDLC). The framework uses Large Language Models (LLMs) to automate code generation in languages such as C++, incorporating safety-focused practices such as static verification, test-driven development and iterative refinement. A feedback-driven pipeline ensures the integration of test, simulation and verification for compliance with safety standards. The framework is validated through the development of an Adaptive Cruise Control (ACC) system. Comparative benchmarking of LLMs ensures optimal model selection for accuracy and reliability. Results demonstrate that the framework enables automatic code generation while ensuring compliance with safety-critical requirements, systematically integrating GenAI into automotive software engineering. This work advances the use of AI in safety-critical domains, bridging the gap between state-of-the-art generative models and real-world safety requirements.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Generating Automotive Code: Large Language Models for Software Development and Verification in Safety-Critical Systems."

**Summary:**

This paper presents a framework that integrates Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI), specifically Large Language Models (LLMs), into the Software Development Lifecycle (SDLC) for safety-critical automotive software. The framework uses LLMs to automate code generation in C++, incorporating safety-focused practices like static verification, test-driven development, and iterative refinement.  A feedback loop involving test, simulation, and verification is used to ensure compliance with safety standards (like ISO 26262). The framework's effectiveness is demonstrated through a case study involving the development of an Adaptive Cruise Control (ACC) system, tested using the CARLA simulator. The authors also benchmark several LLMs to select the most appropriate one for their use case. The overall goal is to reduce manual coding effort and speed up development while maintaining safety and compliance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of leveraging LLMs for code generation in safety-critical systems is gaining traction, so the core concept isn't entirely novel. However, the paper's *specific* contribution lies in the *integration* of LLMs within a structured, feedback-driven SDLC *designed specifically for automotive safety standards*. The strong focus on static analysis, formal verification, and integration testing to ensure compliance is a valuable differentiator.  The iterative refinement loop guided by test suites and integration scripts also adds to the novelty. The benchmark analysis of LLMs for this specific use case (code generation under safety constraints with a focus on data locality) adds practical value. The specific architecture with the Static Validation Module and Integration Testing Module contributing feedback is another point of incremental novelty.

* **Significance:** The automotive industry faces increasing software complexity and stringent safety regulations. If successful, this framework could significantly reduce development time and cost while maintaining safety. The potential to shift engineers from code writers to orchestrators of generative pipelines is a significant shift. The paper bridges a critical gap by attempting to reconcile the rapid advancements in LLMs with the rigorous requirements of safety-critical engineering. The practical validation via the ACC case study, while limited, provides concrete evidence of the framework's potential.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the challenges in automotive software development.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed framework is structured and includes key components like the LLM Handler, Static Validation Module, and Integration Testing Module.
    *   **Emphasis on Safety:** The explicit focus on safety standards (ISO 26262, MISRA) and the integration of verification techniques is a major strength.
    *   **Practical Validation:**  The ACC case study provides a concrete example of the framework in action.
    *   **LLM Benchmarking:** The comparative benchmarking of LLMs helps justify the choice of LLM.
    *   **Structured User Input:** The usage of JSON and YAML for structured user input improves usability and interpretability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Case Study:** The ACC case study is relatively simple. A more complex and comprehensive case study would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Performance Metrics:** The paper mentions saving time and reducing error rates but doesn't provide detailed quantitative metrics or comparisons to traditional development methods. More concrete data comparing the framework's performance with existing approaches would be beneficial.
    *   **Generality:** While ACC is a good starting point, more discussion about the framework's applicability to other automotive software domains would be valuable.
    *   **Deployment feasibility:**  The paper glosses over the practical challenges of deploying LLMs locally, especially in resource-constrained environments. A more in-depth analysis of these challenges and potential solutions is needed.
    *   **Threats to validity:** The paper would benefit from a more thorough discussion of the threats to validity of the case study. For example, the reliance on synthetic data from the CARLA simulator may limit the generalizability of the results to real-world driving scenarios.
    *   **Lack of mathematical guarantee:** The paper uses some mathematical equations, however, the lack of proper mathematical guarantee in the form of software correctness prevents it from getting a higher score.

*   **Impact:** The paper's impact will depend on how effectively the framework can be scaled to more complex applications and how widely it is adopted in the automotive industry. It has the potential to influence the development of future automotive software development methodologies.

**Overall Score Justification:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper presents a valuable contribution but has room for improvement. It effectively integrates LLMs into a safety-conscious automotive software development framework. However, the limitations of the case study, the lack of in-depth performance analysis, and the missing discussion of mathematical guarantee reduces the overall score.

### Mitigating Hallucinations in Large Vision-Language Models via Entity-Centric Multimodal Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04039v1)
- **Authors**: Jiulong Wu, Zhengliang Shi, Shuaiqiang Wang, Jizhou Huang, Dawei Yin, Lingyong Yan, Min Cao, Min Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Visual Language Models (LVLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities across multiple tasks. However, their trustworthiness is often challenged by hallucinations, which can be attributed to the modality misalignment and the inherent hallucinations of their underlying Large Language Models (LLMs) backbone. Existing preference alignment methods focus on aligning model responses with human preferences while neglecting image-text modality alignment, resulting in over-reliance on LLMs and hallucinations. In this paper, we propose Entity-centric Multimodal Preference Optimization (EMPO), which achieves enhanced modality alignment than existing human preference alignment methods. Besides, to overcome the scarcity of high-quality multimodal preference data, we utilize open-source instruction datasets to automatically construct high-quality preference data across three aspects: image, instruction, and response. Experiments on two human preference datasets and five multimodal hallucination benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of EMPO, e.g., reducing hallucination rates by 85.9% on Object-HalBench and 49.8% on MM-HalBench.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Mitigating Hallucinations in Large Vision-Language Models via Entity-Centric Multimodal Preference Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of hallucinations in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). It argues that existing methods focus on aligning model responses with overall human preference without adequately addressing image-text modality misalignment and inherent LLM hallucinations, particularly concerning entity-centric factual information. The authors propose Entity-centric Multimodal Preference Optimization (EMPO). EMPO explicitly focuses on aligning entity features and semantic concepts across images, user instructions, and model responses. The method constructs a multimodal preference dataset by automatically editing entities, attributes, and relationships within image-instruction-response triplets using existing image-text datasets and tools like GPT-4V and Stable Diffusion. The EMPO method then uses Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) loss across image, instruction, and response aspects, with entity-specific token weighting, to enhance LVLM's focus on critical features. Experiments on several multimodal hallucination benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of EMPO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its explicit entity-centric focus for multimodal preference optimization. While DPO and preference alignment are known techniques, the paper's careful engineering of preference data around factual entities within the image and the introduction of entity-specific weighting is a differentiating factor. Automatically generating the preference data to address the scarcity of high-quality multimodal preference data is another positive contribution. However, the use of GPT-4V and Stable Diffusion for dataset construction introduces a dependency on other potentially flawed models, raising concerns about error propagation. The authors could better analyze the potential biases introduced by those tools.

*   **Significance:** Reducing hallucinations is crucial for trustworthy LVLMs. The paper reports substantial improvements in hallucination metrics on standard benchmarks compared to existing methods, including DPO and GPT-4V. The findings suggest that attending specifically to entity-level alignment can be a significant factor in mitigation. The proposed data construction method presents a practical way to generate preference data, thereby allowing more efficient alignment of the model. However, it's crucial to consider the generalizability of these improvements to more complex and less constrained scenarios. Are the specific edits and token weights learned transferable to other tasks or datasets? The paper would be strengthened by analyzing these aspects in more depth. The paper could improve the experimental section by explicitly outlining and comparing with their method the cost of computation and the size of the training dataset.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Addresses a critical problem in LVLMs: hallucinations.
    *   Presents a well-defined and implemented method.
    *   Demonstrates significant performance gains on several benchmarks.
    *   Provides an automated way to construct multimodal preference data using existing datasets.
    *   Offers ablation studies to evaluate the importance of different components.
    *   Includes visualization of model attention.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Dataset construction relies on potentially flawed models (GPT-4V, Stable Diffusion).
    *   The generalizability of learned entity-specific weights and edits is not thoroughly investigated.
    *   The reliance on specific tools like Stable Diffusion limits the portability of the data creation pipeline.
    *   The paper would be strengthened by explicitly outlining and comparing with their method the cost of computation and the size of the training dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in LVLM alignment by emphasizing the importance of entity-level factual consistency. The automated data construction method could be adapted and improved upon by others in the field. The paper provides a strong baseline for future improvements in mitigating hallucination in LVLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to mitigating hallucinations in LVLMs by explicitly focusing on entity-centric alignment. The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements on standard benchmarks. However, the reliance on other AI models for data construction, the limited exploration of generalizability, and cost makes it a solid but not exceptional contribution. While the gains are significant, a more critical analysis of the limitations of the proposed approach, a demonstration of computational and data usage costs and a discussion of generalizability would have merited a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Unveiling and Eliminating the Shortcut Learning for Locate-Then-Edit Knowledge Editing via Both Subject and Relation Awareness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04042v1)
- **Authors**: Xiyu Liu, Zhengxiao Liu, Naibin Gu, Zheng Lin, Ji Xiang, Weiping Wang
- **Abstract**: Knowledge editing aims to alternate the target knowledge predicted by large language models while ensuring the least side effects on unrelated knowledge. An effective way to achieve knowledge editing is to identify pivotal parameters for predicting factual associations and modify them with an optimization process to update the predictions. However, these locate-then-edit methods are uncontrollable since they tend to modify most unrelated relations connected to the subject of target editing. We unveil that this failure of controllable editing is due to a shortcut learning issue during the optimization process. Specifically, we discover two crucial features that are the subject feature and the relation feature for models to learn during optimization, but the current optimization process tends to over-learning the subject feature while neglecting the relation feature. To eliminate this shortcut learning of the subject feature, we propose a novel two-stage optimization process that balances the learning of the subject feature and the relation feature. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach successfully prevents knowledge editing from shortcut learning and achieves the optimal overall performance, contributing to controllable knowledge editing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the issue of uncontrolled knowledge editing in large language models (LLMs) when using locate-then-edit methods like ROME. The authors identify a "shortcut learning" problem where the editing process over-learns the subject feature while neglecting the relation feature, leading to unintended changes in facts related to the subject. To mitigate this, they propose a Two-stage Optimization Process (TOP) that explicitly balances learning of both subject and relation features. The first stage optimizes the relation feature, and the second stage optimizes the subject feature.  Experimental results demonstrate improved performance over existing methods on knowledge editing benchmarks, particularly in terms of specificity and controllability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The identification of the shortcut learning issue (over-reliance on the subject feature during knowledge editing) and the proposed TOP to address it is novel. While causal tracing and feature analysis have been used in prior work related to LLMs, the specific application to understand and improve knowledge editing controllability, focusing on balancing subject and relation features, appears to be a significant contribution. The two-stage optimization process is a simple, yet effective way to address this problem.

* **Significance:** The work directly addresses a crucial limitation of existing knowledge editing techniques: the unintended modification of unrelated facts. Making knowledge editing more controllable is essential for building trustworthy and reliable LLMs. The paper's experimental results demonstrate a clear improvement in specificity without sacrificing other performance metrics like efficacy and generalization. Addressing the "shortcut" learning problem opens up avenues for more refined and targeted knowledge interventions in LLMs.  The trade-off analysis on choosing optimal layers for relation awareness offers practical guidance for implementing this method.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper provides a well-defined and easily understandable explanation of the shortcut learning issue in knowledge editing.
    * **Thorough Analysis:** The gradient saliency analysis and causal tracing experiments provide empirical evidence to support the authors' claims.
    * **Effective Solution:** The proposed TOP method is relatively simple to implement and yields significant improvements in controllability.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes a thorough evaluation on established knowledge editing benchmarks and compares against strong baselines.
    * **Trade-off Analysis:** The examination of layer selection and its impact on specificity and generalization offers valuable insights for practitioners.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Solution:** While TOP addresses the specific shortcut learning issue related to subject-relation imbalance, it might not be a comprehensive solution to all controllability problems in knowledge editing. The current TOP is based on ROME, a specific locate-then-edit method.  Its applicability and potential modifications for other knowledge editing architectures (e.g., those using attention modification) might require further investigation.
    * **Implementation Detail:** The paper mentions editing 18th and 19th layers successively for GPT2-XL which adds complexity of applying the method.
    * **Ablation Study:** It is important to isolate how each component in the proposed TOP contributes to the performance. Additional controlled experiments are necessary to determine the individual effects of the optimization components, leading to a better understanding of the system.

* **Potential Impact:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the direction of knowledge editing research by highlighting the importance of controllability and providing a practical solution to a key limitation of existing methods.  It also provides insights that could be generalized to other problems related to controlling the behavior of LLMs.

* **Score Justification:**
Given the clear novelty in identifying and addressing the shortcut learning issue, the significance of the work in improving the controllability of knowledge editing, the rigorous analysis and experimental results, and the potential impact on the field, the paper warrants a high score. However, the limited scope of the solution and the potential for further improvements justify not assigning a perfect score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Lacuna Inc. at SemEval-2025 Task 4: LoRA-Enhanced Influence-Based Unlearning for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04044v1)
- **Authors**: Aleksey Kudelya, Alexander Shirnin
- **Abstract**: This paper describes LIBU (LoRA enhanced influence-based unlearning), an algorithm to solve the task of unlearning - removing specific knowledge from a large language model without retraining from scratch and compromising its overall utility (SemEval-2025 Task 4: Unlearning sensitive content from Large Language Models). The algorithm combines classical \textit{influence functions} to remove the influence of the data from the model and \textit{second-order optimization} to stabilize the overall utility. Our experiments show that this lightweight approach is well applicable for unlearning LLMs in different kinds of task.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents LIBU (LoRA-enhanced influence-based unlearning), a two-phase algorithm designed to remove specific knowledge from large language models (LLMs) without requiring retraining from scratch and while maintaining overall utility.  The first phase uses influence functions and Fisher Information approximation to compute parameter updates that minimize retain-set disruption. The second phase applies second-order optimization using the Sophia optimizer to stabilize the model after the influence-based update. The method utilizes LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation) for parameter-efficient fine-tuning.  The authors evaluate LIBU on the SemEval-2025 Task 4: Unlearning Sensitive Content from LLMs using the OLMo model and a novel evaluation benchmark. The paper shows the method achieves a competitive performance, balancing regurgitation rates on the forget set and MMLU accuracy on the retain set.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practicality:** The focus on computational efficiency is a major strength. Unlearning LLMs is computationally expensive; therefore, a method that can achieve good performance within reasonable resource constraints is valuable. The use of LoRA and gradient accumulation further enhances practicality.
    *   **Two-Phase Approach:**  Decoupling the influence-based forgetting from the stabilization step with Sophia is a good design choice. This approach seems to address issues often found with joint optimization.
    *   **Clear Evaluation:** The evaluation is performed on a standard benchmark (SemEval 2025 Task 4) allowing for direct comparison with other methods. The paper clearly outlines the experimental setup and reports key metrics.
    *   **Well-defined Problem:** Tackling the unlearning problem specifically for LLMs in a privacy-conscious manner is a relevant and increasingly important research direction.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** The paper acknowledges a lack of ablation studies examining the individual contributions of Sophia and the influence function component. This makes it difficult to determine the precise benefit of each element. The assertion that the components work better together should be supported with more experimental evidence.
    *   **Model Size:** The evaluation is conducted on relatively small models (OLMo-7B and OLMo-1B). While this is understandable given the computational constraints, it raises questions about how well the method scales to larger, state-of-the-art LLMs.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The results show that the performance is highly sensitive to hyperparameter choices. This makes the method less robust and more difficult to deploy in practice. While the paper acknowledges this limitation, more detail about best practices for hyperparameter tuning would be beneficial.
    *   **Methodological Novelty:** While the combination of LoRA, influence functions, and Sophia optimizer in a two-phase approach is technically a novel combination, the individual components are well-established techniques. The novelty lies more in the application and integration of these components rather than in fundamentally new theoretical contributions.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a pressing challenge in the field of LLMs, namely the ability to selectively remove knowledge without significant performance degradation. The practical focus and resource-efficient design of LIBU make it potentially valuable for real-world applications.
*   **Justification:** The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field by proposing a practical and efficient unlearning algorithm for LLMs. The method demonstrates a promising balance between regurgitation rate and model utility. However, the limited ablation studies, reliance on smaller models, and hyperparameter sensitivity detract from the overall impact.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:**
The paper presents a valuable contribution by addressing the practical challenges of machine unlearning in LLMs with limited compute. While the individual techniques aren't groundbreaking, the combination and two-phase approach demonstrate a promising result in balancing unlearning performance and preserving model utility in small models. However, the lack of ablations and limited model scale impact the paper's overall novelty and significance, justifying a score of 6. Future work should address these weaknesses by investigating the performance of LIBU on larger models, conducting thorough ablation studies, and developing more robust hyperparameter tuning strategies.
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Explainability-Based Token Replacement on LLM-Generated Text
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04050v1)
- **Authors**: Hadi Mohammadi, Anastasia Giachanou, Daniel L. Oberski, Ayoub Bagheri
- **Abstract**: Generative models, especially large language models (LLMs), have shown remarkable progress in producing text that appears human-like. However, they often exhibit patterns that make their output easier to detect than text written by humans. In this paper, we investigate how explainable AI (XAI) methods can be used to reduce the detectability of AI-generated text (AIGT) while also introducing a robust ensemble-based detection approach. We begin by training an ensemble classifier to distinguish AIGT from human-written text, then apply SHAP and LIME to identify tokens that most strongly influence its predictions. We propose four explainability-based token replacement strategies to modify these influential tokens. Our findings show that these token replacement approaches can significantly diminish a single classifier's ability to detect AIGT. However, our ensemble classifier maintains strong performance across multiple languages and domains, showing that a multi-model approach can mitigate the impact of token-level manipulations. These results show that XAI methods can make AIGT harder to detect by focusing on the most influential tokens. At the same time, they highlight the need for robust, ensemble-based detection strategies that can adapt to evolving approaches for hiding AIGT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of explainable AI (XAI) techniques to both hide and detect AI-generated text (AIGT). The authors develop an ensemble-based AIGT detector and then investigate how SHAP and LIME can be used to identify influential tokens that contribute most to the detector's predictions. These tokens are then replaced using four different strategies (human-preferred synonyms, POS-constrained synonyms, GPT-based substitutions, and genre-enriched GPT substitutions) to make the AIGT harder to detect. The experiments evaluate the detectability and textual fidelity of the rewritten text, showing that while token replacement can fool some single-model detectors, the ensemble classifier is more robust.  The work highlights a tension between making AI text less detectable (for potentially beneficial uses) and the need for robust detection strategies to prevent misuse.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Ensemble Approach:** The ensemble-based detector is a strong and crucial component. It addresses a key weakness of many AIGT detection methods, which are often easily fooled by simple manipulations. The paper demonstrates the value of combining multiple models for greater robustness.

    *   **XAI Application:** The use of SHAP and LIME to identify influential tokens is a novel and insightful application of XAI techniques to the AIGT detection problem. It provides a way to target specific areas of the text for modification.

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates multiple rewriting strategies and token selection methods. The inclusion of both detectability and textual fidelity metrics provides a more complete picture of the trade-offs involved.
    *   **Multilingual & Multidomain**: The study examines English and Dutch texts, across news, Twitter, and reviews, thus improving the robustness of the claims and approaches tested.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Rewriting Strategies:** While the paper explores several rewriting strategies, they are primarily focused on token replacement. More sophisticated paraphrasing or style transfer techniques could potentially be even more effective at evading detection.
    *   **GPT-4 Red-Teaming:** Given GPT-4's central role in detection and attack, a more comprehensive red-teaming exercise involving fine-tuning or adaptation of GPT-4 for this specific adversarial task could have strengthened the paper.
    *   **Fidelity Metrics:** While BLEU and ROUGE are used to assess fidelity, they can be crude measures of meaning preservation. Human evaluation or more advanced semantic similarity metrics could provide a more nuanced understanding of the impact of rewriting on the text's content.
    *   **Focus on detectability, not attribution:** The paper only covers detectability, but does not attempt to address the problem of attribution, i.e., trying to identify which model produced the altered text.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    The novelty lies in the use of XAI to find the most important tokens in AIGT and the systematic evaluation of different methods for modifying these tokens to evade detection. The finding that a robust ensemble classifier can withstand these targeted attacks is also a significant contribution. However, the specific token replacement strategies are relatively straightforward. A deeper dive into more sophisticated rewriting techniques or an exploration of the theoretical limits of detection in the face of adversarial rewriting could significantly increase the paper's impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid and well-executed study that makes a valuable contribution to the AIGT detection literature. The ensemble approach and the XAI-driven token replacement are innovative and well-motivated. However, the limited exploration of rewriting techniques and the relatively crude fidelity metrics prevent the paper from achieving a higher score. The paper's impact is also somewhat limited by its focus on detectability rather than attribution, although the former is a necessary first step towards the latter. Thus, the paper deserves a score of **7** due to its novelty, rigor, and practical relevance, albeit with some limitations that could be addressed in future work.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### High Accuracy, Less Talk (HALT): Reliable LLMs through Capability-Aligned Finetuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04051v1)
- **Authors**: Tim Franzmeyer, Archie Sravankumar, Lijuan Liu, Yuning Mao, Rui Hou, Sinong Wang, Jakob N. Foerster, Luke Zettlemoyer, Madian Khabsa
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) currently respond to every prompt. However, they can produce incorrect answers when they lack knowledge or capability -- a problem known as hallucination. We instead propose post-training an LLM to generate content only when confident in its correctness and to otherwise (partially) abstain. Specifically, our method, HALT, produces capability-aligned post-training data that encodes what the model can and cannot reliably generate. We generate this data by splitting responses of the pretrained LLM into factual fragments (atomic statements or reasoning steps), and use ground truth information to identify incorrect fragments. We achieve capability-aligned finetuning responses by either removing incorrect fragments or replacing them with "Unsure from Here" -- according to a tunable threshold that allows practitioners to trade off response completeness and mean correctness of the response's fragments. We finetune four open-source models for biography writing, mathematics, coding, and medicine with HALT for three different trade-off thresholds. HALT effectively trades off response completeness for correctness, increasing the mean correctness of response fragments by 15% on average, while resulting in a 4% improvement in the F1 score (mean of completeness and correctness of the response) compared to the relevant baselines. By tuning HALT for highest correctness, we train a single reliable Llama3-70B model with correctness increased from 51% to 87% across all four domains while maintaining 53% of the response completeness achieved with standard finetuning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "High Accuracy, Less Talk (HALT): Reliable LLMs through Capability-Aligned Finetuning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HALT (High Accuracy, Less Talk), a novel post-training finetuning method for Large Language Models (LLMs).  HALT aims to improve the reliability of LLMs by encouraging them to abstain (partially or fully) from generating content they are not confident about, thereby reducing hallucinations.  The method works by first generating a preliminary response from a pretrained LLM, then splitting this response into factual fragments. An evaluator LLM, conditioned on ground truth information, assesses the correctness of each fragment.  The final finetuning response is constructed using only the fragments deemed correct, or replacing incorrect fragments and those following them in a causally dependent chain with "Unsure from Here". A trade-off between response completeness and accuracy is controlled by a tunable threshold. Experiments across biography writing, mathematics, coding, and medicine show HALT effectively trades off completeness for correctness, leading to increased accuracy and overall F1 score improvements compared to standard finetuning and other baselines. Furthermore, a single, reliable multi-domain Llama3-70B model trained using HALT achieves high correctness (87%) while retaining a reasonable level of completeness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of capability-aligned finetuning is where the novelty primarily lies. The method is clever in that it attempts to finetune based on what a LLM *already* knows, not by introducing entirely new concepts. The creation of finetuning data based on fragmenting and evaluating preliminary responses is a reasonable approach. Compared to methods that focus on detecting and removing hallucinations from pre-existing outputs, HALT takes a more proactive approach by training the model to be more selective in its responses from the outset. The tunable threshold allowing for control of the completeness vs accuracy tradeoff is a valuable contribution, increasing the applicability to a wider range of use cases.  However, the individual components (fragmentation, correctness evaluation using another LLM, finetuning) are not entirely novel in isolation. The innovation lies in the integration of these components into a cohesive pipeline specifically designed for capability alignment.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in its potential to improve the trustworthiness of LLMs, especially in high-stakes applications where factual accuracy is paramount (e.g., medicine, law). The ability to tune the trade-off between completeness and correctness makes HALT practical for different deployment scenarios. The reported improvements in accuracy and F1 score, along with the creation of a more reliable multi-domain model, demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. However, the significance is somewhat limited by the reliance on another LLM for evaluation, which introduces potential biases and inaccuracies (as acknowledged by the authors). Additionally, the method's performance hinges on the accuracy of the fragmenter; poor fragmentation could cripple performance. Despite these limitations, the concept of aligning finetuning with inherent capabilities is a significant step towards more reliable and trustworthy LLMs. The method provides tangible improvements demonstrated through empirical studies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear and well-defined methodology.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation across multiple datasets and models.
    *   Demonstrated improvements in accuracy and F1 score.
    *   Tunable threshold for balancing completeness and correctness.
    *   Ablation studies demonstrating the importance of various components.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Reliance on another LLM (Llama3-405B) for evaluating correctness, introducing potential bias.
    *   Performance is contingent on the accuracy of the fragmentation process.
    *   Assumes a limited response structure in terms of independent or causally dependent fragments. Complex dependencies are not fully addressed.
    *   The results are somewhat limited by the fact that only publicly available data and LLMs are used.
    *   The absolute completeness of the multi-domain HALT model is somewhat limited (25%), indicating that substantial information is still being omitted.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of LLM finetuning research, particularly in the area of reliability and trust. The concept of capability alignment could become a standard practice in developing more trustworthy LLMs.  The method also suggests interesting directions for further research, such as developing more robust fragmentation techniques, exploring different evaluation strategies, and extending the approach to multi-modal domains.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The assigned score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field of LLM finetuning and reliability. The methodology is well-defined, the experimental results are compelling, and the potential impact is significant. However, the weaknesses related to evaluator bias, fragmentation challenges, and limited scope prevent it from achieving a higher score.  While the improvements shown in the paper are solid, the reliance on Llama3-405B prevents the solution from being easily ported over to new architectures and models.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Crowd-SFT: Crowdsourcing for LLM Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04063v1)
- **Authors**: Alex Sotiropoulos, Sulyab Thottungal Valapu, Linus Lei, Jared Coleman, Bhaskar Krishnamachari
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly rely on Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) to align model responses with human preferences. While RLHF employs a reinforcement learning approach with a separate reward model, SFT uses human-curated datasets for supervised learning. Both approaches traditionally depend on small, vetted groups of annotators, making them costly, prone to bias, and limited in scalability. We propose an open, crowd-sourced fine-tuning framework that addresses these limitations by enabling broader feedback collection for SFT without extensive annotator training. Our framework promotes incentive fairness via a point-based reward system correlated with Shapley values and guides model convergence through iterative model updates. Our multi-model selection framework demonstrates up to a 55% reduction in target distance over single-model selection, enabling subsequent experiments that validate our point-based reward mechanism's close alignment with Shapley values (a well-established method for attributing individual contributions) thereby supporting fair and scalable participation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Crowd-SFT, a novel crowdsourced supervised fine-tuning (SFT) framework for large language models (LLMs).  It tackles limitations of traditional SFT and RLHF, which often rely on small, curated groups of annotators, leading to biases and scalability issues. Crowd-SFT enables broader feedback collection for SFT without extensive annotator training. The framework uses a point-based reward system correlated with Shapley values to ensure incentive fairness and guides model convergence through iterative updates.  Multiple user groups train competing model instances, and a selection mechanism picks the best model to advance. Users receive rewards based on their group's performance relative to a baseline, ensuring proportionate compensation.  The authors validate the framework through simulations, demonstrating improved model convergence and accurate estimation of group-wise contributions.  Key contributions include an iterative fine-tuning framework, a Shapley-value-aligned reward system, and empirical validation of improved convergence and fair reward distribution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of crowdsourced SFT with a focus on fairness and contribution tracking is novel. Existing literature often overlooks the practical challenges of data collection in SFT, such as annotation bias and scalability, which this paper explicitly addresses.  The use of Shapley values to align the reward system is a strong theoretical grounding.
*   **Significance:** Addressing the limitations of SFT (and indirectly RLHF) is significant. Democratizing LLM alignment could lead to more robust, less biased models.  The framework's potential to scale fine-tuning more effectively addresses a crucial bottleneck in LLM development.
*   **Technical Soundness:** The paper provides a well-defined framework with clear algorithms. The simulation setup is detailed, and the evaluation metrics are appropriate (convergence distance, Pearson correlation with Shapley values).
*   **Experimental Validation:** The simulations demonstrate the framework's effectiveness, showing improved convergence compared to single-model SFT. The experiments also support the claim that the point-based reward mechanism aligns with Shapley values, validating fairness.
*   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to understand, although some parts of the simulation setup could be clearer.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Simulation vs. Real-World Complexity:** The biggest weakness is the reliance on simulations. While the simulations are comprehensive, they make simplifying assumptions about user behavior, motivation, and annotation quality.  Real-world crowdworkers may not behave as predictably as simulated agents. The MovieLens dataset used in the simulation, while providing user preference data, may not fully capture the nuances of text-based interactions and the inherent subjectivity in LLM alignment tasks.
*   **Lack of Human Evaluation:**  The absence of real-world human evaluation is a major gap.  It's unclear how well the proposed framework would translate into a practical system where annotators are prone to error, bias, and strategic behavior.
*   **Limited Evaluation Metrics:** The evaluation primarily focuses on convergence distance and correlation with Shapley values. While these are important, they don't fully capture the subjective quality of the aligned LLM. Metrics like human preference ratings or evaluations of specific LLM behaviors (e.g., safety, helpfulness) would strengthen the validation.
*   **Simplistic Reward System:**  The basic points system (best model gets *m* points, next gets *m-1*, etc.) is quite simplistic. Real-world reward design needs to consider factors like cost per annotation, user engagement, and mechanisms to prevent gaming the system.

**Novelty and Significance Assessment:**

While the paper makes a valuable contribution by addressing the practical challenges of scaling SFT, its novelty is somewhat limited by its reliance on simulations and the lack of real-world validation.  The ideas are promising, but their impact on the field will depend on their successful implementation in practice. I'd put more emphasis on its significance, because democratization of LLM alignment can be a critical factor to unlock innovation in AI.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of 6.5 out of 10.

*   **The core idea is novel and addresses a significant problem (6 points):** Crowdsourced SFT with a focus on fairness is a worthwhile contribution. It provides a direction for making AI more inclusive.
*   **The theoretical foundation is sound (Shapley values) (+0.5 point):** Provides a fairness benchmark.
*   **Simulation-based validation is present, but insufficient (-1 point):** The simulations are well-designed but cannot fully capture real-world complexities.
*   **Lack of human evaluation is a major limitation (-1 point):** The lack of real-world validation significantly weakens the claims.
*   **Reward system is simplistic and needs further refinement (0 points):** A more elaborate reward system will unlock more potential.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 6/10

### Progressive Mastery: Customized Curriculum Learning with Guided Prompting for Mathematical Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04065v1)
- **Authors**: Muling Wu, Qi Qian, Wenhao Liu, Xiaohua Wang, Zisu Huang, Di Liang, LI Miao, Shihan Dou, Changze Lv, Zhenghua Wang, Zhibo Xu, Lina Chen, Tianlong Li, Xiaoqing Zheng, Xuanjing Huang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable performance across various reasoning tasks, yet post-training is constrained by inefficient sample utilization and inflexible difficulty samples processing. To address these limitations, we propose Customized Curriculum Learning (CCL), a novel framework with two key innovations. First, we introduce model-adaptive difficulty definition that customizes curriculum datasets based on each model's individual capabilities rather than using predefined difficulty metrics. Second, we develop "Guided Prompting," which dynamically reduces sample difficulty through strategic hints, enabling effective utilization of challenging samples that would otherwise degrade performance. Comprehensive experiments on supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning demonstrate that CCL significantly outperforms uniform training approaches across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, confirming its effectiveness across both paradigms in enhancing sample utilization and model performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Customized Curriculum Learning" (CCL), a novel framework for post-training large language models (LLMs) to improve their mathematical reasoning abilities. CCL addresses limitations in existing curriculum learning approaches by:

1.  **Model-Adaptive Difficulty Definition:**  Instead of using predefined, heuristic difficulty metrics (like problem length or level in a dataset), CCL assesses difficulty based on *each model's* individual performance on training samples. It calculates the accuracy of the model's responses to determine the difficulty level of each sample for that *specific* model.
2.  **Guided Prompting:** To avoid simply discarding challenging samples (which can be data-inefficient), CCL uses "Guided Prompting." This involves augmenting difficult input examples with targeted hints derived from step-by-step solutions. This dynamically modulates the difficulty, allowing the model to learn from samples that would otherwise be beyond its capabilities.

The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of CCL through experiments on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) paradigms across several mathematical reasoning benchmarks. Results show CCL significantly outperforms uniform training approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the *combination* of model-adaptive difficulty assessment and guided prompting.  While curriculum learning itself is not new, adapting the curriculum to individual model strengths and weaknesses is a valuable step. The Guided Prompting is also a strong addition.  Many CL methods rely on filtering which is data-inefficient; providing tailored assistance is a good way to overcome that.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is in improving the sample efficiency and performance of LLMs on complex reasoning tasks like mathematics.  The results demonstrate clear improvements over baseline approaches. Further, the paper provides insights into the limitations of using static difficulty metrics across different models, contributing to a better understanding of how to train LLMs for specific capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing post-training methods.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:** CCL is well-explained and easy to understand.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple models, training paradigms, and benchmarks.  The ablation studies provide further insight into the contribution of individual components (difficulty definition, difficult sample processing).
    *   **Strong Results:** The empirical results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of CCL.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Computational Cost:** Determining each model's adaptive difficulty can be computationally expensive, requiring initial inference on the entire training set. While this cost isn't explicitly discussed, it should be a consideration. It should be emphasized, though, that this is an offline process and doesn't affect the cost of inference.
    *   **Domain Specificity:** The paper focuses primarily on mathematical reasoning. The generalizability of CCL to other domains requires further investigation.
    *   **Hint Engineering/Decomposition:** The effectiveness of "Guided Prompting" hinges on the quality of the step-by-step solutions used to generate hints. The paper could provide more details on how these solutions are obtained and how the hints are extracted/selected from them. Is the process automated? How do the extracted hints affect the quality and usefulness of the adaptation?

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper's ideas could influence future research in curriculum learning and model adaptation for LLMs.  The model-adaptive difficulty assessment approach could be adopted in other domains and combined with other techniques. The idea of guided prompting is a natural direction for curriculum learning, and this approach provides a concrete way of implementing that.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution with a good combination of novelty, empirical validation, and potential impact. The model-adaptive curriculum and guided prompting are well-motivated and provide a practical way to improve LLM reasoning abilities. The weaknesses identified (computational cost, domain specificity, reliance on hint quality) are areas for future research, but do not significantly detract from the overall value of the work. Thus the score reflects the substantial progress made.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Controlling Difficulty of Generated Text for AI-Assisted Language Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04072v1)
- **Authors**: Meiqing Jin, Liam Dugan, Chris Callison-Burch
- **Abstract**: Practicing conversations with large language models (LLMs) presents a promising alternative to traditional in-person language learning. However, most LLMs generate text at a near-native level of complexity, making them ill-suited for beginner learners (CEFR: A1-A2). In this paper, we investigate whether controllable generation techniques -- specifically modular methods that do not require model fine-tuning -- can adapt LLM outputs to better support absolute beginners. We evaluate these methods through both automatic metrics and a user study with university-level learners of Japanese. Our findings show that while prompting alone fails to control output difficulty, the use of future discriminators (Yang and Klein, 2021) significantly improves output comprehensibility (from 40.4\% to 84.3\%). We further introduce a novel token-level evaluation metric, Token Miss Rate (TMR), that quantifies the proportion of incomprehensible tokens per utterance and correlates strongly with human judgments. To support future research in AI-assisted language learning, we release our code, models, annotation tools, and dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of controllable generation techniques, specifically future discriminators (FUDGE), to adapt large language model (LLM) outputs for beginner-level Japanese language learners (CEFR A1-A2).  The authors argue that standard LLMs generate text at a level too complex for beginners. They focus on modular methods that don't require model fine-tuning. The paper evaluates FUDGE through automatic metrics and a user study with university students learning Japanese. Results indicate that future discriminators significantly improve comprehensibility, as quantified by a newly introduced metric, Token Miss Rate (TMR), while maintaining fluency and naturalness.  The authors release their code, models, annotation tools, and dataset.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several areas: 1) its focus on absolute beginner language learners, a group often overlooked in LLM-based language learning research; 2) the evaluation of modular, controllable generation techniques (specifically future discriminators) for difficulty control, avoiding expensive fine-tuning; 3) the introduction of Token Miss Rate (TMR), a token-level metric that correlates with human judgments of comprehensibility; 4) a rigorous experimental setup including both automatic and human evaluation. Previous work often focuses on re-ranking or fine-tuning approaches and doesn't specifically target absolute beginners. TMR provides a finer-grained and more scalable way to assess comprehensibility than relying solely on user studies.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance is in demonstrating a practical path towards adapting LLMs for beginner language learners.  Difficulty control is crucial for effective language tutoring, and the paper shows that modular techniques like FUDGE can achieve significant improvements without the need for retraining.  The release of code, models, and data will facilitate further research in AI-assisted language learning. The TMR metric offers a valuable tool for evaluating and comparing different difficulty control methods.  The potential impact is to make AI-driven language tutoring systems more accessible and effective for a wider range of learners.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly articulates the problem of LLM outputs being too complex for beginner language learners.
    * **Focus on Practicality:** The focus on modular techniques makes the approach more accessible, as it doesn't require access to model weights or extensive computational resources.
    * **Rigorous Evaluation:** The combination of automatic metrics (including the newly introduced TMR) and a user study provides a comprehensive evaluation.
    * **Open Science:** The release of code, models, and data is commendable and will facilitate future research.
    * **Well-Defined Metrics:**  TMR is carefully defined and validated.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited User Study Size:** While the user study is valuable, the small number of participants (6) limits the generalizability of the findings. A larger study would strengthen the conclusions.
    * **Specific Language:** The paper focuses on Japanese. While the findings are likely applicable to other languages, further research is needed to confirm this.
    * **Reliance on Existing Tools:** While using existing tools is practical, it means the results are constrained by the accuracy and effectiveness of those tools (e.g., the Tokohu-BERT model for ControlError).
* **Potential Influence:** This paper has a good chance of influencing research in AI-assisted language learning, particularly in the area of difficulty control. The TMR metric could become a standard tool for evaluation, and the findings related to FUDGE can inspire further development of modular, controllable generation techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper addresses an important problem, presents a novel and practical solution, and supports its claims with solid experimental evidence. The introduction of TMR and the focus on absolute beginners are significant contributions. The main weakness is the small size of the user study. While well-designed, the conclusions drawn from only 6 participants are less robust than they could be. Additionally, while the paper makes a case for modular approaches, future work could also explore how the benefits of FUDGE can be synergized with techniques such as fine-tuning.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Novel Data Augmentation Approach for Automatic Speaking Assessment on Opinion Expressions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04077v1)
- **Authors**: Chung-Chun Wang, Jhen-Ke Lin, Hao-Chien Lu, Hong-Yun Lin, Berlin Chen
- **Abstract**: Automated speaking assessment (ASA) on opinion expressions is often hampered by the scarcity of labeled recordings, which restricts prompt diversity and undermines scoring reliability. To address this challenge, we propose a novel training paradigm that leverages a large language models (LLM) to generate diverse responses of a given proficiency level, converts responses into synthesized speech via speaker-aware text-to-speech synthesis, and employs a dynamic importance loss to adaptively reweight training instances based on feature distribution differences between synthesized and real speech. Subsequently, a multimodal large language model integrates aligned textual features with speech signals to predict proficiency scores directly. Experiments conducted on the LTTC dataset show that our approach outperforms methods relying on real data or conventional augmentation, effectively mitigating low-resource constraints and enabling ASA on opinion expressions with cross-modal information.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the challenge of limited labeled data for automated speaking assessment (ASA) on opinion expressions. The authors propose a novel data augmentation approach that leverages a large language model (LLM) to generate diverse responses conditioned on proficiency levels. These responses are then converted into synthesized speech using a speaker-aware text-to-speech (TTS) system. To account for the differences between synthesized and real speech, a dynamic importance loss is introduced to adaptively reweight training instances based on feature distributions. Finally, a multimodal large language model (MLLM) integrates textual features with speech signals for direct proficiency score prediction. Experiments on the LTTC dataset demonstrate the effectiveness of this approach compared to methods relying solely on real data or conventional augmentation techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a well-structured and relevant solution to the data scarcity problem in automated speaking assessment (ASA), particularly for opinion expressions, which are inherently subjective and require diverse training data. The combination of several techniques demonstrates some novelty, but each component is based on existing work.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Integrated Approach:** The integration of LLM-based text generation, speaker-aware TTS, and dynamic importance loss creates a cohesive framework.
    *   **Addresses Data Scarcity:** The data augmentation strategy effectively expands the training dataset, mitigating the limitations of small labeled datasets.
    *   **Dynamic Importance Loss:** The adaptive reweighting of training instances based on feature distributions is a key contribution, as it addresses the inherent differences between synthesized and real speech.
    *   **Multimodal Fusion:** The use of an MLLM to combine textual and speech features allows for a more comprehensive understanding of the spoken response.
    *   **Experimental Results:** The paper demonstrates improved performance compared to baselines, providing empirical evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** While the overall approach is novel in its combination of techniques, each individual component (LLM for text generation, TTS, dynamic loss weighting) has been explored in prior work.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs and TTS:** The performance of the system heavily relies on the quality of the LLM-generated text and the TTS-synthesized speech. Errors or biases in these components could negatively impact the overall performance.
    *   **Limited Dataset:** While the LTTC dataset is relevant, it is relatively small and may not fully represent the diversity of opinion expressions in real-world scenarios. The dataset is primarily of Chinese students learning English, which may not generalize well to other learner populations.
    *   **Ablation Study:** The ablation study is not super extensive. It would be beneficial to break down how each different setting of temperature affects the results, or the weight on real instances.
    *   **Lack of Specificity on the MLLM:** The Phi-4 multimodal base model is not well explained in the paper.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The work has practical significance, offering a solution to the data scarcity problem that is common in many ASA applications.
    *   The dynamic importance loss is a valuable contribution that could be applied to other tasks involving synthesized data.
    *   The multimodal fusion approach has the potential to improve the accuracy and robustness of ASA systems.
    *   The paper provides a replicable paradigm for low-resource speaking assessment tasks.

**Justification:**

While the paper demonstrates a performance improvement and offers a valuable approach to addressing data scarcity in ASA, the novelty is somewhat limited by its reliance on existing techniques. The performance is also heavily influenced by an external model. The experimental results are encouraging, but the limitations of the dataset and the lack of robustness analyses hinder the generalizability of the findings. Therefore, a score of 6.5 reflects the paper's valuable contribution while acknowledging its incremental novelty and the need for further validation.

Score: 6.5

### LLMEval-Med: A Real-world Clinical Benchmark for Medical LLMs with Physician Validation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04078v1)
- **Authors**: Ming Zhang, Yujiong Shen, Zelin Li, Huayu Sha, Binze Hu, Yuhui Wang, Chenhao Huang, Shichun Liu, Jingqi Tong, Changhao Jiang, Mingxu Chai, Zhiheng Xi, Shihan Dou, Tao Gui, Qi Zhang, Xuanjing Huang
- **Abstract**: Evaluating large language models (LLMs) in medicine is crucial because medical applications require high accuracy with little room for error. Current medical benchmarks have three main types: medical exam-based, comprehensive medical, and specialized assessments. However, these benchmarks have limitations in question design (mostly multiple-choice), data sources (often not derived from real clinical scenarios), and evaluation methods (poor assessment of complex reasoning). To address these issues, we present LLMEval-Med, a new benchmark covering five core medical areas, including 2,996 questions created from real-world electronic health records and expert-designed clinical scenarios. We also design an automated evaluation pipeline, incorporating expert-developed checklists into our LLM-as-Judge framework. Furthermore, our methodology validates machine scoring through human-machine agreement analysis, dynamically refining checklists and prompts based on expert feedback to ensure reliability. We evaluate 13 LLMs across three categories (specialized medical models, open-source models, and closed-source models) on LLMEval-Med, providing valuable insights for the safe and effective deployment of LLMs in medical domains. The dataset is released in https://github.com/llmeval/LLMEval-Med.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "LLMEval-Med: A Real-world Clinical Benchmark for Medical LLMs with Physician Validation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LLMEval-Med, a new benchmark for evaluating large language models (LLMs) in medical applications. It addresses limitations in existing benchmarks by using real-world electronic health records and expert-designed clinical scenarios to generate 2,996 questions across five core medical areas. The benchmark also features an automated evaluation pipeline using an LLM-as-Judge framework with expert-developed checklists and physician validation to assess the factual correctness, reasoning, and ethical considerations in LLM responses. The paper evaluates 13 LLMs across different categories and provides insights for safe and effective LLM deployment in medicine. The dataset is publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Relevance and Timeliness:**  The paper directly addresses the pressing need for robust evaluation methods for medical LLMs, which is increasingly important as these models are deployed in healthcare.
*   **Real-world Data Source:** The use of real-world electronic health records (EHR) is a significant strength. This ensures the benchmark reflects the complexities and nuances of real clinical practice, unlike many existing benchmarks that rely on exam questions or simplified scenarios.
*   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The breadth of the five core medical areas and 27 secondary capability indicators ensures a granular assessment of different aspects of medical LLM performance.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation Methodology:**  The LLM-as-Judge framework, coupled with expert-developed checklists and human validation, represents a sophisticated and rigorous approach to evaluation. The human-machine agreement analysis is a valuable addition for ensuring the reliability of the automated scoring. The feedback loop to dynamically refine checklists based on expert opinions is excellent.
*   **Emphasis on Open-Ended Tasks:** The focus on open-ended generation tasks and complex reasoning is commendable, as it moves beyond the limitations of traditional multiple-choice formats that often dominate medical benchmarks.
*   **Public Availability:** Releasing the dataset publicly is crucial for enabling further research and development in the field.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Geographic Specificity:** The dataset primarily focuses on Chinese medical contexts, which potentially limits its generalizability to other healthcare systems and languages.  While the authors acknowledge this, it is a significant caveat.
*   **Lack of Multimodality:** The current benchmark primarily evaluates text-based capabilities. The absence of multimodal data (images, audio) restricts its ability to assess LLMs' performance in real-world clinical settings where such data is prevalent.
*   **Potential for Benchmark Contamination:**  Given the rapid development and training of LLMs, there's a risk that some models may have already been exposed to components of the used real-world datasets, skewing results. The paper does not sufficiently address this concern. The use of "real world" data is a plus *if* it can be determined that its presence was minimized in pretraining data.
*  **Limited Evaluation**: Only 677 samples were tested with all models, representing only a small fraction of the 2996 available questions. A more thorough evaluation would strengthen the claims.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies in the combination of a real-world data source, a comprehensive evaluation framework with LLM-as-Judge & human validation, and a focus on open-ended tasks. It makes a significant contribution by providing a more realistic and comprehensive assessment of medical LLMs compared to existing benchmarks. While the geographic specificity and lack of multimodality are limitations, the paper's rigorous methodology and open dataset will likely stimulate further research in this critical area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a strong and well-executed benchmark for evaluating medical LLMs. The use of real-world data and a rigorous evaluation framework are major strengths that address significant shortcomings in existing benchmarks. However, the geographical limitations, lack of multimodality, and limited experimental evaluation prevent a score in the very highest range.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### EuroLLM-9B: Technical Report
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04079v1)
- **Authors**: Pedro Henrique Martins, João Alves, Patrick Fernandes, Nuno M. Guerreiro, Ricardo Rei, Amin Farajian, Mateusz Klimaszewski, Duarte M. Alves, José Pombal, Manuel Faysse, Pierre Colombo, François Yvon, Barry Haddow, José G. C. de Souza, Alexandra Birch, André F. T. Martins
- **Abstract**: This report presents EuroLLM-9B, a large language model trained from scratch to support the needs of European citizens by covering all 24 official European Union languages and 11 additional languages. EuroLLM addresses the issue of European languages being underrepresented and underserved in existing open large language models. We provide a comprehensive overview of EuroLLM-9B's development, including tokenizer design, architectural specifications, data filtering, and training procedures. We describe the pre-training data collection and filtering pipeline, including the creation of EuroFilter, an AI-based multilingual filter, as well as the design of EuroBlocks-Synthetic, a novel synthetic dataset for post-training that enhances language coverage for European languages. Evaluation results demonstrate EuroLLM-9B's competitive performance on multilingual benchmarks and machine translation tasks, establishing it as the leading open European-made LLM of its size. To support open research and adoption, we release all major components of this work, including the base and instruction-tuned models, the EuroFilter classifier, and the synthetic post-training dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the EuroLLM-9B technical report:

**Summary:**

The EuroLLM-9B technical report introduces a 9 billion parameter large language model (LLM) trained from scratch with the express goal of better supporting European languages, specifically the 24 official EU languages, plus 11 additional languages.  The report details the model's development, including tokenizer design (using BPE with byte-fallback), architectural specifics (Megatron-LM codebase, GQA), data filtering pipeline (including a new multilingual filter called EuroFilter), and training procedures (a three-phase approach).  A novel synthetic dataset, EuroBlocks-Synthetic, is introduced for post-training to further enhance language coverage. Evaluation results demonstrate competitive performance on multilingual benchmarks and machine translation tasks, positioning it as a leading open European-made LLM in its size range. All components (base model, instruction-tuned model, EuroFilter, synthetic dataset) are released publicly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Focus on Under-Resourced Languages:**  The paper's core strength is its explicit aim to address the under-representation of European languages in existing LLMs.  This directly tackles a crucial issue of equity and accessibility in AI.
    *   **Comprehensive Technical Detail:** The report provides a clear and detailed account of the model's development, covering various aspects such as tokenizer, architecture, data preparation, and training. This level of detail is valuable for reproducibility and further research. The release of EuroFilter is also an important contribution.
    *   **Multilingual Data Filtering (EuroFilter):** A valuable contribution is the introduction of EuroFilter, an AI-based multilingual filter. This addresses the challenges of high-quality multilingual data curation, often a bottleneck in training effective multilingual models. The report's claim of EuroFilter's high quality should be evaluated carefully.
    *   **EuroBlocks-Synthetic Dataset:** The design of EuroBlocks-Synthetic is a creative approach to augment language coverage through synthetic data, offering a potentially efficient way to improve performance in less common languages. However, it is important to carefully evaluate the impact of synthetically generated data on the downstream tasks as it may introduce biases into the model.
    *   **Open Release:**  The public release of the model, EuroFilter, and EuroBlocks-Synthetic promotes open research and allows the community to build upon this work. This is commendable and contributes to democratization of AI.
    *   **Competitive Performance:** The report highlights the competitive performance of EuroLLM-9B on multilingual benchmarks compared to other open LLMs. This suggests that the model effectively leverages its training data and architecture. It is important to carefully evaluate EuroLLM's performance on specialized tasks with high accuracy demands.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Novelty of Architecture & Training:**  The report primarily utilizes existing technologies and techniques, such as BPE tokenization, GQA attention, and a trapezoid learning rate scheduler. The innovation lies in the application of these methods to the specific problem of European language support, and in the creation of Eurofilter and Euroblocks-Synthetic.
    *   **Evaluation Depth (beyond Benchmarks):** While benchmark results are presented, the report could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the model's capabilities and limitations. Further, it would be helpful to include more complex reasoning abilities in European languages.
    *   **Synthetic Data Risks:** Using synthetic data (EuroBlocks-Synthetic) carries a potential risk of introducing biases and artifacts in the model's output. More discussion on measures taken to mitigate these risks would strengthen the report. Furthermore, details on what version of TOWER was used to translate would be helpful.
    *   **Limited Comparative Analysis:** It would be useful to see more comparisons of performance by comparing across different LLMs and different aspects of the overall model that have been previously studied in papers.
    *   **Limited Analysis:** The report lacks a clear analysis in certain cases that would have made a larger impact for the reader.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The EuroLLM project is highly significant because it contributes to addressing the growing demand for LLMs that can understand and generate text in a broader range of languages.
    *   The open release of the resources will help grow research and development of natural language technologies for European languages.

**Justification:**

EuroLLM-9B makes a valuable, though incremental, contribution to the field. While it doesn't introduce groundbreaking architectural innovations, it effectively applies existing techniques to a practical and important problem – increasing the availability of capable LLMs for European languages. The detailed report and the open release of key components considerably enhance its value to the community. Its strengths lie in its focus on under-resourced languages, and careful data curation. The fact that it doesn't outperform LLMs in all areas or benchmark categories is expected.
